#3103. Google Fi now switches between the best mobile networks automatically – how it works

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Home & Office > Networking > Mobile Carriers

Pixel users also get a bonus Wi-Fi upgrade. Here's what to know.

From left to right: Samsung Galaxy Z Flip 8, Google Pixel 11, Google Pixel 11 Pro XL.

Google Fi wireless customers with Pixel phones just got a serious upgrade to their mobile and Wi-Fi connections for free.

Google has announced two new “super network” connection features for Fi Wireless: one that automatically switches between two of the largest 5G cellular networks, and another that automatically connects to premium Wi-Fi in millions of locations without you needing to log in.

The features are rolling out today for Pixel 9 phones and newer models on every Google Fi plan. If you’re not a current Fi customer, you can sign up for a free seven-day trial to test it alongside your current carrier.

Here’s how the new mobile connection feature works: if your main connection slows or becomes unstable, Fi will automatically move to a secondary connection. This not only means you’ll have the fastest option when your connection is good, but you’ll have fewer dead zones when service is limited.

Also: I compared Verizon, T-Mobile, and AT&T 5G coverage on a road trip – and the winner surprised me

The feature will run on MEP (Multiple Enabled Profiles) technology, which allows a single eSIM chip to host and run multiple SIM profiles simultaneously. Footnotes in Google’s release note that you’ll need to have “any non-Fi SIMs disabled.”

Google Fi originally offered automatic switching between two networks when the service debuted a decade ago, but it was between Sprint and T-Mobile, which merged in 2020. Google didn’t reveal what the “two of America’s best 5G cellular networks” were, but ZDNET has reached out to ask. The service usually runs on T-Mobile. Along with T-Mobile, the three biggest carriers are AT&T and Verizon, so it’s likely the second carrier is one of those.

In addition to a mobile connection upgrade, users are getting better Wi-Fi, too. Wi-Fi Auto Connect+ will automatically connect you to faster Wi-Fi in millions of locations like airports and malls (Google defines “faster” as two times better reliability and 30% improved latency compared to normal Google Fi cellular networks) without you having to log in or head to your settings. These connections will be automatically encrypted, Google said, keeping your online activity safe.

Also: 6 default Pixel 11 settings I changed that made a big difference – and why

This feature is also coming to Samsung phones running One UI 9 or higher and select modern Motorola phones.

Both of these features, Google explained, are available internationally and are included on Unlimited Premium and Flexible plans at no extra cost.

#3102. Top Wall Street analysts like these 3 dividend stocks for steady income

The stock market continues to grapple with volatility amid geopolitical tensions in the Middle East and concerns about the sustainability of the AI boom. Given this uncertainty, investors looking for steady income can add dividend-paying stocks to their portfolios.

Tracking the recommendations of top Wall Street analysts can help investors pick the right dividend stocks, as these experts assign their ratings after an in-depth analysis of a company's financials and ability to pay dividends consistently.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Downstream energy company Phillips 66 (PSX) is this week's first dividend pick. With a quarterly dividend $1.27 per share (annualized dividend of $5.08), PSX offers a yield of 2.25%. The company recently reported solid Q2 earnings, as the Middle East conflict affected global supplies and drove up refining margins.

Following the Q2 print, TD Cowen analyst Jason Gabelman reiterated a buy rating on Phillips 66 and raised his price target to $255 from $240, reflecting higher 2026 earnings expectations and lower interest expense next year.

Gabelman highlighted the quarter-over-quarter reduction in Phillips 66's net debt, noting management's optimism at achieving its estimated $15.5 billion net debt target one year ahead of schedule. The 5-star analyst expects the company to end 2026 with a net debt of $14.6 billion.

"The [balance sheet] improvement could re-establish PSX as a go-to defensive refiner," said Gabelman.

Management agreed that PSX's payout ratio lagged year-to-date, but expects buybacks to increase in the second half. Gabelman said Phillps indicated the possibility of a larger dividend hike following an annual increase of 5% over the past two years.

Gabelman ranks No. 554 among more than 12,400 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 66% of the time, delivering an average return of 14.9%. See Phillips 66 Statistics on TipRanks.

Crescent Energy (CRGY) is an exploration and production company with activities focused in the Eagle Ford, Permian and Uinta Basins. Earlier this month, the company reported better-than-expected Q2 earnings and announced a quarterly dividend of $0.12 per share, payable on August 31. At an annualized dividend of $0.48 per share, CRGY boasts a dividend yield of about 4%.

In reaction to Q2 results, Evercore analyst Stephen Richardson reaffirmed a buy rating on Crescent Energy stock with a price target of $18. He noted continued strength in the company's performance, with second-quarter oil production and cash flow surpassing the Street's expectations.

"CRGY's cash flow exceeded expectations by 10%, reinforcing its trajectory of capital efficiency," said Richardson.

The 5-star analyst highlighted that Crescent raised its full-year oil production guidance, citing the effective integration of the Vital Energy acquisition. Specifically, Crescent tripled its synergy target from the Vital Energy deal to as much as $300 million, significantly reducing the effective purchase price. This reflects Crescent's solid execution following the acquisition, Richardson said.

Additionally, the analyst noted that Crescent's capital spending is and heading toward the lower end of management's prior guidance, indicating disciplined financial controls.

Richardson ranks No. 579 among more than 12,400 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 65% of the time, delivering an average return of 12.5%. See Crescent Energy Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Finally, Viper Energy (VNOM) is effectively controlled by Diamondback Energy. Viper owns and acquires mineral and royalty interests in oil-weighted basins, mainly the Permian in West Texas. Viper recently announced its Q2 2026 results and declared a 32% hike in its base dividend, effective in the third quarter of 2026. The new base dividend indicates a 4.5% annualized yield.

Viper also removed its quarterly commitment to return at least 75% of cash available for distribution. The company believes that this move will ensure increased flexibility to make opportunistic share repurchases and pursue accretive mergers and acquisitions.

Following the Q2 results, TD Cowen analyst Aaron Bilkoski reiterated a buy rating on Viper, slightly raising his price target to $59 from $58. Bilkoski said Viper's solid Q2 performance supports his view that the strength in oil prices and rising operator activity are driving higher production.

"Viper has delivered, and we forecast will continue to deliver, one of the highest production per share growth profiles in our royalty universe" through the end of 2027, said Bilkoski. He believes Viper deserves a premium valuation, given its above-average growth.

The 5-star analyst said that while the biggest surprise in the quarter was Viper's revision to its shareholder return framework, he doesn't believe that the move indicates a significant change to its underlying strategy. Under the new framework, a larger portion of excess free cash flow is expected to be allocated to share repurchases than to variable dividends, Bilkoski said.

Bilkoski ranks No. 719 among more than 12,400 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 57% of the time, delivering an average return of 12%. See Viper Energy Financials on TipRanks.

#3101. Why This Anker Universal Charger Is the Only Power Accessory I Pack for Travel

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

The slew of port and plug options is great, and the built-in display is just as useful.

I never realized the importance of having a universal charger until I traveled to Berlin a few months ago. It was a last-minute work trip, so I packed lightly, stashed my go-to portable charger — with its flip-out plug — and flew out not knowing what struggles lay ahead.

I had it all mapped out. I’d top up my phone, laptop, earbuds and cameras at the hotel each night, go out for a döner and be ready to tackle all my work the next day. But my lack of an international plug adapter — specifically, US to EU — meant that I’d have to deal with a series of physical, technical and safety obstacles first.

Had I discovered the Anker Universal Charger sooner, I wouldn’t have gone through the ordeal of finding an electronics store open late on a Sunday. That’s because the latest product from the mobile accessory brand takes a kitchen sink approach to charging — whether you need its more excessive features or not.

I’ve been testing the $80 Anker Universal Charger, both for general use in the States and abroad, and it’s more practical than I thought. Here’s why it’ll stay in my bag going into the holiday travel season.

The Anker Universal Charger is two things: an international plug adapter and a multiport charger. It has slide-out Type A, Type C and Type G prongs that lock each other whenever one is active. That covers most countries in the world, including the US, Canada, Japan, China, Europe and the UK. Notably, the charger doesn’t have a Type I prong (commonly used in Australia and New Zealand), so it’s not completely universal.

The charger can output up to 100W, either individually through one of the two USB-C ports (labeled USB-C1 and USB-C2) or shared across the third USB-C port, which caps at 15W, and the one USB-A port, which caps at 10W. So if you want the most optimal charging, stick with only one of the main USB-C ports.

In practice, the Anker is powerful enough to charge a modern-day iPhone or Android, smartwatch or laptop. My 14-inch MacBook Pro, for example, supports 100-watt charging, so the Anker is just enough to fast-charge it. It took 33 minutes to charge my MacBook from 0% to 50%, and about 50 more minutes to reach 100% as trickle charging kicked in.

The Anker wouldn’t be able to hit the 140-watt rating of a 16-inch MacBook Pro or a similarly specced laptop, though. I also noticed the charger would throttle its output — as shown on its smart display — after prolonged use at 100 watts. That meant when I charged devices back-to-back, the second device charged much more slowly because of the reduced output.

Generally speaking, make sure you’re using a proper, high-quality charging cable with the adapter, as Anker doesn’t include one in the box.

One of the key features of the new Anker Universal Charger is the 1.3-inch smart display on the front. It’s there to show live line voltage, internal thermal readings and real-time wattage draw per port, so you know exactly how effectively it’s charging your device(s).

You navigate the display with short and long taps on the capacitive button below it, which I’ve found less intuitive than just swiping on the display, but at least it reduces fingerprint smudges.

That said, I’ve still found the smart display quite useful, as I don’t have to second-guess whether the cable I’m using or the outlet I’m plugging into can charge my phone or laptop efficiently. There’s nothing worse than plugging a device in, only to come back to it barely charged because the wattage wasn’t as high as you expected.

I’m particularly fond of how the display indicates when an outlet is safe to charge, as it can detect the output (so long as it’s within 100-275V) and signal when there’s excess electrical pressure in the circuit.

This is important when you’re traveling abroad and/or plugging into a high-voltage outlet; if you’re using a charger that’s not rated for the higher capacity, you risk sparking and other dangerous thermal reactions.

The Anker Universal Charger is relatively compact for how much power it delivers. In the past, I’ve lugged around larger charging bricks — such as Apple’s proprietary one for MacBooks — that had fewer ports.

For reference, the Anker is about the size of a small computer mouse; it’s tiny enough to fit in a backpack pocket, with just enough weight (262 grams) to keep it from shifting when plugged into an outlet. Yet, I wouldn’t say this is a lightweight charger by any means, especially when compared to a conventional plug adapter.

The Anker Universal Charger is fairly priced at $80, though it’s still more expensive than generic international adapters that sell for around $20 to $50. Those don’t have a cool smart display, though.

While the price keeps the Anker out of “impulse purchase” territory, I’ve found it easier to justify than most other high-end chargers, given its functionality and safety features.

#3100. This tiny box gives your older tech smart home superpowers – and it’s only $69

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Home Assistant gets new equipment to battle planned obsolescence and abandoned cloud services.

Home Assistant is perhaps the most versatile smart home automation system of them all, thanks to the extreme customization power it offers. Today, a tiny, new box-shaped device is giving Home Assistant users a way to make their existing electronics smarter, rather than replacing them to keep up with the times.

Also: Ring’s new drone patrols your home to keep an eye on things

The Home Assistant Connect AUX-2 is a multi-purpose piece of hardware made by Nabu Casa, the company behind official Home Assistant hardware. The device brings together several connectivity options, including RS-232 serial for older TVs and other equipment, and the ability to connect older stereo systems and amplifiers to modern multi-room audio setups.

“The most sustainable device is the one you already own,” said Paulus Schoutsen, founder of Home Assistant and Nabu Casa. “No device should be left behind because it doesn’t have smart capabilities, or worst of all, its cloud services get turned off.

With the AUX-2, Home Assistant is joining the fight against planned obsolescence and abandoned cloud services.

Modern smart home systems have a major drawback: they exclude working electronics simply because they weren’t designed to integrate with today’s smart home platforms. This is the problem that the AUX-2 addresses.

Instead of buying a new stereo system with smart features, consumers can use the $69 AUX-2 to make the devices they already own smarter, more connected, and easier to control.

A single AUX-2 can control multiple devices, depending on how they’re connected and the interfaces they use. The device’s limit has more to do with the number of physical connections required.

Also: How I open-sourced my smart home – and ditched Google for Siri

The AUX-2 lets you connect two serial-controlled devices directly through the RS-232 ports. The USB port can act as an expansion for compatible adapters. You can place one AUX-2 on an entertainment center and connect an older stereo and TV, then use another AUX-2 elsewhere in your house for additional equipment with physical serial connections.

According to Home Assistant, with an AUX-2, you can control an old AV receiver without the original app, have an older TV respond to commands without relying on its manufacturer’s cloud servers, or integrate existing audio equipment into modern multiroom sound systems.

This way, consumers won’t need to trash a functional piece of equipment simply because the manufacturer stops supporting it.

Aside from RS-232 serial, the AUX-2 also supports Bluetooth, Z-Wave, USB, PoE, and Wi-Fi for connectivity.

#3099. Should You Buy Dogecoin While It's Below $0.10?

The world's original meme-cryptocurrency is fighting an uphill battle to deliver positive returns.

Dogecoin (DOGE -4.01%) has spent most of 2026 trading below $0.10. It's hovering at around $0.07 as I write this on Nov. 10, representing a decline of more than 90% from its 2021 record high of $0.73. However, the cryptocurrency's significant decline doesn't necessarily mean it's cheap.

Dogecoin was created as a joke by two friends in 2013, so they didn't put much thought into giving it an actual purpose. Practically every rally since then has been fueled by pure speculation, which simply isn't sustainable, hence the coin's steep decline from its all-time high.

But developers are working on new innovations like DogeOS, an application layer where people can build decentralized software. This could revive the ecosystem and create organic demand for Dogecoin, so should investors buy it while it's still under $0.10?

An asset can't rise in value over the long term without a sustainable source of demand. If it isn't attractive or useful to consumers, businesses, or investors, then the asset will eventually run out of buyers, and its value will collapse. That is exactly what happened to Dogecoin after 2021.

Although Dogecoin can technically be used to make payments, only 2,400 businesses around the world are willing to accept it in exchange for goods and services (according to Cryptwerk). Its extreme volatility would make cash-flow management very difficult, so it's no surprise, but if consumers can't spend Dogecoin at their preferred stores, they have no reason to own it.

However, the team of developers behind the MyDoge self-custodial digital wallet created DogeOS to give Dogecoin some actual utility. It's still in the testing phase, but it will allow people to create a wide range of decentralized applications, from games to crypto exchanges to lending marketplaces, and Dogecoin will play a central role in their operation.

DogeOS is compatible with the Ethereum (ETH -2.07%) virtual machine, the software engine that powers Ethereum's smart contracts. It gives DogeOS the same app-building functionality as Ethereum, which is the world's most popular platform for developing decentralized software.

But when people use an app that was created on the DogeOS layer, they will have to use Dogecoin to pay fees instead of Ethereum's native cryptocurrency, Ether. These fees are commonly called "gas," and they cover the computing costs required to process actions in the given app. As a result, DogeOS could create a new source of organic demand for Dogecoin.

As promising as DogeOS sounds, it won't solve Dogecoin's biggest problem. New coins have always been issued through a process called mining, which involves people using computers to solve complex mathematical problems for the right to validate transactions on the blockchain. It's a critical process for maintaining a secure system of record, so validators are rewarded with payment in Dogecoin to encourage further participation.

The world's largest cryptocurrency, Bitcoin, also relies on mining, except with one key difference. Bitcoin's supply is capped at 21 million coins, so once all of them have been mined, no new coins will enter circulation.

Dogecoin has no such cap. Although a maximum of 5 billion coins can be mined each year, there is no end date, so its circulating supply will grow forever. In theory, this will steadily erode the value of each existing coin over time.

There are two main reasons I think Dogecoin will continue trending lower, despite innovations like DogeOS. First, there isn't much reason for people to use the new application layer, because they can just build decentralized software on the proven and trusted legacy Ethereum network instead.

Second, any potential value created by those who do adopt DogeOS might be erased by Dogecoin's growing supply. Personally, I've never come across an asset with an unlimited supply that increased in value over the long term, and I don't think this particular cryptocurrency will buck that trend.

There are 156.7 billion Dogecoins in circulation as I write this. If we assume 5 billion more enter circulation every year, then supply will double over the next 31 years or so, which could slash the current price of $0.07 per coin in half.

In summary, a beaten-down cryptocurrency isn't necessarily a cheap cryptocurrency, and I think investors who buy Dogecoin today are likely to suffer losses.

#3098. Is Your Console Ready for Grand Theft Auto 6? Here’s What You Can Do to Make Room

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

Deleting old games isn’t the only option you have to make space for GTA 6.

Grand Theft Auto 6 hits shelves on Nov. 19, 2026, and if you preordered the game, you can preload it on your PlayStation 5 or Xbox Series X/S now. Preloading ensures you can quickly boot the game up when it’s released.

Rockstar Games revealed that GTA 6 is 212 GB, which will take up about half of my Xbox Series S’s storage space. The company announced that GTA 6 is available to stream via Xbox Cloud Gaming without downloading it to your console. However, cloud streaming GTA 6 might affect the game’s performance and relies heavily on a strong internet connection. To avoid those issues, I plan on downloading the game.

If you’re like me and are excited to play GTA 6 but don’t have much storage space, you have two options: delete games or upgrade your console’s hard drive. Here’s what you should know about both options.

The simplest and cheapest way to free up space on your console for Grand Theft Auto 6 is to delete files and other games, which is what I’ll be doing. Uninstalling games is a good choice for folks who plan on spending a lot of time with GTA 6 and don’t intend to juggle different games. Rob Nelson, who oversees Rockstar North with Aaron Garbut, told The New York Times that he had an 80-hour playthrough of the game in February, so you can easily stay immersed in Grand Theft Auto’s state of Leonida.

You may not need to delete much – uninstalling a game like Call of Duty alone could free up nearly 315 GB, giving you enough digital storage for GTA 6. Since deleting files is almost instantaneous, you can quickly reclaim hard drive space. Because you don’t need to buy additional storage, you’ll also save a lot of money – storage prices are through the roof at the moment.

Ultimately, deleting games is a great option if you don’t want to spend any additional money or you don’t mind redownloading games later.

If you plan on hopping between GTA 6 and other games, or you don’t want to delete many of your downloaded titles, you can upgrade your console’s storage. Buying storage is more expensive than deleting files from your console, but benefit from being able to seamlessly hop between Grand Theft Auto 6 and Call of Duty or Madden.

You can use internal or external storage for your console, but you might have to move files between your internal and external storage to launch games. An internal hard drive lets you seamlessly play games. Xbox consoles rely on proprietary expansion cards from Seagate or SanDisk that plug into your console for additional storage. The PlayStation 5 uses M.2 solid-state drives, which are installed alongside the PS5’s built-in storage, and CNET’s David Carnoy recommends the Lexar NM 790 as a fast SSD with a straightforward installation process.

There shouldn’t be any performance, quality or load-time differences between running games from installed storage expansion devices versus your console’s internal storage. Both expansion cards and SSDs can be expensive, especially when compared with deleting games.

You could also use an external hard drive to expand your storage, which can be cheaper than buying expansion cards and internal SSD upgrades – you may even have a compatible USB external drive sitting around that you can use. However, there are caveats around what games can and can’t be played directly from external HDDs.

For Xbox consoles, some older Xbox, Xbox 360 and Xbox One titles can be stored and played from external HDDs. But games optimized for Series X/S consoles can only be stored on HDDs and must be moved back to your internal drive to play. Similarly, the PlayStation 5 only lets you store PS5 games on external HDDs, while you can store and run PS4 games from external drives.

Therefore, using an external HDD requires playing digital musical chairs, moving current-gen games back and forth between your external hard drive and internal console storage in order to play them. The LaCie Rugged SSD4 earned a CNET Editors’ Choice Award and could be a good option. If its price tag scares you, the Western Digital P10 offers more storage at a more economical price point.

Rockstar announced that GTA 6 will be available to stream on the Xbox consoles – no download required – via Xbox Cloud Gaming, with some quality and time limitations. You’ll need to purchase either an Xbox Game Pass plan or Cloud Gaming hours. Folks who don’t want to delete games or upgrade their console storage can stream GTA 6, but you’ll need an Xbox One or Series X/S console, won’t get the highest visual quality and will have to pay for more playtime hours if they run out. Streaming GTA 6 could help budget-conscious gamers who don’t want to upgrade from Xbox One consoles or who don’t have the allotted storage space on their devices.

Normally, you can stream many games on PCs, mobile devices and TVs or streaming devices using Xbox Cloud Gaming or Game Pass, but Grand Theft Auto 6 is limited to the Xbox One, Series S and Series X. Additionally, GTA 6 streaming quality is capped at Series S graphical settings. So if you have a Series X, consider deleting games or upgrading your storage to run the game locally with better graphics.

While you’ll need to install the full game on your console first, remote play for Xbox and PlayStation consoles lets you stream GTA 6 to other devices, like your phone, tablet, streaming device or TV.

If you’re willing to pay a little more for the convenience of having multiple games ready to play at a moment’s notice, Xbox expansion cards or an internal SSD for a PS5 are your best options. If you want a cheaper option and don’t mind moving game files around, an external hard drive could be for you. If you have Xbox Game Pass or Xbox Cloud Gaming, you could stream the game to your console without needing more storage space. However, the cheapest option is to delete games from your internal storage and redownload them later, which can be time-consuming.

You only have a few days left until Rockstar releases GTA 6, so you’d better prepare your digital storage now, or you might not be immediately able to play. I plan on deleting old games to make room on my console. You won’t catch me slipping on Nov. 19.

#3097. Best Hotels in Chase’s The Edit in 2026

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

So how do we make money? Our partners compensate us for advertisements that appear on our site or when you click to or take an action on their website. This compensation helps us provide tools and services - like free credit score access and monitoring. With the exception of mortgage, home equity and other home-lending products or services, partner compensation is one of several factors that may affect which products we highlight and where they appear on our site. Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

Chase's hotel and resort program, The Edit, gives some cardholders the chance to book stays at spectacular hotels.

Many premium credit cards have their own travel portals where you can earn and redeem your points. They also have a curated list of hotels with which they partner to offer elevated benefits to their cardholders who book directly through their program.

The Edit is Chase’s program, and similar to the Fine Hotels & Resorts program from American Express, travelers need to book a prepaid reservation directly through a Chase Travel channel and pay with their Chase card to receive the benefits.

What benefits do you receive when you book through the program? Well, they can be substantial.

At nearly 1,400 hotels and resorts, The Edit customers will enjoy:

If the hotel is an all-inclusive property, guests would instead receive a $200 property credit to use for experiences like spa treatments. While stays at hotels in The Edit Boutique Collection do include the other perks, those stays are not eligible for a room upgrade or early check-in/late checkout considerations.

The tradeoff of using The Edit program is that it does not necessarily always have the lowest rate. Sometimes you can find a lower rate directly with the hotel or another website. But, when you consider the list of benefits above, any differential in price can more than outweigh the extra cost.

While it belongs to Chase, The Edit is not open to all Chase cardholders. It is a dedicated perk for primary cardholders and authorized users of the Chase Sapphire Reserve® and J.P. Morgan Reserve cards, as well as primary Sapphire Reserve for Business℠ cardholders.

Even better is that these eligible Chase cards come with a total of $500 in credits that can be used at The Edit hotels each year. They are divided into two $250 credits that each must be used for at least a two-night stay. And when paying for the rest of your stay through The Edit, cardholders earn 8x Chase Ultimate Rewards® points per dollar spent. In addition, hotel stays booked through The Edit and labeled as “Loyalty Plus” are eligible to also earn points with any associated hotel loyalty program.

These are some of our favorite hotels in the program where you can take advantage of your annual $500 credits as well as earn significant Chase points for a prepaid stay.

It also has a full-service spa, kids club and access to Al Maaden Golf course. Its Moroccan restaurant is worth a visit and offers cuisine from all of Morocco and the North African region (with many ingredients coming from the chef’s onsite garden). Guests can add a stash of World of Hyatt points to their account after a stay here, but be sure to look to see if it is “eligible” in the search results (as shown below).

Guests can arrive at this luxury resort by its own fleet of two sea planes that serve the three properties in the Four Seasons portfolio. (Two of the properties are in The Edit, the third is a private island: Four Seasons Maldives Kuda Huraa, Four Seasons Resort Maldives at Landaa Giraavaru and Four Seasons Maldives Private Island at Voavah, respectively.)

Overwater villas with their own pools and hammocks dangling over the sea, beach bungalows with private access pools and excellent dining (from Asian to Lebanese) are hallmarks of the resort. Its newest element is the AyurMa wellness program at the spa with onsite practitioners who guide guests on personalized, multiday journeys covering everything from nutrition to physical therapy.

This resort is designed around 64 pool suites and villas with access to a beautiful beachfront, swimming pools and a jungle spa. Its island location lies within the Gulf of Thailand near Koh Samui, and its beach is well-known for its famous Full Moon parties. Other activities, besides relaxing, include Muay Thai boxing, scuba diving, snorkeling, kayaking, stand-up paddle boarding and yoga sessions.

When you enter the lobby, the first thing you notice is the grand Waterford crystal chandelier, one of the largest in the Southern Hemisphere.

This is also one of the city’s biggest hotels, and it is highly regarded as a top hotel in town. Be sure to request one of the rooms with a balcony or terrace to take in the views.

Known by many as Dublin’s grande dame, this Autograph Collection member is fresh off the heels of a top-to-bottom renovation that renewed its interiors, but didn’t change its historic spirit. While renovation work will linger into 2027, the refreshed rooms are worth experiencing.

#3096. Where Will IonQ Stock Be in 5 Years?

IonQ is beating the odds, but a lot can happen between now and 2031.

The quantum computing industry is in its infancy. Although some analysts believe the industry won't hit its stride for several more years, many small companies have emerged to capitalize on this technology.

Among these emerging companies, I believe IonQ (IONQ -5.78%) has better odds than most of its start-up quantum computing peers and could easily beat the market over the next five years. However, with the high valuation and considerable uncertainty, earning those gains may be a rough ride for investors.

Given its inability to address profitability and the uncertainty of how the megacaps will compete in the quantum industry, the first question is why IonQ will even survive the next five years.

The likely answer to this question is the fact that IonQ's successes appear to stand in contrast to the more dire predictions about the industry. For example, Ark Invest's Cathie Wood predicts the industry will not develop "meaningful applications" for the technology until the mid-to-late 2040s, which bodes poorly for the company on the surface.

Nonetheless, companies such as Microsoft and AstraZeneca have applied IonQ's technology to help solve problems. Thus, even if quantum computing stocks generate less investor interest than other tech names, IonQ seems to already be attracting commercial interest.

Investors should also consider its competitive advantages. IonQ stands out from its start-up peers, and even from larger companies in quantum computing such as IBM and Google's parent Alphabet, through trapped-ion architecture.

Trapped-ion quantum bits (qubits) hold their quantum states longer. This reduces error rates, a critical attribute since error rates have long plagued the development of quantum systems.

Moreover, IonQ has formed key partnerships, natively integrating quantum-as-a-service (QaaS) with Google Cloud, Amazon's AWS, and Microsoft's Azure.

However, the most critical partnership may be with Nvidia. IonQ's Superion 256 links to Nvidia's supercomputing rack. As it orchestrates workloads with Nvidia's CUDA-Q software, IonQ's quantum processing unit (QPU) and Nvidia's graphics processing units (GPUs) can work seamlessly, giving it an edge over its peers. Additionally, the fact that IonQ appears to follow Nvidia's playbook in leading its industry could be an added bonus for investors.

Unfortunately, it is unclear how these breakthroughs drive the stock now and in the future.

The $319 million in revenue it earned in the first nine months of 2026 was far above the $68 million reported in the same period a year ago. Investors should also note that it earned $174 million of that revenue in the third quarter.

However, operating expenses remain high, and the massive increase did not stop the company from losing $501 million in the first three quarters of the year. Also, because it holds only about $1 billion in liquidity, IonQ will likely need to issue more debt or equity in the foreseeable future, which could pressure the stock.

Furthermore, even though its valuation is at multiyear lows, its price-to-sales (P/S) ratio of 32 implies significant overvaluation. Still, investors should also consider the analysts forecast 293% revenue growth for this year before it slows to 68% in 2027. That growth places downward pressure on its valuation, so much so that its forward P/S ratio is 26 and the forward one-year sales multiple (the 2028 P/S ratio) is just 15.

Growth rates beyond 2028 remain unknown. Still, if IonQ maintained rapid revenue growth until 2031, that would take the sales multiple into the low single digits at current prices. That fact also applies even if the growth rate fell by 10 percentage points in each of the next three years. That situation alone would probably be enough to push the stock higher, assuming the company stays out of financial trouble.

IonQ appears on track to trade at a significantly higher level in 2031.

Admittedly, a lot can happen in five years, and since it likely does not hold enough liquidity to keep its doors open for that long, investors should expect it to issue shares or take on additional debt during that time. If investors view that negatively, it could hurt the stock.

However, its 32 P/S ratio is low enough that revenue growth in the mid double digits and higher is going to place significant pressure on the valuation, and a sales multiple in the low single digits is possible if the stock's price stayed the same. That means as long as it can maintain investor confidence, it should push IonQ stock higher.

Ultimately, this stock is likely too volatile and uncertain for risk-averse investors. Nonetheless, if one has some tolerance for risk and stock price fluctuations, owning IonQ stock over the next five years could ultimately bring market-beating returns.

#3095. You Can Get Google’s New Smart Glasses Next Month. I’ve Tried Them Out

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

Warby Parker and Gentle Monster's Google and Samsung-created smart glasses have cameras like Meta's, but these have some subtle differences when it comes to AI.

Google’s return to smart glasses has been promised for nearly two years, but the first waves of actual prescription-ready eyewear are only now rolling in nearly at year’s end, an unusual time for a product launch. Really, it feels more like a kickoff for efforts that will continue next year, as Google also works on AI-enabled glasses that have displays built in.

I’d worn Google and Samsung’s smart glasses in multiple demos before, but this month for the first time I got to try on and test the final versions designed by Warby Parker and Gentle Monster. I also visited two immersive showrooms the companies are opening in New York City to demo how these glasses work. These showrooms will be open by appointment.

The showrooms were like playgrounds, full of installation art to try the glasses in. Warby’s space was a series of library-themed rooms, with travel themes and whimsical sculptures. Gentle Monster’s was a stark, surreal series of rooms, blending industrial design and realistic (sometimes robotic) horses, people, chickens, floating heads, and … a big blue gentle monster.

The glasses I wore give me a strong sense of deja vu after years of reviewing Meta smart glasses and lots of others, but there are also signs of how their deeper-connected Gemini smarts could be a big difference-maker — and likely a sign of how Apple might approach its own glasses expected in 2027.

The various models of smart glasses across Warby Parker and Gentle Monster designs, which start at $395 and $449 respectively, all work the same: The frames do not have displays, just standard glasses lenses that support a range of prescriptions and lens types that match what Warby and Gentle Monster already offer. They have speakers on the arms that project audio, along with four beamforming microphones that filter out background noise and focus on the direction you’re facing. They have a single camera on the left side, and an LED light indicator on the right.

I got to try on all the frame designs and then wore frames with my prescription lenses popped in so that I could actually use the glasses around the exhibits. The glasses feel reminiscent of what Meta’s made for years, with similar-looking frame size and arms compared to recent Ray-Ban Gen 3 models. Most of the frames weigh around 40 grams, which felt pretty easy on my face, but I wear chunky glasses to begin with.

And the frame designs, while mostly clean and somewhat conservative, all looked really good. Warby Parker’s collection of designs came in several understated color shades (I liked the dark green Pine the best), and familiar round and square frames.

All of the Intelligent Eyewear models across both brands have cameras, a feature that Google’s head of XR, Shahram Izadi, still sees as key to having a deep assistive experience, although he’s also open to the idea of future versions that could be camera-free like Meta’s recent Ray-Ban Audio model.

These glasses use the same type of indicator system as Meta’s glasses do, flashing an LED that also auto-adjusts brightness outdoors and playing a sound when taking photos and videos.

I was particularly impressed by how Gemini can just listen to any snippet of language and translate it to what language you need, a trick that makes these glasses feel like a Swiss Army knife for travel. Several demos showed this off, including the surreal room of robotic heads that discussed Gentle Monster’s design philosophy in a handful of languages. I heard them all, sometimes with a short delay or hiccup, over 10 minutes or so.

The glasses can recognize written languages, too, in a much larger range than I’m used to: over 70. Live translation kicks in by pressing and holding two fingers on the glasses temple, and the translated voices change to try to match different speakers in a room as well as varied intonation.

Pressing and holding the side temple turns on Gemini, and once it’s on, it’s on until a significant pause of about 15 seconds or so. That meant that I could keep chatting and asking questions without asking “Hey Google” (or “Hey Gemini,” a new trigger phrase). I could end a session by swiping backward on the arm.

Any of the chats I have can also be turned into a note or summary instantly, hooking into Google Keep. The glasses connect with Google Maps, and Izadi promises that most of Gemini’s key features on phones will carry over to the glasses. On iPhones, the glasses will be able to triangulate across Google apps via the cloud to access Gmail, calendars and more.

I found a few clever demos that showed me some promise. In a pantry installation full of world foods in Gentle Monster’s showroom, I asked Gemini to compare brands of international mayo in front of me. I could point to things and casually ask, “What is this?” And Gemini knew what I was referring to. In another demo, Gemini knew the sheet music I was holding up and played back the matching song. In another demo, I sat down and gave myself a tarot card reading — Gemini could read the cards, regardless of whether the cards were right-side up or reversed.

Other times, Gemini said text I asked it to read was too far away, even though I could read it on a wall. Sometimes it seemed to hallucinate an odd answer to a question. (It responded with “8 minutes” to a question on a wall about whether it was OK to wait 45 minutes for a restaurant table.)

Tapping the temple arm starts and stops music, swiping changes tracks and two-finger swipes change volume (which was a bit confusing). Meanwhile, a top button on the arm takes photos and videos just like Meta glasses do, but in some demo sessions, it seemed like photos got beamed over to a Pixel phone much faster than they do to Meta’s app.

The music playback sounded good based on some quick tries in a crowded room. The audio seemed particularly loud, maybe even a bit more so than Meta’s glasses, but I’ll know more on that when I review.

The real test of Google’s Intelligent Eyewear is in how truly well connected those AI services feel and on the battery life. Meta’s up-to-eight-hour battery life on the Scriber Optics glasses I’ve been wearing the last few months tend to drain battery fast in heavy use. Will Google manage battery better, or similarly?

It’s the AI features and Google app hook-ins that could give Google a leg up. My several hours wearing pairs and wandering through two different immersive public installation spaces only showed me hints, and really, I’m most curious about how the glasses can tap into my entire Gemini history, my Gmail, my Google Docs, or my Google Maps history. How smart and connected can they be, and how good (or unsettling) will that feel?

Google’s also not enabling any livestreaming from these glasses, a move that leans towards careful compared to Meta’s livestreaming modes on its glasses. “We’ll always take the conservative path right now just to rebuild trust,” Payne said.

But it remains to be seen how much any of this will help make anyone in public feel comfortable with someone wearing a pair of these glasses with camera-enabled AI modes on. It’s already hard to convince people that smart glasses aren’t recording people all the time, and Google’s clearly trying to restore confidence in the idea of smart glasses in public situations.

The Warby Parker and Gentle Monster models are not the only new smart wearables Google has coming: A pair of tethered mixed reality glasses that come with a processor puck and work almost like a portable VR headset, called Xreal Aura, is arriving in the next few weeks. And Google’s also starting to test its display-enabled Intelligent Eyewear ahead of a possible launch next year.

Izadi also told me that more assistive features are coming to these camera-enabled glasses, including what Google calls Guided Vision, something of an extension to the Project Astra tech Google has been working on for years. “You’ll hear more about our plans in the coming months,” Izadi said.

But agentic AI is clearly the road going forward, something both Google and Meta are expanding on for glasses. “We’ll bring more and more agentic and core AI features so that people can depend more on the AI capabilities,” Izadi said. Google’s leaning on these glasses being true phone extensions, as well as watch-connected.

But there could be a lot of cascading hardware pieces in between. Google and Samsung have already promised multiple waves of tethered and wireless smart glasses, ones with displays and ones without, and these glasses are apparently just the start.

I’m curious to see how well Warby Parker and Gentle Monster approach support for the glasses and how easy it’ll be to understand how they work. Dec. 10 isn’t far away.

#3094. Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang Resigns

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

The company's founder stepped down after reports of multiple affairs.

Jensen Huang has resigned as CEO of $4 trillion chip giant Nvidia, just days after news broke that he was having an affair with a Chinese data center executive alleged to have ties to China’s intelligence community.

The company named Colette Kress, its chief financial officer, as interim CEO. Nvidia confirmed the news in a press release Thursday morning.

As reported Wednesday by The Wall Street Journal, the removal of Huang as CEO follows the revelation over the weekend that the company’s board of directors had opened an investigation into whether the company’s chief executive and founder had been targeted by foreign intelligence.

Huang had been revealed to be having an affair with Alice Huang (no relation), a Chinese citizen and executive at Megaspeed, a Singapore-based data center company that buys chips from Nvidia to run data centers that sell computing power to Chinese AI labs. News reports citing US intelligence officials have claimed Alice Huang was working as a spy for China. She was reportedly under surveillance by US intelligence, as reported in late October by The New York Times.

The Information reported on Tuesday that Alice Huang is believed to be involved with a network of Chinese companies that facilitated the sales of $7.5 billion in chips to China. The US has tried to control China’s access to advanced chips for years, but reports show that there are still illegal operations smuggling chips on the banned list from the US to China.

The New York Times also reported on Wednesday a separate affair between Huang and one of his direct reports at Nvidia.

The announcement of an investigation has rocked the AI chip behemoth, which makes the cutting-edge chips that enable AI and AI data centers. A proposed class-action lawsuit was filed on Monday against Nvidia, charging the company made false or misleading statements about Huang’s affair and the company’s relationship with Megaspeed.

On Tuesday, the Nvidia board said it had “retained former acting US Attorney for the Southern District of New York Joon H. Kim and his firm Wachtell, Lipton, Rosen & Katz to conduct a thorough and independent investigation.”

#3093. 2 Memory Stocks to Buy and Hold Through 2030

Memory chip stocks were hot in 2026, but they will continue to be top picks through 2030.

Memory stocks have had quite the year in 2026. Two of the best performers are Sandisk (SNDK -6.81%) and Micron (MU -2.44%), which are each among the top five best-performing stocks in the S&P 500 (^GSPC -0.43%) this year.

Sandisk holds second place, rising around 270%, while Micron has risen about 150%. However, I think both of these stocks are due for more over the next few years, and all investors have to do is listen to what they're saying.

I think these two will be great stocks to buy and hold over the next few years, and could deliver monster returns.

Memory chips are required for many devices to function. Long-term data storage utilizes NAND memory, such as in solid-state drives (SSDs). Computing units need DRAM memory to function, and these are being deployed heavily in data centers for computing power.

While memory chips are necessary, there isn't much to differentiate one memory chip from another. With no competitive advantage, memory chips basically became a commodity. As a result, pricing depends heavily on supply and demand. This makes memory chips a cyclical industry, and investors have historically given companies in these fields deep discounts to avoid losing vast sums of money when the cycle turns.

That's what the market is betting will happen for Micron and Sandisk. With the AI build-out in full force and consuming all available memory, demand for chip production capacity for their products has never been higher. With production capacity sold out, prices have also skyrocketed. This has led to ludicrous growth rates for each company, but the market isn't valuing them fully because of their cyclical nature.

For reference, the S&P 500 trades at 19 times forward earnings, so if both stocks just had a market-average valuation, they would rise nearly 5 times in value. But because the market is betting on a downturn, they're cheap.

Both of these companies are taking steps to reduce the cyclical nature of their businesses. Instead of selling products on the open market, both are now entering into long-term agreements with their clients. Sandisk calls this its "New Business Model" (NBM), and a large chunk of its revenue is coming from these agreements. Micron is doing the same thing and has locked in approximately 35% of revenue through 2030 with these types of contracts.

Why is this a big deal? It raises the floor for the companies. Instead of dealing with a significant pricing decline as demand falls when the AI build-out ends, there are floor pricing levels with their clients that keep the business running well. So, Micron and Sandisk may not see the massive pricing highs they're seeing now ever again, but they won't experience the lows that result in the stocks not being highly valued. This means the market should reprice the stock accordingly, which could lead to massive upside in the near future.

Furthermore, the industry has been sold out for many years, and Micron told investors that market conditions in 2027 and 2028 will likely be tighter than in 2026. The reality is that the memory space will be tied up for multiple years, likely through 2030, and these two stocks will make for excellent investments during that time. There's a good chance memory stocks will become a hot commodity sometime in the near future, and that makes now the perfect time to load up on them.

#3092. Ray Dalio says Bessent move is sign that a debt crisis is getting closer; recommends gold and bitcoin

Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent's debt buyback announcement this week fits into a broader pattern that can signal a forthcoming crisis, according to billionaire investor Ray Dalio.

The Bridgewater Associates founder said Bessent's plan to increase government debt purchases may portend trouble for the U.S. economy. Coupled with the Japanese government reducing its U.S. bond market exposure and surging long-dated American bond yields, Dalio said investors may want to prepare their portfolios for increased risk by owning cryptocurrencies and gold.

"I am confident that the government's financial condition is at an inflection point," Dalio wrote in a LinkedIn post published Friday. "If this is not dealt with now, the debts will build up to levels where they can't be managed without great trauma."

Dalio noted that the Treasury Department has "only limited capacity" to buy back bonds. Bessent told CNBC on Thursday that his team was going to "make a market" and that the purchases would likely top $4 billion.

The U.S. is spending about 40% more than it's bringing in, resulting in the burgeoning budget deficit, Dalio said. The U.S. budget deficit topped $432 billion in July, but Bessent told CNBC that it's likely peaked under President Donald Trump's administration.

Bessent said in the interview that a team was looking at ways to shrink spending by hundreds of billions of dollars. But Dalio said there is "very little ability" to do so given that it is either committed or considered essential.

After years of overspending, Dalio said total debt now dwarfs what the U.S. brings in each year. If the U.S. government was a business, Dalio said debt service payments would come in at roughly $11 trillion — about 200% of annual revenue.

Dalio, 77, warned the cost of repaying principal and servicing the debt will only grow over time.

To solve this, Dalio said the U.S. needs to carefully employ a three-part strategy to get the budget deficit down to 3% of gross domestic product.

First, Dalio said the U.S. government needs to reduce its spending. Second, he said tax revenue needs to be raised. Finally, the New York native and Harvard Business School alumni said lower interest rates were needed.

"All three need to happen concurrently so as to prevent any one from being too large," Dalio said. "If any one is too large, the adjustment will be traumatic."

Dalio cautioned against ramming through these adjustments by "force." For instance, "it would be very bad if the Federal Reserve unnaturally forced interest rates down," he said.

Dalio said it's important to to take steps now, while the economy is healthy. An economy in recession requires increased government spending, he said.

Dalio said the exact timing of a debt crisis can be swayed by variables ranging from military conflict to political change. On its current trajectory, the U.S. could enter such a crisis in as early as one year or as late as five. "My guess, which I suppose will be a bad one, is that it will come in three years, give or take two, if the course we're on is not changed."

To prepare, Dalio recommended investors are underweight debt assets, such as bonds.

As much as 10% to 15% of a portfolio could land in gold, as well as "a bit" of bitcoin, the hedge fund founder said.

Dalio's post came at the tail end of a volatile week for U.S. financial markets. Rising long-term Treasury yields have pressured stocks, leading the S&P 500 to snap a a three-week-long advance.

#3091. The Homebuying Advice Service Members Need (but Don’t Always Get)

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

So how do we make money? Our partners compensate us for advertisements that appear on our site. This compensation helps us provide tools and services - like free credit score access and monitoring. With the exception of mortgage, home equity and other home-lending products or services, partner compensation is one of several factors that may affect which products we highlight and where they appear on our site. Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

VA mortgage borrowers are quietly navigating a knowledge gap. Here’s how to close it.

If your understanding of a VA home loan starts and ends with “it’s for the military,” you’re not alone. For many eligible veterans and service members, the intricate workings (and benefits) of this U.S. government-backed mortgage are obscured behind lackluster guidance.

While there are classes and resources available, the military is broadly wary of steering members in a specific direction, says Alex Rodino, a real estate agent and U.S. Army veteran who served 10 years as an infantry officer and military advisor. “A lot of the course material is just very generic,” he says.

And that’s not a surprise. Considering the sheer size and scope of the military, providing personalized advice on VA loans to all its members would be no easy task.

That leaves real estate agents and lenders as the last line of defense, which is where a big part of the knowledge gap exists. Rodino purchased his first home in 2020 with a VA loan and says he didn’t like how ignorant he felt during the process.

“The reason I actually got my real estate license to begin with was because of that experience I had,” Rodino says. “Looking back on it, I had a lot of questions that didn't really get answered.”

But how can VA borrowers navigate this knowledge gap without having to become mortgage experts in their spare time? Here are some of the most important things to know when considering a VA loan, according to the financial experts and borrowers who have lived it.

“My mortgage broker said, hey, you might qualify for this, look it up,” says Elizabeth Valente, a real estate investor and former U.S. Army officer who bought her first home using a VA loan in 2021. When it came to the application process, she felt crucial information wasn’t readily available to her or fully explained.

In hindsight, Valente says, working with an agent or lender who had detailed VA loan experience would have been beneficial. Not only would it help untangle the specific paperwork and appraisal requirements, but that expertise offers an important layer of support for military borrowers.

“For some processes in the military, it's very hand-holding,” Valente says. “I'll tell you what to wear, what to bring, when to be there.” But that’s not always true when transitioning back to civilian life, and parsing specifics of a loan process can be challenging. “I don't think a typical Realtor would understand what that person's going through,” Valente says.

Finding professionals with the right expertise involves asking questions — and lots of them.

“If I'm the smartest person in the room, we have an issue,” Valente says. “Don't be afraid to tell someone if something doesn't make sense or if you don't understand the numbers.”

Before agreeing to work with a lender or mortgage broker, ask how their process works, what companies they work with, and how familiar they are with VA loans. Treat the initial conversation like an interview — you’re hiring them, not the other way around.

Knowing where to find your Certificate of Eligibility (COE) and how much you might be eligible to borrow is the responsibility of the person applying, Valente says. “All that information — it is available in the [VA] website but you really have to dig for it.”

And this information isn’t recreational reading — your COE lists your "basic entitlement," which is a crucial figure in VA lending. If you have full basic entitlement, the U.S. Department of Veterans Affairs (VA) generally guarantees 25% of the loan, which in most cases lets you put 0% down.

Why? With a quarter of the loan guaranteed by the VA, the lender is more protected and thus more willing to forgo a down payment.

So how do you know if you have full basic entitlement? Look for the figure $36,000 on your COE. This dollar amount represents full entitlement. Technically, basic entitlement is for loans up to $144,000, but then your bonus entitlement kicks in and you can go higher. The VA doesn’t set loan limits when you have full entitlement — though your lender will still decide how much you can borrow based on your income, debts, credit and the home’s appraised value.

Let’s say you want to buy a home in Fayetteville, North Carolina, and you need a $200,000 loan. In order to put no money down, your entitlement needs to cover at least 25% of the loan amount — in this example, that’s $50,000. After you purchase the home, your COE will show your basic entitlement as $0 and the entitlement charged to a previous loan as $50,000.

“Serving in the military is a unique investing opportunity that most people don't get,” Rodino says. “I wish someone had told me to buy a house when I got to my first duty station.”

If he’d known it was possible or understood how, Rodino says he would have been able to buy in 2017, move in 2020, rent out his first home and then repeat the process. “I'd have three by now and the first one would probably be paid off.”

This particular plan is partly based on the unique schedule of many military members. “Generally, you're moving anywhere from two to four to six years,” Rodino says. Owning multiple properties also leverages two big (and often misunderstood) perks of the VA loan: It’s possible to have more than one active loan at the same time, and after one year, you may be able to convert your primary residence to a rental.

Let’s go back to Fayetteville, North Carolina. After two years, you decide to rent out the home because you’re moving to Bell County, Texas. The home you want to buy in Bell County is $300,000, and in order to make no down payment, you need the VA to guarantee 25% of the loan ($75,000).

Because you no longer have full basic entitlement — remember, you already have an active VA loan elsewhere — your entitlement is capped at 25% of the conforming loan limit in the county where you plan to buy, minus what’s already being used on your first loan ($50,000 in our Fayetteville example).

Now for the math: The current conforming loan limit in Bell County is $832,750. Multiply this figure by 25% and subtract the $50,000 already in use: You have $158,187.50 of entitlement available to you — plenty to guarantee the $75,000 needed on the new loan.

If all of this sounds overwhelming, you’re not wrong. There are resources out there to help you figure all of this out, Rodino says. “The problem is, they're not always practical or they're not always apparent.”

The VA has a website that can walk you through entitlement calculations, but you should also ask your lender, broker or agent. If you’re working with experienced folks, they’ll be able to explain these details and help you get the most out of the VA’s guarantees.

The key takeaway is this: While each VA loan must initially be for a primary residence, borrowers can leverage their entitlement to build an investment portfolio without needing to cough up large down payments.

But these examples don’t consider all of the factors that go into approvals. Like most mortgages, your income, debts and assets dictate your eligibility. And although the VA doesn’t impose credit score minimums, most lenders do — NerdWallet's analysis shows 620 is a common floor.

It’s also important to note that a VA loan’s 0% down isn’t free. VA loans carry a funding fee based on how much you’re putting down and whether it’s your first or subsequent use (certain disabled veterans are exempt). You can pay this up front at closing or roll it into the loan amount; again, your team of professionals should be able to walk you through your options.

“I think there's the assumption that this is what we do: We get our VA home loan and we go buy a home,” says Julie Soares, financial readiness director for the Wounded Warrior Project. When we’re given access to a benefit that offers discounts and perks, it can be hard not to take advantage of it. But this is why financial awareness is critical.

There are a lot of conversations happening right now around homeownership and whether it’s the right choice, especially as affordability remains challenging. So if you haven’t heard it recently, here’s a reminder: Buying property is not the only way to build wealth or create financial security.

“The financial readiness program at Wounded Warrior Project is really leaning into getting further upstream in those conversations,” Soares says. Having a clear understanding of your benefits before you need to use them allows you to make more informed choices.

While it's helpful to understand your entitlement and what you may be eligible to borrow, you don't have to know every little detail about mortgages, or navigate the process alone. Sometimes, the most important thing to know about VA loans is where to turn for help.

If you are unsure of your eligibility or want more information about home loans, you can call the VA's home loans hotline at 877-827-3702.

“Please reach out to Wounded Warrior Project, reach out to the VA, reach out to any service organization,” Soares says. “Not one organization is gonna be able to fix or solve all of these problems. We have to do this as a group.”

#3089. Investing $196 Per Week in This ETF Could Build a $1 Million Portfolio. Here's How.

Time can do a lot of the heavy lifting for you.

For investors saving up for retirement, hitting the million-dollar mark is a huge financial milestone to be proud of. It takes a lot of work. Surprisingly, it doesn't actually require saving a million bucks, which would be very difficult for many investors.

Thanks to the power of compounding interest, an investor can potentially hit the million-dollar-saved mark by investing just $196 weekly in an S&P 500 (^GSPC -0.69%) exchange-traded fund (ETF) like the Vanguard S&P 500 ETF (VOO -0.58%).

Since it began trading in September 2010, the VOO ETF has averaged 12.5% annual returns (14.6% when dividends are included). That's above-average growth, so it might be better to calculate returns using the S&P 500's long-term return average of 10%. Assuming it averages a more modest 10% annually, $196 invested weekly ($10,192 annually) could easily grow to $1 million in 25 years.

That's the power of compounding interest.

If VOO maintains its 12.5% average (which is unlikely but not impossible), an investor could hit the $1 million mark in a little under 22 years. If dividends are reinvested and the 14.6% total return is used, the $1 million mark is reached in just over 20 years.

Past results don't guarantee future performance, so you never want to assume anything when it comes to potential returns. Historically, the S&P 500 has always grown over the long term, and that's likely to continue. However, only time will tell what that growth looks like (if at all).

Either way, the most important thing you can do as an investor is to stay consistent and let time do much of the heavy lifting. There will be inevitable rough patches along the way, but VOO is made up of roughly 500 of the world's most successful and promising companies. It doesn't make it foolproof, but it does put the odds in your favor.

#3088. Redwire Pivoted to Orbital Computing Right as Anthropic Said It Wants Gigawatts in Space. Is This $8 Stock the Cheapest Way to Play the Data Center Satellite Concept?

Redwire stock offers investors upside potential, but significant risks remain.

On Sept. 30, aerospace tech company  Redwire (RDW -3.93%) announced a partnership with Sophia Space to develop infrastructure for orbital data center satellites. Just months earlier, artificial intelligence (AI) lab Anthropic had disclosed its interest in working with Space Exploration Technologies to put multiple gigawatts of computing capacity into orbit.

For Redwire, the timing couldn't be much better. The stock, recently trading around $8 per share, offers investors exposure to a potentially enormous new market. But whether it's actually a good bargain will depend on how quickly orbital computing becomes commercially viable.

Under its agreement with Sophia Space, Redwire will explore developing scalable computing infrastructure using Sophia's modular TILE architecture. This technology combines computing hardware, solar power, and thermal management systems designed to operate in space. These are essential components of data center satellites, which would use freely available solar energy to power their computing systems.

The companies plan to pursue government and commercial opportunities, too. This would potentially include projects involving the U.S. military.

In May, Anthropic announced an agreement to lease more than 300 megawatts of computing capacity at SpaceX's Colossus 1 data center in Tennessee. It also expressed interest in partnering with SpaceX to develop multiple gigawatts of orbital AI computing capacity.

To be sure, building that much compute capacity and placing it in orbit would require enormous investments in solar arrays, spacecraft, rocket fuel, power distribution, and cooling equipment. But it's worth noting that Redwire already manufactures several of the key technologies needed, putting it in a position to benefit if orbital computing takes off.

In the third quarter, Redwire's revenue increased 5.4% year over year to $109 million. Its backlog reached a record $619.7 million, up nearly 75%, while its gross margin improved from 16.3% to 32.3%. Management also reaffirmed its full-year revenue forecast range of $450 million to $500 million.

Of course, Redwire also reported a quarterly net loss of $23.2 million and adjusted earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation, and amortization (EBITDA) of negative $5.3 million. Given the company's market capitalization of approximately $2 billion, the stock trades around 4.6 times trailing revenue. That's hardly an obvious bargain valuation for an unprofitable company.

Orbital computing faces substantial obstacles, including launch costs, protecting delicate hardware from radiation exposure, communications bandwidth limitations, the difficulty of cooling servers efficiently in a vacuum, and an inability to easily maintain or update satellites’ hardware. Indeed, large-scale commercial deployments of cost-effective data center satellites could take years.

Redwire also faces competition from other space infrastructure suppliers, including companies that could develop their own systems. Nevertheless, it does have an advantage over companies entering the market without established aerospace operations. Redwire already supplies a variety of types of hardware used in space, has existing government and commercial customers, and possesses technologies applicable to orbital computing.

With all that in mind, the company offers speculative exposure to this emerging industry. But its low share price doesn't automatically mean the stock is cheap.

I consider Redwire an interesting long-term space infrastructure investment rather than a proven orbital data center winner. Its upside could be substantial if the market develops, but don't assume those future contracts are guaranteed.

#3087. Top Wall Street analysts believe in the growth potential of these 3 stocks

Geopolitical uncertainty in the Middle East and soaring bond yields weighed on global markets this past week. And investors are closely assessing the valuations of artificial intelligence stocks amid concerns about the sustainability of high spending on AI infrastructure.

Despite those continued challenges, top Wall Street analysts are bullish on the prospects of several stocks due to their long-run growth potential and exposure to lucrative business lines, including AI.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Cybersecurity company CrowdStrike Holdings (CRWD) is this week's first stock pick. The company is scheduled to announce fiscal second-quarter results on August 26.

Ahead of Q2 FY27 earnings, Truist analyst Junaid Siddiqui reiterated a buy rating on CrowdStrike stock and raised his price target to $245 from $187.50. Following CrowdStrike's impressive fiscal first-quarter performance and guidance raise, Siddiqui expects another strong quarter.

The 5-star analyst's optimism is backed by continued strength in Falcon Flex adoption and encouraging large deal activity. Siddiqui also cited continued momentum across emerging products. With the FY27 outlook assuming persistent strength in CrowdStrike's net new annual recurring revenue (ARR), Siddiqui is more focused on the longevity of key growth catalysts such as Falcon Flex adoption, platform consolidation and AI-led demand.

The analyst highlighted that talks with partners were encouraging, with pipeline generation and momentum in large deals remaining healthy in the fiscal second quarter despite tough, year-over-year comparisons.

"We continue to view CrowdStrike as one of the primary beneficiaries of platform consolidation, though we expect investors to remain focused on competitive dynamics and whether current growth levels represent a new baseline or a period of demand acceleration," the analyst said.

Siddiqui ranks No. 226 among more than 12,400 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 80% of the time, delivering an average return of about 40%. See CrowdStrike Financials on TipRanks.

Shares of personal computer maker Dell Technologies (DELL) have rallied 251% this year, driven by strong demand for AI servers. Recently, Evercore analyst Amit Daryanani reiterated a buy rating on DELL and raised his price target to $550 from $500.

The 5-star analyst contends that while Dell's AI compute segment is getting investors' attention, the company's Storage business is underappreciated and well-positioned to drive revenue and profit acceleration. Daryanani expects the storage business to see higher demand once enterprises start to bring workload back on-premises.

The analyst highlighted that Dell's Storage business, estimated to account for only 10% of FY27 revenue, is one of the factors that could help improve profit margins, given its higher profitability compared to AI servers. Daryanani estimates that the Storage business's operating margin is currently in the low-20% range, while margins for the AI server and traditional server businesses are in the mid-single-digit and mid-teens range, respectively.

Overall, Daryanani views the Storage business as an important catalyst for three reasons: Dell has one of the broadest storage portfolios in the industry; Storage has a significantly higher margin profile than AI servers; and the shift toward proprietary, software-defined storage and AI-focused platforms.

"We think DELL's storage business is becoming increasingly strategic as AI infrastructure deployments move beyond GPU procurement and toward full stack + AI-ready architectures," said Daryanani.

Daryanani ranks No. 19 among more than 12,400 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 71% of the time, delivering an average return of 38.70%. See Dell Technologies Statistics on TipRanks.

SanDisk (SNDK) is benefiting from AI-driven demand for its memory and storage products. Following the company's 2026 Investor Day event, J.P. Morgan analyst Harlan Sur resumed research coverage of SNDK with a buy rating (it was earlier, "Not Rated") and a price target of $2,250.

The 5-star analyst believes SanDisk is well-positioned to capitalize on a structural inflection reached in NAND demand, driven by strong growth in AI inference. Sur's bullishness is based on three key factors.

First, Sur believes that SanDisk's New Business Model (NBM) framework (or long-term agreements) has enhanced its margin profile and reduced cyclicality. SNDK's eight signed NBMs represent a total contract value of $94 billion and a gross margin of about 80%, even at the floor pricing.

Second, Sur highlighted the growing NAND market and expects the total addressable market to surge from about $70 billion in calendar year 2025 to over $300 billion in 2026 and about $500 billion in 2027. Importantly, the analyst noted that data centers are the largest driver of NAND growth, as hyperscalers increasingly deploy flash for AI inference workloads.

Finally, Sur is optimistic on SanDisk due to its technology dominance. The company's BiCS10 technology is sampling ahead of schedule and has 65% more bits per wafer than BiCS8, the analyst said. Also, Sur expects SanDisk's High Bandwidth Flash to become a differentiated memory platform for AI inference.

"Layered on top, mgmt's commitment to returning 100% of excess cash flow to shareholders should drive an aggressive share count reduction trajectory that further amplifies EPS growth on a per-share basis," said Sur.

Sur ranks No. 15 among more than 12,400 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 71% of the time, delivering an average return of 43.20%. See SanDisk Ownership Structure on TipRanks.
