#3086. 2 AI Robotics Stocks That Could Help Set You Up for Life

While AI models and chips take center stage, the AI robotics boom is silently building.

While chips and AI models have been receiving most of the attention, robotics is a long-term growth opportunity that can mint new millionaires. Robots can help with a wide range of tasks, including security, physical labor, and military.

Grandview Research projects a 32% CAGR for the AI robotics industry through 2033, and these two robotics stocks are well-positioned for that opportunity.

All robots need GPUs to operate, and Nvidia (NVDA -13.63%) remains the gold standard. It's leagues ahead of the competition, with revenue surging by 106% year-over-year in its fiscal 2027 second quarter.

The high revenue growth also comes with rising profit margins. Net income was up by 126% year-over-year, and net profit margins reached 62%. Nvidia was able to return $26 billion to shareholders that quarter and guided for 12.2% sequential growth in its fiscal 2027 third quarter.

Despite the strong quarter, Nvidia has stumbled significantly over the past month. The main headwind for the company is Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang's two affairs. News about an affair with a spy was quickly followed by details about a separate affair with a Nvidia executive.

These details are affecting Nvidia's stock price because the company's board is reportedly preparing to oust the CEO from his position. Investors tend to get jittery when there is uncertainty around a company's leadership.

However, Nvidia's AI chips are already the preferred choice for many companies, and the company is far more than Jensen. Just as Apple (AAPL +0.68%) has continued to thrive after Steve Jobs' passing and Tim Cook stepping down as CEO, I anticipate a similar story for Nvidia, assuming Huang is actually removed from his position. If Huang manages to stay put, the entire dip over the past week would be hard to justify.

The robotics industry will need plenty of chips to support the long-term buildout. Nvidia has the strongest reputation and delivers better results than other GPUs. Its market share should be safe regardless of recent developments.

Just as humanoid robots need GPUs, they also need memory chips. Micron (MU -4.53%) specializes in that part of the trade and has delivered solid fundamental results that justify its shares almost tripling year-to-date.

The company's high-bandwidth memory chips have become a staple for many hyperscalers and data centers. This level of reach, combined with ongoing memory chip shortages, helped Micron boost its revenue by 30.8% sequentially in its fiscal 2026 fourth quarter.

The chipmaker crushed guidance by delivering $54.23 billion in revenue during that quarter instead of the expected $50 billion. Micron anticipates a stronger fiscal 2027 and guided for $61.5 billion at the midpoint for Q1 FY27.

Humanoid robots can eventually become a big part of Micron's fundamental growth. These robots require robust data centers, and all of those facilities need plenty of memory chips. However, each of these same humanoid robots needs its own memory chips inside the physical unit to function properly. Each humanoid robot increases the need for memory chips for physical units and the actual data centers.

While you can guess which humanoid robot will be the biggest winner, Micron is essentially selling shovels during the gold rush. All of the humanoid robot start-ups will need memory chips, including the ones that turn into tech giants in the future.

In the meantime, Micron is already producing substantial revenue growth while guiding for even better results in future quarters. All of that momentum also comes with high net profit margins. That figure came to 69.5% in the most recent quarter, showing that Micron keeps a large majority of its revenue.

A 4 forward P/E ratio is the icing on the cake that highlights Micron's low valuation compared to most tech stocks. It's trading like a bank stock despite delivering some of the highest growth rates in the entire stock market.

#3085. First Look: Inside United’s New Club at Washington Dulles

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

So how do we make money? Our partners compensate us for advertisements that appear on our site or when you click to or take an action on their website. This compensation helps us provide tools and services - like free credit score access and monitoring. With the exception of mortgage, home equity and other home-lending products or services, partner compensation is one of several factors that may affect which products we highlight and where they appear on our site. Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

The airline’s newest lounge will be the second-largest in the network, offering flyers an upgraded experience at Dulles.

You’ll no longer have to go to the basement to get into a United Club at Washington Dulles International Airport.

The brand-new United Club in the new IAD Concourse E spans nearly 40,000 square feet across an upstairs floor and can seat 630 passengers, making it a significant upgrade to the carrier’s existing lounge portfolio at the airport. It’s the second-largest United Club after its newest lounge in Houston, which opened just weeks earlier.

The new lounge by gates E14 and E16 opens to customers Nov. 10, 2026. United invited me to a media preview of the lounge, and here’s what you can expect inside.

The lounge features several nods to D.C.’s iconic landmarks and monuments, as well as the region’s natural beauty.

When you first enter the lounge, you’ll need to scan your boarding pass and then head up the escalators (stairs and elevators are also available) to get to the lounge.

One of my favorite design features as a former D.C. resident is that escalator ride up, which has been recreated to feel like you’re inside a Metro station.

Thankfully, the escalator ride is not as long as at some stations. Looking at you, Dupont Circle!

Once you're upstairs, you’ll walk into the heart of the lounge, with two buffet stations, a centerpiece bar and plenty of seating options, whether you’re traveling as a group or by yourself. (More on food and beverage options later.)

It’s impossible to miss the soaring ceilings in this lounge, especially compared to the bunker-like atmosphere at some of the existing United Clubs.

Don’t forget to look up, where you’ll see a cherry blossom sculpture, an homage to one of the region’s most popular attractions.

There are also two additional lounging areas on both sides of the central area. One is inspired by the Blue Ridge Mountains, which you can see in the distance.

The other has a cherry blossom theme, with light fixtures that branch out from the ceiling.

Massive windows run throughout the lounge, allowing in tons of natural light and offering fantastic views of the airfield. United has a major presence at IAD, so expect to see plenty of United planes.

The United Club has a full kitchen, allowing it to offer upgraded food options compared to its existing lounges at IAD.

You can expect to find sandwiches, salads and hot protein dishes. The lounge also had a ramen bar and a chili bar set up during my visit.

The two buffet stations are located right next to each other, with a dessert station in between.

If you want something to drink, you can head to the bar or one of the hydration stations throughout the space.

There’s plenty of seating at the bar, which has a double archway design. There are also large TVs if you’re trying to keep up with one of Washington’s many sports teams.

The United Club has eight phone booths for those who need to make a call. They’re split between the two lounging areas in the club.

You'll also find a single-stall private restroom, a wellness room, and dedicated men’s and women’s restrooms.

There are no showers in this lounge. To access a shower in a United lounge, you’ll need to head to the Polaris Lounge, reserved exclusively for eligible business class passengers flying on United and select partner airlines.

You can access the United Club by holding elite status, a lounge membership, the right credit card or an eligible premium cabin ticket.

If there’s availability, United sells day passes at the door for flights departing within three hours. You’ll also need a valid same-day boarding pass on a United or Star Alliance flight.

Holding the right credit card is probably the easiest way to get in. United’s most high-end card comes with a United Club membership, while others might offer one-time passes.

If you’re looking for a United lounge, the newest club in Concourse E is where you want to be. It offers more space, lots of light and elevated food options compared to other United Clubs.

Its massive footprint could also ease overcrowding, a common complaint with other lounges.

One thing to consider is location. The concourses at IAD are spread out, and you may need to take a mobile lounge or train to get there. If you want to check out the lounge, make sure you give yourself enough time to get to your gate.

#3084. Forget Buying All 7 "Magnificent Seven" Stocks. Here's How to Rank 6 Top AI Stocks Right Now (Hint: It Has to Do With Anthropic.)

Some AI stocks are far more tied to Anthropic's IPO than others.

The "Magnificent Seven" has become the market's shorthand for AI investing. These giants each have played a role in the hot technology and have reaped the rewards. But owning all seven isn't the only way to play this boom, and right now it may not be the sharpest one. A new name is about to dominate the conversation: Anthropic, the company behind the Claude AI models.

Anthropic isn't public yet, but it's close. The AI lab said that it aims to sell 825 million shares in the range of $70 to $78. CNBC, citing people familiar with the matter, says pricing may happen on Nov. 18, and trading will begin the following day. Investors have closely watched Anthropic in recent months as it announced its intention to launch an IPO. Considering Anthropic's soaring revenue, growth investors may flock to this tech player.

Meanwhile, we may consider Anthropic-related investment opportunities, focusing on who gets paid when Anthropic spends. With all of this in mind, here's how I'd rank six AI stocks by how directly they're tied to the AI lab.

First, a quick catch-up. Anthropic was founded in 2021 and sells access to Claude through its platform and apps, including the Claude Code coding agent. Growth has soared, but so has the bill. The prospectus says Anthropic has roughly $900.9 billion in purchase and lease commitments, substantially all for compute and infrastructure, with about $70.7 billion due in the 12 months after Sept. 30. To keep up, it spreads work across chips from Amazon's (AMZN +0.71%) Amazon Web Services, Alphabet's (GOOG +0.38%) (GOOGL +0.68%) Google, Nvidia (NVDA -9.82%), and Advanced Micro Devices (AMD +1.58%).

Much of that spending lands on someone else's income statement, which makes "Anthropic exposure" a spectrum. Here's my scorecard, from most to least direct.

1. Amazon -- Very direct. Amazon is investor, supplier, and sales channel at once. It holds convertible notes that had $6.2 billion in principal outstanding as of June 30, according to the filing, which also says Amazon will own more than 5% of Anthropic after conversion. Anthropic runs Claude on Amazon's Trainium chips and sells it through AWS.

2. Alphabet -- Very direct. Alphabet also expects to hold more than 5%. It's one of seven companies that agreed to buy shares in a $26 billion private placement that closes after the IPO. Anthropic also relies on Google's TPUs.

3. Nvidia -- Direct, but narrower. Nvidia is participating in the same private placement, and Anthropic uses its graphics processing units (GPUs). But Anthropic deliberately mixes chips from several suppliers, and Nvidia's fortunes ride on far more than one customer.

4. Bloom Energy (BE -4.66%) -- Indirect. Anthropic plans dedicated data centers, with partners securing the power. Bloom's onsite fuel cells fit that need, and Bloom already supplies other AI data center builders, so the company could potentially benefit from Anthropic's growth.

5. Vistra (VST -0.60%) -- Indirect. Vistra sells electricity to data center operators; Meta Platforms, for instance, signed a 20-year deal for output from three of its nuclear plants. That shows the demand is real, but it's Meta's deal, not Anthropic's.

6. Oklo (OKLO -3.38%) -- Speculative. Oklo, an advanced nuclear tech company, is developing a power campus in Ohio that will support Meta's data centers in the area. So we could imagine Oklo potentially providing Anthropic with power down the road, but this link is hypothetical.

Amazon and Alphabet are also Anthropic's suppliers, so their gains depend on Anthropic's valuation as well as its spending. The $26 billion placement is contingent on the IPO closing, and a delay or weak debut could negatively impact those involved. The power names, meanwhile, may benefit from AI demand overall without ever signing a contract with Anthropic.

So rather than buying all seven "Magnificent Seven" stocks, if you aim to benefit from Anthropic's growth and the general AI boom, you might select a heavily exposed player such as Amazon as well as one with lighter exposure, like an energy company, and hold onto them as part of a well-diversified tech and industrial portfolio.

#3082. Prediction Markets Are Arriving in Your Investing App, and That Makes Us Nervous

NerdWallet, Inc. is an independent publisher and comparison service, not an investment advisor. Its articles, interactive tools and other content are provided to you for free, as self-help tools and for informational purposes only. They are not intended to provide investment advice. NerdWallet does not and cannot guarantee the accuracy or applicability of any information in regard to your individual circumstances. Examples are hypothetical, and we encourage you to seek personalized advice from qualified professionals regarding specific investment issues. Our estimates are based on past market performance, and past performance is not a guarantee of future performance.

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. And while our site doesn’t feature every company or financial product available on the market, we’re proud that the guidance we offer, the information we provide and the tools we create are objective, independent, straightforward — and free.

So how do we make money? Our partners compensate us. This may influence which products we review and write about (and where those products appear on the site), but it in no way affects our recommendations or advice, which are grounded in thousands of hours of research. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews of their products or services. Here is a list of our partners.

Prediction markets are booming, but that doesn’t make them a sound investment strategy.

The investing information provided on this page is for educational purposes only. NerdWallet, Inc. does not offer advisory or brokerage services, nor does it recommend or advise investors to buy or sell particular stocks, securities or other investments.

Around this time every year, we conduct NerdWallet’s annual broker review process. We assess what’s changed in the industry, discuss which brokerage features matter most (and which ones now matter less), and finally, we do the work by opening accounts to test them.

Prediction markets, which have made their way into some of the most popular investing apps, came up more than we expected in testing this year.

At first, we decided to simply collect information on who does and doesn’t offer access to them, without intending to reward or penalize a broker either way. It would be a neutral presentation of facts, not something that would factor into how we scored a broker.

But one afternoon, all together in the office, we confronted an uncomfortable question: “Do prediction markets, and such easy access to them within an investment app, go against NerdWallet’s editorial house views?”

After careful consideration, we all agreed: they do.

So we started to configure our 2027 rubric with a firmer stance against this trendy feature, penalizing brokers that actively market sports and entertainment event contracts, and whose prediction markets come with few, if any, guardrails.

We’ve distilled our discussion down to three main points:

These products are being marketed alongside investments.

We fear they could be discouraging long-term investing.

Let’s start here: Many aspects of trading could be seen as gambling-adjacent. Options, futures, highly leveraged single-day equity bets and even crypto can all easily fall into the “highly speculative” category. So why are we picking on prediction markets in particular?

In our view, a key differentiator is a much lower barrier to entry with few in-app guardrails in place.

Most of the products we mentioned above require some level of fluency — and at times even permission from the broker — to dabble in. To trade options, for example, you need to answer questions about your investing proficiency, your finances and more. And even after approval, the terminology required to trade can be so intimidating that the language itself can become an indirect deterrent.

Event contracts, which form the backbone of prediction markets, are largely presented in the vernacular, and our testing showed approval to trade them is completed with a few taps from the home screen. Moreover, their content is often unrelated to finance — according to a May 2026 report by the Pew Research Center, around 80% of volume on the prediction market platform Kalshi was for sports events[1]Pew Research Center. Trading Volume on Prediction Markets Has Soared in Recent Months. Accessed Nov 2, 2026. View all sources .

Answering a binary question (yes/no; this/that), posed in everyday language about a familiar topic — something like, “Will the Knicks beat the Spurs on Sunday?” — can be far less daunting than figuring out what an options chain is or understanding forex pairs.

In other words, what makes prediction markets accessible to a wider audience is also what makes them problematic, especially when housed within an investing account. Which brings us to our next point.

On the platforms that feature them, we found that prediction markets almost always appear alongside stock and ETF trading. We think this is a mistake. What has made these mobile-first investing apps so good for so long (making it easy to move, manage and invest money through a beautiful interface) is what’s now making us nervous.

For example, most investing platforms let users do any number of smart things with idle cash. They could open an Individual Retirement Account (IRA), a custodial account or a robo-managed portfolio all through a single app. But now, they can dip into that same pot of money to bet on whether Beyoncé or Alicia Keys will play the Super Bowl halftime show.

But is anyone actually trading these event contracts? Let’s use data from publicly traded brokers that offer prediction markets to explore this.

In Q3 2026, Robinhood’s revenue from event contracts rose more than 600% year over year to $173 million, while the number of event contracts traded grew more than sevenfold to 16.4 billion[2]Robinhood. Robinhood Reports Third Quarter 2026 Results. Accessed Nov 2, 2026. View all sources . This was the second-highest source of transaction revenue behind options trading. In a forecast reported on by Cointelegraph, Bernstein analysts projected Robinhood’s prediction markets could drive $1.7 billion in annual revenue by 2028[3]Yahoo Finance. Bernstein Sees Robinhood Prediction Markets Reaching $1.7 Billion in Revenue by 2028, Topping Crypto. Accessed Nov 2, 2026. View all sources .

Coinbase's Q1 2026 quarterly report called prediction markets “one of its fastest-scaling products ever, driven by strong retail interest[4]Coinbase. Coinbase Q1 Financial Results Show Resilient Financial Performance Driven by New All-Time High Crypto Trading Volume Market Share. Accessed Nov 2, 2026. View all sources ."

In a call discussing Webull’s Q2 results, U.S. CEO Anthony Denier noted trading volume in Webull’s nascent prediction market product grew about 70% quarter over quarter[5]Gloom. Webull (BULL) Q2 2026 earnings call transcript. Accessed Nov 3, 2026. View all sources .

Gemini’s Q2 report shows prediction market volume rose 93% quarter over quarter[6]Gemini. Shareholder Update Q2 2026. Accessed Nov 2, 2026. View all sources .

So, now that we’ve established that these markets are growing in popularity — and investing platforms appear to have a large incentive to lean into them — how are all those event contract traders faring? That brings us to our final point.

Is all this even worth it? Several studies show how hard it is to actually turn a profit in prediction markets. Here are a few:

One study, conducted by researchers at ESSEC Business School, HEC Montréal and the University of Toronto's Rotman School of Management, covered 2.4 million Polymarket accounts across $67 billion in trading volume between November 2022 and March 2026, and found that 69% of users lost money[7]SSRN. Who Wins and Who Loses In Prediction Markets? Evidence from Polymarket. Accessed Nov 2, 2026. View all sources . Moreover, the top 1% of users who made a profit accounted for about 76% of all gains. Translation: Even if you did turn a profit, there’s a good chance you didn’t make much.

A Bloomberg analysis supports this: From January 2025 to April 2026, more than 100,000 Polymarket accounts lost at least $1,000[8]Bloomberg. Most Prediction Market Traders Are Losing Money While Bots Rack Up Gains. Accessed Nov 3, 2026. View all sources . Of the 2 million active wallets studied, almost half made or lost less than $10 during the period, while far fewer accounts collected larger profits.

A separate Wall Street Journal analysis echoed the same trend: 67% of Polymarket profits go to 0.1% of accounts, and more than 70% of Polymarket users lost money[9]The Wall Street Journal. Why Almost Everyone Loses—Except a Few Sharks—on Prediction Markets. Accessed Nov 3, 2026. View all sources .

While there is far less data available on Kalshi trades (Polymarket runs on a public blockchain, making it easy to extract and analyze data), a Kalshi spokesperson confirmed in the Wall Street Journal analysis above that one month of data in 2026 showed there were 2.9 unprofitable users to each profitable user[10]Wall Street Journal. Why Almost Everyone Loses—Except a Few Sharks—on Prediction Markets. Accessed Nov 10, 2026. View all sources .

The first study above discusses how event contract trading is binary and zero-sum: If one trader wins, another loses. This means traders allocating money to these instruments could be missing out on aspects of equity markets that have helped create long-term wealth for previous generations.

“Unlike equity markets, there is no equity premium, no dividends, and no passive investment options. Their short maturities naturally encourage short-horizon trading, which is precisely what the behavioral finance literature has suggested is most destructive to wealth accumulation. Given the rapid growth of these platforms and their role as a potential entry point for young investors, understanding who bears the losses has direct implications for investor welfare and market regulation.”

As a reminder, the vast majority of Polymarket users bore the losses.

At NerdWallet, our mission has always been to help people make smarter financial decisions, so we’re sticking by that. Starting with our 2027 ratings, brokers that offer easy access to sports and entertainment event contracts will lose points in our scoring system because we believe this new product has the potential to harm long-term wealth building and doesn’t belong in investing platforms.

So, what’s a better way forward? Bring back the friction and make prediction markets a separate app. Financial institutions, businesses and individuals can still explore legitimate use cases for prediction markets, such as forecasting and hedging. And we’re not here to stop anyone from trading event contracts for entertainment with extra money. We just don’t want these markets so easily conflated with the legitimate investing vehicles these apps already offer. And our reviews will reflect that.

#3080. The 'debasement trade' returns after Bessent bond maneuver. Crypto and gold are back in style

The debasement trade is gaining new traction on Wall Street as concern over the size and cost of the budget deficit mounts.

The conventional wisdom behind the trade is that perceived hard assets, like cryptocurrencies and precious metals, gain as investors try to hedge against a weaker U.S. dollar and Treasury debt in the face of ballooning government spending. Those fears reached a fever pitch last week before, and after, the Treasury Department's unusual step of increasing debt buybacks under Secretary Scott Bessent.

"The size of the Treasury purchases announced so far by Bessent are trivial in comparison to the size of the overall market, but the [signaling] effect was very powerful," said Stephen Coltman, head of macro at 21Shares, a crypto-focused creator of exchange-traded funds.

Gold touched three-month highs Monday, building on last week's advance of more than 5%. The yellow metal has climbed for five straight weeks and in August is on track for its biggest monthly rise since 1999.

Bitcoin added 2% Monday, to the highest since May. Last week, the digital currency soared 22%, its biggest three-day rally since 2023. Overnight Tuesday, the crypto touched $80,000.

The U.S. dollar index, which tracks the dollar against six other leading currencies, hit three-month lows last week and recorded its third down week in the last four. The index was little changed on Monday as investors expressed little appetite to bid up the greenback.

The Treasury Department said last week that it would double the maximum size of its bond buyback, to at least $4 billion from $2 billion. Two senior Treasury officials told CNBC Monday that the department could use its General Account to help fund the plans.

Last week's announcement followed news that the monthly U.S. budget deficit in July reached a five-year high and came at the same time as total federal government debt topped $40 trillion. Bessent told CNBC last week that he wields a "big toolkit" to calm the government bond market amid concerns about the government's financial health.

Long-dated U.S. Treasury yields surged last week, at one point sending the 30-year yield to almost a 20-year high of 5.34%, up from 4.82% in late June. Yields dipped and then rebounded in the wake of the Treasury buyback move, a signal that bond investors believed Bessent's moves were inadequate.

"Markets are saying something," the billionaire philanthropist and former energy trader John Arnold said in a Friday post on X. The weaker dollar, lower Treasury prices and strengthening hard assets, are "all part of the debasement trade," he wrote.

The Treasury's moves could aid the bond market, but also translate into outsized pain for the dollar, according to Nohshad Shah, Citadel Securities' head of fixed income sales for Europe, the Middle East and Africa.

The stakes are high for monetary policymakers, as a weaker greenback can ease financial conditions, Shah said. U.S. inflation — above the Federal Reserve's 2% target for five years — could worsen.

The Fed might need to hike interest rates in response, Shah said. Fed funds futures reflect about a 56% chance the central bank raises borrowing costs at its October meeting, up more than seven percentage points from a week ago, according to CME's FedWatch tool.

"The bond market's message is straightforward: fiscal or monetary policy should be tighter," Shah said in a Monday note. "Households may ultimately pay for policymakers' unwillingness to fix the roof whilst the sun is shining."

Coltman at 21Shares said the appetite for alternative stores of value also stems from mounting geopolitical tension. The U.S. on Monday rolled out a global sanctions program focused on segregating Iran from the global economy, days after slapping 50% tariffs on billions of dollars in Canadian exports.

Still, assets that would benefit from a debasement trade have recently received votes of confidence on Wall Street.

Deutsche Bank analyst Michael Hsueh said in a Monday note that gold could surpass his target price of $4,800 an ounce. Already, that level suggests the precious metal only need to rise another 3% or so from where it closed on Friday. "We see the Treasury policy change as underlining the gold constructive view," Hsueh said.

Billionaire investor Ray Dalio recommended investors remain overweight gold and bitcoin ahead of what he warned could become a debt crisis in the U.S. as a result of unchecked government spending. Gold could account for as much as 15% of a model portfolio, the Bridgewater Associates founder said.

"The government's financial condition is at an inflection point," Dalio wrote on LinkedIn Friday. "If this is not dealt with now, the debts will build up to levels where they can't be managed without great trauma."

Others are less sure, devaluing the notion of a debasement trade.

It's probably too early to endorse the idea unless it's clear that the Fed will go along with the Treasury Department, said Alexander Lis, investing chief at Social Discovery Ventures.

#3079. Chewy vs. Etsy: Which Consumer Stock Is a Better Buy for 2027?

Chewy's subscription-driven pet sales face off against Etsy's marketplace of independent sellers, and the valuation gap between them may tip the balance.

As e-commerce shifts from broad retail to specialized niches, many investors are deciding between Chewy (CHWY +0.47%) and Etsy (ETSY +0.04%) to determine which model offers better resilience through 2027.

While both companies operate within the digital marketplace, they serve vastly different needs. Chewy focuses on recurring household essentials for pet owners, while Etsy thrives on unique, discretionary items crafted by entrepreneurs, making this a choice between stability and high-margin creativity.

Chewy is a leading online destination for pet owners, offering everything from food and supplies to medications and professional services. Its primary strength lies in its Autoship subscription program, which drives significant customer loyalty and provides high visibility into future sales. In its latest annual report filed for the fiscal year ended Feb. 1, 2026, the company detailed its expansion into Canada and its massive catalog of over 190,000 products.

During fiscal 2025, revenue reached nearly $12.6 billion, representing growth of approximately 6.2% compared to the previous year. The company reported net income of roughly $222.8 million, which resulted in a net margin of about 1.8%. This positive performance reflects a continued focus on cost management while growing the top line.

As of Chewy's February 2026 balance sheet, the debt-to-equity ratio -- a measure of how much debt is used relative to shareholder money -- is approximately 1. The current ratio, which compares short-term assets to short-term liabilities, is close to 0. Free cash flow for the year was nearly $562.4 million, calculated as cash from operations minus capital expenditures. Note that stock-based compensation accounted for roughly 43.1% of operating cash flow, inflating reported cash generation, since SBC is a non-cash expense added back in the cash flow statement.

Etsy operates a global marketplace for unique and creative goods, connecting millions of buyers with independent sellers who offer handmade or vintage items. Its revenue comes primarily from transaction fees, payment processing, and optional services provided to its 5.6 million active sellers. This asset-light model allows the company to facilitate billions in sales without holding inventory or managing its own warehouses.

In fiscal 2025, revenue reached nearly $2.9 billion, reflecting approximately 2.7% growth over the prior year. The company reported net income of roughly $163 million, resulting in a net margin of nearly 5.7%. While growth has slowed, the business continues to generate steady earnings from its large base of active buyers.

As of Etsy's December 2025 balance sheet, the debt-to-equity ratio is approximately -2.8, meaning total liabilities exceed shareholder equity. The current ratio, indicating the ability to cover short-term bills with liquid assets, is close to 1.4. Free cash flow for the year was nearly $638.8 million. Note that stock-based compensation represented roughly 35.3% of operating cash flow.

Chewy faces intense competition from massive e-commerce platforms and physical retailers, including Amazon (AMZN +0.71%), which can often compete on price and delivery speed. The company is also heavily dependent on third-party shipping and fulfillment infrastructure to get products to pet parents quickly. Any disruption in these logistics networks or rising shipping costs could hurt its ability to maintain service levels or profitability.

Etsy is particularly vulnerable to shifts in discretionary consumer spending, as many of the unique items sold on its platform are not essential goods. The company also faces pressure from other retail stocks and marketplaces like eBay (EBAY -0.22%) that compete for the attention of creative entrepreneurs. Furthermore, maintaining trust and safety standards across millions of independent sellers remains a constant challenge and a potential regulatory risk.

Chewy offers a much lower P/S ratio, but Etsy looks more attractive when looking at forward P/E based on future earnings estimates.

Valuation metrics include sourcing from Financial Modeling Prep (FMP) and may differ from other data providers.

This is a tough call. Chewy is reporting slightly better revenue growth, but Etsy has a somewhat stronger net margin. However, given Etsy's asset-light model -- it's basically just a middleman -- a net margin in the single digits is underwhelming. In fact, it's barely grown at all over the past three years:

Obviously, as a business matures, it often becomes harder to post huge growth because the comps are simply more difficult. So that is something I will concede.

I think the bigger issue for both of these stocks is their performance. Consider this chart:

Since Chewy went public in June 2019, both the pet supply specialist and Etsy have been basically dead money. Neither stock has kept pace with inflation. Sure, you're not sitting on a loss technically, but that's small comfort when you could have stuck your money in an S&P 500 ETF and been up over 163% by now -- even without reinvesting dividends.

Past performance is no indication of future results, but I don't find either stock attractive. I'd sit this one out.

#3077. Alphabet's Cloud Business Doubled, but the Stock Plunged. Here's What a $5,000 Investment Could Be Worth by 2030.

Alphabet is spending big to build out its cloud computing platform.

Alphabet (GOOG +0.53%) (GOOGL +0.52%) reported third-quarter earnings on Oct. 28. Since then, the stock sold off and hasn't recovered. It was down more than 10% since reporting earnings, which is no small amount considering that Alphabet is one of the world's largest companies.

However, Alphabet's sell-off was more focused on its spending rather than its growth. I think this is a very short-sighted view by the market, and it's why Alphabet stock should be considered a strong buy right now.

If the company keeps up the pace, a $5,000 investment could be worth $11,000 by 2030. Here's how.

Alphabet's business was built on Google Search. However, a new segment is starting to lead the company: cloud computing. Google Cloud is one of the largest cloud computing firms in the world and could start closing the gap, due to how fast it's growing.

In Q3, its revenue rose 103% year over year to $30.7 billion. Amazon Web Services (AWS) is the industry leader and generated $46 billion in Q3, growing at 39%. So, Google Cloud has a chance to start catching up to Amazon over the next year, but not all of its revenue is recurring.

For the most part, cloud computing revenue can be recognized as recurring, as clients pay for usage, which is generally fairly recurring revenue. However, Alphabet is starting to sell its proprietary artificial intelligence computing chips to certain firms, which is boosting its revenue.

Unfortunately, Alphabet doesn't provide investors with Tensor Processing Unit sales figures, so it's impossible to parse how much revenue is from chip sales and how much is from a more recurring revenue stream. The best clue investors have is that Alphabet stated that cloud revenue makes up the "vast majority" of revenue and that the growth rate, excluding TPU sales, was "incredibly strong." However, 2027 will include far more chip sales, which could affect the 2027 revenue guidance investors may see during Q4.

However, the legacy business wasn't the greatest for Alphabet. Google Search had an OK quarter, with revenue growing 12% year over year. YouTube didn't fare any better, with revenue rising 9% year over year. But Alphabet's total revenue rose at a 25% year-over-year rate, thanks to the strength of Google Cloud, which now makes up nearly a quarter of its total business.

But where will that take the stock by 2030? Let's take a look.

Many in the cloud computing industry project several years of strong growth driven by rising AI workloads. Google Cloud's peers are growing at a 40% rate, but with Google Cloud's TPUs becoming a popular alternative to GPUs for running workloads, I think it will grow faster than its peers, growing at a 50% rate. This will help boost Alphabet's overall growth rate, and a 20% companywide growth rate from now until 2030 isn't out of the question. If Alphabet does that, it could have trailing 12-month revenue totals of just over $1 trillion.

Trillion-dollar revenue companies are quickly approaching, and Alphabet will be among the first to reach this mark. If it can deliver a 30% profit margin and trade for 25 times earnings, that would value the stock at $7.7 trillion. Alphabet currently has a $3.5 trillion valuation, so this would transform a $5,000 investment today into about $11,000.

That's a great, market-beating investment, and investors should use today's sale price as a smart buying opportunity for Alphabet stock.

TL;DR: Rates fell today, but not by enough to change your mortgage math.

Mortgage rates are a little lower today. It probably won't last, thanks to new inflation data that has investors even more confident that the Federal Reserve will hike rates next month. You can read more on that below the charts.

Today, the average interest rate on a 30-year, fixed-rate mortgage ticked down to 6.9% APR, according to rates provided to NerdWallet by Zillow. This is eight basis points lower than yesterday and 17 basis points lower than a week ago. (A basis point is one one-hundredth of a percentage point.)

Here are the average APRs we're seeing today, as well as movement over the past month for context:

» Take the next step: Compare mortgage rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

Mortgage rates are constantly changing, since a major part of how rates are set depends on reactions to new inflation reports, job numbers, Fed meetings, global news ... you name it. For example, even tiny changes in the bond market can shift mortgage pricing.

This morning the Bureau of Labor Statistics (BLS) dropped the October Consumer Price Index, which shows that inflation rose another 0.4% last month. This outpaced expectations: Economists had forecasted an increase of 0.3%. CPI is one of two key measures of inflation, which the Federal Reserve aims to keep in check relative to the strength of the labor market. (The Fed looks more closely at a different inflation measure, the Personal Consumption Expenditures Index, which comes out Nov. 25.)

Raising the federal funds rate is the Fed’s main tool for bringing inflation down, and futures traders are banking on a rate hike when the committee meets Dec. 8-9. The Fed doesn't set mortgage rates directly, but an anticipated rate hike can put upward pressure on mortgage rates, which respond to investors’ expectations for the economy and Fed policy.

Central bankers raised the federal funds rate in September, but it hasn’t been enough to pull inflation closer to the Fed’s target of 2%. Investors aren’t sure that one more hike in December will do the trick, either. According to CME FedWatch, further rate hikes may be in store for January.

» MORE: How the Federal Reserve affects mortgage rates

Refinancing might make sense if today’s rates are at least 0.5 to 0.75 of a percentage point lower than your current rate (and if you plan to stay in your home long enough to break even on closing costs).

With rates where they are right now, you may want to start considering a refi if your current rate is around 7.4% or higher.

Also consider your goals: Are you trying to lower your monthly payment, shorten your loan term or turn home equity into cash? For example, you might be more comfortable with paying a higher rate for a cash-out refinance than you would for a rate-and-term refinance, so long as the overall costs are lower than if you kept your original mortgage and added a HELOC or home equity loan.

If you're looking for a lower rate, use NerdWallet's refinance calculator to estimate savings and understand how long it would take to break even on the costs of refinancing.

» Time to refi? Compare refinance rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

There is no universal “right” time to start shopping — what matters is whether you can comfortably afford a mortgage now at today’s rates.

If the answer is yes, don’t get too hung up on whether you could be missing out on lower rates later; you can refinance down the road. Focus on getting preapproved, comparing lender offers, and understanding what monthly payment works for your budget.

NerdWallet’s affordability calculator can help you estimate your potential monthly payment. If a new home isn’t in the cards right now, there are still things you can do to strengthen your buyer profile. Take this time to pay down existing debts and build your down payment savings. Not only will this free up more cash flow for a future mortgage payment, it can also get you a better interest rate when you’re ready to buy.

» Is now a good time to buy? See NerdWallet’s analysis

If you already have a quote you’re happy with, you should consider locking your mortgage rate, especially if your lender offers a float-down option. A float-down lets you take advantage of a better rate if the market drops during your lock period.

Rate locks protect you from increases while your loan is processed, and with the market forever bouncing around, that peace of mind can be worth it.

🤓 Nerdy Reminder: Rates can change daily, and even hourly. If you’re happy with the deal you have, it’s okay to commit.

#3075. Not Starlink. Not Starship. This Is the Real Reason SpaceX Stock Could Soar Before 2027 Arrives.

Space Exploration Technologies' AI revenue is accelerating rapidly.

Space Exploration Technologies (SPCX +4.91%), better known as SpaceX, has had an interesting year on the public markets. While its stock price spiked at its public market debut to more than $200 per share, it promptly crashed to nearly $100 per share. Now, it trades around $140 per share, which is slightly less than the price at which investors first bought shares on its IPO day.

However, I think now could be a smart time to buy. Why? It's all about a business that not many investors appreciated (including myself) during its IPO.

While its Starlink business got a lot of headlines for being profitable and growing quickly, and Starship Technologies is cool, what will really drive the stock is its AI business. If the market flips its stance on AI investing, the stock could soar, and investors must be prepared.

SpaceX acquired xAI shortly before going public. xAI operates its own AI lab, which makes the Grok family of large language models. It also owns X, formerly known as Twitter, but that's just a minor subset of the business.

Just like other AI firms, SpaceX has seen its revenue explode as many clients adopt AI models and agents for a growing number of tasks. While it may seem like AI is doing a lot right now, we're just at a starting point for full-on AI adoption. That means there could be huge growth ahead, and SpaceX is counting on it.

The company is already seeing unbelievable revenue growth, as Super Intelligence (SI, SpaceX's AI division's official name) grew at a breathtaking 830% year-over-year pace to $7.56 billion. With SpaceX generating $14.6 billion in total revenue during Q3, xAI now accounts for over half of the business's revenue. So, you could make the case that this is really an AI company with space aspirations, although that's not the direction the company is heading over the long term.

CEO Elon Musk sees huge potential in AI, so he's pivoting the company there in the short term to build a larger business that can help fuel the space exploration side of the business when the time is right. Musk is very bullish on the prospects of the AI business, and he believes that by the end of 2027, annual recurring revenue could reach between $500 billion and $1 trillion.

Now, anyone who has followed Musk's projections knows that he sets massive long-term targets that are rarely reached. However, he's normally right about the direction that industry is headed, so hitting this level in a couple of years may not be out of the question.

However, Musk wouldn't be making this projection if he didn't see huge growth in the near future, and that could be all the stock needs to rise before 2027 arrives.

There isn't really a debate about how fast SpaceX's AI business is growing. Rather, the debate is about how much companies should spend. With the market bearish on several AI firms due to heavy spending, the answer is pretty clear: They're spending too much right now. As a result, AI-related stocks aren't doing well.

If that trend persists to the end of 2026 and throughout 2027, SpaceX's stock could struggle. However, if that flips, SpaceX is one of the best ways to invest in the AI build-out.

I think the market will flip its stance soon, and that could lead to a huge rally by the end of the year. But even if it doesn't, SpaceX is increasingly looking like a great long-term investment due to the strength of its AI business.

#3074. Car Insurance Soars for Drivers With Poor Credit; Here’s What to Do

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

So how do we make money? Our partners compensate us for advertisements that appear on our site. This compensation helps us provide tools and services - like free credit score access and monitoring. With the exception of mortgage, home equity and other home-lending products or services, partner compensation is one of several factors that may affect which products we highlight and where they appear on our site. Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

While building your credit, shop around for auto insurance quotes. Some insurers are more lenient than others.

Lead Writer & Licensed Insurance Specialist

Your driving record may be spotless, but if you have poor credit, car insurance companies view you as a risk — and they’re significantly raising your rates.

Over the past three years, drivers with poor credit have seen auto insurance premiums surge 37.7%, according to NerdWallet’s 2026 Auto Insurance Rates Report. These price hikes were even steeper than those faced by drivers with good credit who caused a crash or got a DUI.

In January 2023, someone with poor credit and a clean driving record would pay a median of $2,864 per year for full-coverage car insurance, NerdWallet’s analysis found. By January 2026, premiums for this driver had risen to $3,944 — an increase of $1,081 per year. By comparison, the same driver with good credit saw an increase of only $576.

“Credit scores are one of the most harmful things that results in you paying higher auto insurance premiums,” says Michael DeLong, a research and advocacy associate with the Washington, D.C.-based nonprofit Consumer Federation of America. “The penalties are extremely severe.”

While higher premiums after a wreck or DUI may not be a surprise, DeLong says many drivers aren’t aware their credit scores will affect their car insurance rates. To further complicate things, building credit can take months.

But there are ways to get relief more quickly. DeLong suggests comparing car insurance quotes from multiple companies, because some insurers penalize poor credit less than others. Raising your deductible, evaluating your coverage and avoiding small claims can also help.

NerdWallet’s analysis found that insurers raised rates more sharply from 2023 to 2026 for drivers with poor credit than for those with good credit — even if the poor-credit driver had a flawless record while the good-credit driver had a DUI, speeding ticket or at-fault crash.

When our sample driver with poor credit also had a recent DUI or speeding ticket, rate increases were even greater. Prices for a full-coverage policy went up by more than 40% for drivers with poor credit and a recent DUI, the biggest increase of any profile we analyzed.

Drivers often think it’s unfair for credit to affect car insurance prices, DeLong says, since it isn’t directly linked to driving behavior. Some states agree; California, Hawaii and Massachusetts don’t allow credit as a factor in setting auto insurance rates.

However, insurers say credit scores can indicate the likelihood that a driver will file a claim.[1]National Association of Insurance Commissioners. Credit-Based Insurance Scores. Accessed Nov 2, 2026. View all sources They use what’s called a credit-based insurance score — similar to the typical credit scores used by lenders but weighted differently.

“It helps in predicting expected claims activity and loss costs more accurately,” said Norman Niami, a member of the American Academy of Actuaries' Automobile Insurance Committee, in an email.

The practice has been controversial, with critics arguing it disproportionately hurts low-income and minority drivers. But a Federal Trade Commission report to Congress in July 2007 concluded that credit-based insurance scores were strong predictors of risk even when controlled for income, race and ethnicity.[2]A Report to Congress by the Federal Trade Commission. Credit-Based Insurance Scores: Impacts on Consumers of Automobile Insurance. Accessed Nov 2, 2026. View all sources

The explanation may be that some people simply tend to take risks, according to a statistical analysis published in the North American Actuarial Journal in 2016.[3]North American Actuarial Journal. Empirical Evidence on the Use of Credit Scoring for Predicting Insurance Losses with Psycho-social and Biochemical Explanations. Accessed Nov 2, 2026. View all sources Researchers concluded that risk-taking behavior can affect credit scores as well as insurance claims. Their analysis found that average losses per policy were 64.6% higher for drivers with the lowest credit scores than for those with the best credit.

Working to build your credit over time may help lower your car insurance premiums, but it’s not a sure thing. A credit-based insurance score is just one of many factors (along with things like location, age and driving history) that insurers use to rate a person’s risk.

Building credit “can produce a noticeable premium decrease in some cases, while producing little or no change in others,” Niami said, depending on whether the new score causes an insurer to move you into a better rating tier. Each insurer sets its own score thresholds, so a score viewed as “poor” by one insurer might be considered “fair” by another.

At the same time, consumer advocates argue it’s difficult for many people to take the steps needed to strengthen their credit scores.

“Most people probably have some idea that you can improve your credit score by trying to pay your bills regularly and reliably, and using different kinds of financial resources and credit cards,” DeLong says.

“But again, that’s easier said than done," he says. "If you’re struggling at a low-wage job and trying to juggle bills, or if you don’t easily have access to a credit card, that advice may not be especially helpful."

Drivers with poor credit or no credit may be able to take other steps to lower their premiums.

Comparing car insurance quotes from different companies is one of the best ways to ensure you have the cheapest rate possible. Insurers set their own pricing formulas, and some are more forgiving of poor credit than others. A NerdWallet analysis of October 2026 rates found that in about half (49.3%) of U.S. ZIP codes in states that allow credit scoring, the cheapest insurer for a driver with poor credit was a different company than the cheapest for a driver with good credit.

Although you can’t improve your risk profile overnight, “there’s a bunch of small things you can do that over a two- to three-year span make you look a lot better” to insurers, says Bradley Flowers, founder of Portal Insurance agency in Mobile, Alabama.

Consider a higher deductible. Raising your car insurance deductible — the amount of a future claim that you agree to cover yourself — lowers your premium and signals to insurers that you’re less likely to file a claim. But be sure you can afford to pay the higher deductible if you have to make a claim.

Adjust how you’re billed by insurers. By signing up for automatic payments and ensuring you don’t miss insurance payments each month, you’ll get a small discount while improving your risk profile for insurers.

Avoid minor claims if you can. Flowers recommends thinking twice about filing minor claims, such as windshield cracks. “When you’re in one of these situations, let’s save our insurance for the really bad stuff,” he suggests.

Lastly, be sure your coverage fits your vehicle. If you could afford to replace your car on your own, consider dropping collision and comprehensive coverage to bring your premiums down. Your trimmed-down policy would still provide you with liability coverage after a crash, while leaving you to cover damage to your vehicle.

NerdWallet's 2026 Auto Insurance Rates Report is based on an analysis of more than 1.4 billion rate estimates provided by Quadrant Information Services for all 50 states and Washington, D.C., from January 2023 to January 2026.

Quadrant derives its rate data from modeled estimates based on carrier rating algorithms; figures do not reflect every available carrier or every consumer's individual rate. Liberty Mutual does not participate in Quadrant's rate data and is not represented in this analysis.

Quadrant’s figures are modeled rate estimates produced by running a fixed driver profile through carrier rating algorithms. They represent both new business and renewal rate estimates, and they are not a record of what actual drivers paid.

NerdWallet anchored its analysis to a 3-year old Toyota Camry LE, ensuring each analysis period reflected rates for the same-aged car.

The primary rate analysis is based on a 35-year-old single driver with good credit, no recent at-fault accidents or driving violations, and a full-coverage policy with $1,000 deductibles for collision and comprehensive coverage. Rates are pooled across male and female drivers.

In states where drivers must carry other types of insurance not included in our full coverage profile specifications, we added the minimum amount of coverage required by those states. Some policies include additional coverage at the insurer’s discretion.

In addition to our base profile driver with good credit and a spotless driving history, we analyzed rates for the following driver profiles:

Drivers with an at-fault crash. We added a single at-fault crash causing $10,000 in property damage.

Drivers with a speeding ticket. We added a single speeding ticket for driving 16 mph over the speed limit.

Drivers with a DUI. We added a single drunken-driving violation.

Drivers with poor credit. This is the same as our regular base profile, except the driver has poor credit instead of good credit. We also analyzed rates for drivers with poor credit and each of the three incidents above.

NerdWallet applied a strict comparability filter before calculating year-over-year figures. Only coverage variants and carrier/state combinations present in all four years of data were included in median calculations. This ensures reported changes reflect genuine pricing shifts among stable carriers, rather than differences caused by insurers entering markets, exiting, or going insolvent during the analysis period.

#3073. Top Wall Street analysts suggest these 3 dividend stocks for consistent income

Inflation data, earnings reports and tensions in the Middle East have been influencing the market. In this volatile backdrop, investors seeking income can bolster their portfolios by adding dividend-paying stocks.

The recommendations of top Wall Street analysts can help inform investors in their search for attractive stocks from the vast universe of dividend-paying companies. These experts assign ratings after analyzing a company's financials and ability to consistently pay dividends while also seeking capital appreciation.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Integrated oil and gas giant Exxon Mobil has increased its dividends for 43 consecutive years. At a quarterly dividend of $1.03 per share (annualized dividend of $4.12), XOM stock offers a yield of 2.6%.

In his latest research report on energy players, Morgan Stanley analyst Devin McDermott reiterated a buy rating on Exxon Mobil stock and increased his price target to $177 from $168.

The analyst raised his free cash flow estimates to reflect Q2 guidance and the oil price strip, with the largest increase noted for integrated players due to higher refining margins. Notably, the five-star analyst raised his cash flow estimates by an average of 20% for the second half of 2026 and 7% for 2027.

McDermott highlighted that while pure-play refiners have rallied on stronger margins, integrated energy stocks have lagged, creating relative-value opportunities in major North American integrated producers, including Exxon Mobil, Suncor and Cenovus Energy.

Overall, McDermott is bullish on Exxon Mobil, as he expects the company to deliver strong earnings and cash flow growth, driven by robust upstream volumes, high-value projects in Product Solutions, and more than $5 billion of additional structural cost savings.

"XOM's large global integrated operations and strong balance sheet (0.0x 2027 net leverage) make it a clear defensive play in an uncertain macro backdrop," said McDermott.

McDermott ranks No. 726 among more than 12,490 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 59% of the time, delivering an average return of 9.80%. See Exxon Mobil Stock Buybacks on TipRanks.

Moving on to natural gas producer Expand Energy. The company announced a quarterly base dividend of $0.575 per share to be paid on Sept. 3. At an annualized dividend of $2.30 per share, EXE offers a yield of about 2.3%.

Recently, Goldman Sachs analyst Neil Mehta reaffirmed a buy rating on Expand Energy stock and increased his price target to $113 from $99. Mehta attributed his higher price target to cash flow improvement and a strong outlook for shareholder returns. He also cited the stock's attractive valuation, with an 11% free cash flow, or FCF, yield based on his 2027/2028 estimates compared with the peer average of 9%.

The five-star analyst expects Expand Energy to generate FCF per share of about $10 in FY28, up from the prior estimate of about $8 at his mid-cycle price forecast of $3.50/MMBtu Henry Hub. Mehta raised his FCF estimate to reflect an improved outlook for share repurchases, operational efficiencies, and gas pricing realizations.

Furthermore, the analyst expects the recently announced $1.25 billion Twin Eagle acquisition to enhance the competitive positioning of the combined business to expand to premium markets and capture incremental deals in the power and LNG end markets.

Mehta ranks No. 449 among more than 12,490 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 64% of the time, delivering an average return of 12.1%. See Expand Energy Insider Trading on TipRanks.

Mehta is also bullish on Diamondback Energy, an independent oil and natural gas company focused on energy reserves that are primarily in the Permian Basin in West Texas. The company recently paid a base cash dividend of $1.10 per share for the second quarter of 2026. FANG stock offers a dividend yield of 2.2%.

Mehta increased his price target on FANG stock to $220 from $212 and reiterated a buy rating, citing the company's capital-efficient volume growth as oil prices remain high amid persistent supply disruption in the Middle East.

"We remain constructive on FANG, a high-quality, pure-play Permian Basin operator that continues to drive incremental capital efficiencies and strong well productivity relative to peers," said Mehta.

Also, the analyst said that the removal of a minimum return of capital commitment gives Diamondback more flexibility in taking capital allocation decisions amid high oil price volatility and macro uncertainty.

Mehta added that Diamondback sees a favorable environment for production growth, driven by the potential need for global inventory restocking. He highlighted that during Q2 2026, FANG's production volumes of 1,018 Mboe/d exceeded the high end of the company's guidance, driven by a stronger-than-expected natural gas output from Barnett development and improved downstream gas marketing.

Overall, Mehta is optimistic about Diamondback's growth and sees more upside in the stock, given higher production estimates, solid productivity and a deepening position in the Barnett shale formation. See Diamondback Energy Financials on TipRanks.

#3072. Texas Wants to Rethink Data Center Construction. Here's the AI Stock Most Exposed.

Oracle's extensive presence in the Lone Star State puts it in the spotlight.

The recent push in Texas to pause data center construction has sparked concerns across the AI industry. This applies in particular to cloud companies, which have driven the rapid development of these facilities amid rising demand.

Texas Governor Greg Abbott had some state agencies audit data center requests beginning in August. In September, he ordered the Texas Commission on Environmental Quality to halt the issuance of final permits for data center projects. This moratorium effectively paused all new data center development until at least December.

The concerns that created these pauses aren't going away, and they were likely escalated after Gina Hinojosa's upset victory in the 2026 Texas gubernatorial election. The public sentiment around data centers is growing increasingly negative.

Most cloud companies have some business interests in Texas, but the most affected is likely to be Oracle (ORCL -2.82%). Fortunately, the current actions around data centers could create more of a buying opportunity than a risk to the stock.

The Oracle footprint in Texas is extensive.

The company chose areas around Abilene for its largest deployments, citing the region's capacity to generate wind and solar power. Moreover, it has placed capacity in the Dallas-Fort Worth area, as that is one of the world's most interconnected corridors for data centers. Furthermore, even though it plans to relocate its corporate headquarters from Austin to Nashville over the next few years, Austin remains its operational base for now and is a hub for hosting.

Fortunately, despite Oracle stock being on pace for its worst year since 2001, Oracle investors may not need to panic. For one, Hinojosa proposed a moratorium on new data centers, which does not apply to current facilities or those under construction. This gives the company the opportunity to build more outside of Texas if necessary.

Secondly, the moratorium would presumably remain in effect only until new regulations are in place. Hence, the change in leadership may only have minimal effects on Oracle's data center plans within the Lone Star State.

Oracle may also be in better financial shape than the many bears fear. Admittedly, the data center construction boom is the main factor in its more than $125 billion in total debt, a level well above the company's $67 billion book value.

Still, in the first quarter of fiscal 2027 (ended Aug. 31), revenue of $19 billion increased by 30% from the same quarter last year. Most of that growth came from the cloud segment, which accounts for 60% of total revenue.

Nonetheless, the other 40% consists of its legacy businesses. Should the cloud segment struggle, these other enterprises could provide Oracle with stability. This gives Oracle stock an advantage over a company like CoreWeave, which is totally dependent on the cloud for its revenue.

Moreover, investors should note that Oracle's fiscal Q1 net income of nearly $4.8 billion rose 63% from the year-ago period. Since that figure also includes $1.4 billion in interest expenses, Oracle can presumably afford to service its loans.

Amid debt concerns, the cloud stock now trades near 52-week lows. However, its 20 P/E ratio is close to its lowest valuation since the pandemic. That could make the stock worth buying despite the risks of its Texas exposure.

Although Oracle has heavy exposure to Texas, it appears well-positioned to manage the state's political shift.

Hinojosa's proposed moratorium on data center construction would only apply to future projects, leaving Oracle's current Texas operations unaffected.

Additionally, Oracle benefits from rapid expansion, bolstered by well-established legacy businesses, providing both growth and stability. When considering its discounted stock price and ability to service its considerable debt load, Oracle investors probably do not need to worry about Texas's political shift.

#3069. Breakfast News: What’s Nvidia’s Biggest Risk?

Nvidia falls as board meets to discuss Huang, AMD and Broadcom gain in pre-market, and more…

Nvidia (NVDA -9.82%) fell over 6% in pre-market trading after the WSJ reported that the board is preparing to oust co-founder and CEO Jensen Huang. Bloomberg Law further reported that the board is working with an outside firm to find successors and is weighing a temporary chief.

Nvidia's artificial intelligence (AI) dominance hasn't suddenly disappeared, but its key-man risk is real. The Nov. 17 quarterly earnings report and conference call may be among the most closely watched in the company’s history.

During a special Fool24 session last night for members, Team Rule Breakers analyst Dan Caplinger compared the situation at Nvidia to a familiar case, and added a warning.

Think back to when Steve Jobs handed Apple (AAPL +0.43%) to Tim Cook. It looks like an easy call now, but it didn’t feel that way at the time. “Don’t forget just how uncertain things were at the time,” Dan reminded Fools.

Nvidia may come through this in good shape. Even so, shareholders will probably face months of open questions. Investors who can live with that uncertainty and stay focused on the business will be in a better position to make clear decisions, whichever way this goes.

Nvidia shares fell, but Advanced Micro Devices (AMD +1.58%) rose more than 4% after hours Wednesday while Broadcom (AVGO +3.66%) gained over 2%. Investors are already weighing who could benefit if Nvidia loses its longtime leader. Both stocks are recommended by Team Hidden Gems and Team Rule Breakers.

Nvidia may have a Jensen problem, but its rivals could suddenly be looking at a bigger market opportunity.

Team Rule BreakersSay you’ve set aside $3,000 for a stock. Split it into three $1,000 pieces. Buy the first today.

Buy the second on the same date next month or next quarter, at whatever price it trades. Buy the third one period later, on the same terms.

Because the next move can’t be reliably predicted, the method doesn’t ask you to try. The calendar decides when you buy.

If prices rise, part of your money is already working. If they fall, your remaining thirds will likely buy cheaper and bring down the average you paid. Motley Fool co-founder David Gardner calls the approach “an incredibly effective win-win way to get invested in any volatile stock that’s trading high.”

We think buying in thirds is worth its small cost when two things are true. You believe the company will be worth meaningfully more three or more years from now. Without a plan, you’d keep waiting or put everything in at once on a hunch.

What’s a stock you stuck with through the doubt – and were glad you did?

#3067. What The First 48 Hours After An AI Failure Says About Your Governance

​You log on to your computer for the workday and see an AI agent with access to your CRM send a message to your entire customer list. It’s not a phishing attempt. The agent is doing what it was authorized to do but with the wrong list attached and the wrong offer inserted. The clock starts ticking the moment the first customer replies.

Now ask yourself: What did the agent do, which of those actions are reversible, who can stop it mid-workflow and what has to be disclosed, to whom?

Every organization with a crisis plan knows what happens in the first 48 hours of a cybersecurity incident. There’s typically a named incident commander, a crisis team and a call tree that reaches legal and comms. But ask that same group what happens after an AI agent does something it shouldn’t have, and the next steps become more uncertain. There isn’t an industry equivalent to follow in this situation.

Agent logging wasn’t designed to reconstruct a decision chain the way security logging was, making it harder to answer, after the fact, what the agent did and why. This failure isn’t an edge case; it’s the default state most in which organizations already operate.

What I described is not a hypothetical risk sitting somewhere in the future. Grant Thornton’s 2026 AI Impact Survey found that nearly three in four organizations are giving agentic AI access to their data and processes, whether piloting, scaling or running autonomous AI. Just 20% have a tested AI incident response plan for when it fails. The gap between adoption and readiness is apparent, leaving most of the enterprise operating without a plan for the day an agent does something it wasn’t supposed to.

The same survey also revealed that 54% of COOs are concerned about regulatory and compliance uncertainty related to agentic AI, compared with just 20% of CIOs and CTOs. Those running operations and those deploying the technology are not aligned on where the risk is. When builders and operators don’t share the same risk sentiment, that readiness gap between them sits there, latent—until an incident forces the question. ​

A plan on paper isn’t the same as true readiness. Sygnia’s 2026 CISO survey found that 73% of security leaders say their organization would not be fully ready to execute their incident response plan under pressure, despite 99% having one on the books. Cybersecurity has decades of regulatory pressure behind it and still has this gap. AI incident readiness doesn’t have the same maturity to rely on, and the technology is moving faster than the governance. The gap between deploying agentic AI and being able to respond when it fails isn’t shrinking anytime soon, which further compounds the financial and reputational cost of unpreparedness.

The lack of readiness often comes down to structural misalignment. A new model deployment produces a demo. A governance framework produces a slide. An incident response plan for AI doesn’t produce value until the day you need it. That’s the trap tech leaders fall into.

Most AI governance conversations right now still focus on the front end: what agents are allowed to do, which use cases get approved and how permissions get scoped before deployment. This work is important but treats governance as a gate you build once and walk through. AI incident readiness is different since it’s based on the assumption that something will eventually get through the gate anyway and asks whether your organization can handle the fallout when it happens.

That’s a harder conversation to have, because it means admitting in advance that the AI you’re deploying will, at some point, act beyond your intention. Most leadership teams would rather debate the next use case than plan for this. But the organizations willing to are building something durable. They’re assigning decision rights before they’re needed, designing logs to be read by a stakeholder under pressure rather than an engineer debugging a model and establishing a disclosure threshold in advance.

None of this is exclusively a technology problem. The leadership decisions about who owns the moment when AI is wrong are equally relevant. And ownership isn’t a straight line. Responsibility is split across the user, the agent interface, the model provider and the system on which the agent acts. Engineers and technical teams are no longer the only ones on the hook. AI is touching more enterprise data with each deployment, which means operations, legal and marketing all have to be in the room when something goes wrong, not just IT.

Sygnia’s 2026 CISO survey also lists difficulty coordinating key stakeholders as a top challenge in executing incident response, cited by 90% of respondents. You can test that within a day. Pull five people in a room, including operations, legal and marketing, run the scenario, and time how long it takes to answer the questions from the top of this piece. If the room can’t answer within the hour, you’ve found the gap for the cost of an afternoon.
