#3064. Why Engineering Productivity Metrics Keep Misleading CTOs

Elton Chan is the Co-Founder of Second Talent, a solution that connects global tech leaders with AI-native engineering talent across Asia.

​Engineering teams have more ways to measure developer activity than ever. CTOs can track pull requests, commits, deployment frequency, review times and AI tool usage.

But a busy dashboard can still leave one question unanswered: Is the team delivering better software, or producing more work that someone else has to process?​

I’ve seen how AI coding tools can help engineers produce a first version faster, but the time saved during implementation can get absorbed by code reviews, test updates and integration issues. Even if the team appears more productive when measured by code output, the time it takes to move a change from development to a reliable production release often hasn’t improved.

To solve this, engineering leaders need to measure the entire delivery process, not just the speed of individual tasks.

​A team can write more code and still ship features at the same pace. Developers might open twice as many pull requests, only for reviewers to spend more time checking them. More code enters the pipeline, but the team still has to test it, integrate it and deal with whatever breaks.

The 2025 DORA report found AI adoption is associated with higher software delivery throughput, while also finding a negative relationship with delivery stability.

For a CTO, the practical question is what happens between writing code and getting a change safely into production. If implementation speeds up but testing, review or integration falls behind, the team may be generating work faster without delivering proportionally more value.

Lines of code, commit counts and tickets completed are easy to count. They are also easy to misread.

A developer who splits one change into 10 commits can look more active than someone who delivers the same change in two. A team can close more tickets by breaking work into smaller pieces without improving its delivery rate.

The SPACE framework offers a broader approach, looking at satisfaction and well-being, performance, activity, communication and collaboration, and efficiency and flow. Its central point is straightforward: No single metric captures developer productivity.

CTOs do not need to measure everything in the framework, but they should avoid drawing conclusions from one number in isolation. A rise in completed tickets means little without knowing whether the work reached users, met quality expectations and reduced the problems the team was hired to solve.

A pull request waiting three days for review is a different problem from a feature waiting three days for a product decision. A test environment that fails unpredictably creates a different delay from code that repeatedly needs rework.

Yet a dashboard showing that delivery has slowed down may not tell a CTO which of these problems is responsible.

Look at where work waits and where it has to be repeated. Review wait times can expose a shortage of reviewer capacity, while rework and failed changes can point to testing or quality problems. Long lead times between a task starting and reaching production can reveal delays across several teams.

The response should follow the evidence. Hiring more engineers will not clear a review queue if nobody has time to review. Adding another testing tool will not fix a team that receives changing requirements halfway through implementation.

AI tools can shorten implementation time, but that saving needs to be assessed alongside the work that follows. If developers spend less time writing code and more time checking generated output, resolving integration problems or fixing regressions, measuring coding speed alone gives CTOs an incomplete picture.

The 2025 DORA research finding that AI adoption is associated with higher throughput but also with lower delivery stability offers a useful starting point. Leaders evaluating their own AI investment need to examine both sides rather than relying on adoption rates or developers’ reported time savings.

To do so, compare delivery before and after introducing a tool. Look at how long changes take to reach production, how often they cause problems, how much rework they generate and whether the team is delivering outcomes that users need.

Then, ask engineers where the time saved has gone. Their answers can help explain changes in the metrics.

If implementation time falls while review queues and rework grow, the next investment may need to be in testing, review capacity or the development workflow rather than another AI tool.

A useful engineering dashboard should help leaders understand how work moves from a business need to a reliable release. A small set of measures can cover delivery speed, change failure rate, review delays, rework and the outcome the work was meant to achieve.

These measures should be read together and at the level where teams can act on them. They are useful for finding constraints and deciding where to invest. They are much less useful as a league table ranking individual engineers by commits, tickets or lines of code.

Instead of trying to make a dashboard look more productive, understand whether the engineering organization is delivering better software, where work is getting stuck and what needs to change.​​

#3063. Apple Is Hovering Around an All-Time High Despite the Broader Tech Sell-Off. Will New Products Like the iPhone Duo and HomeView Give Apple Even More Room to Run?

The innovation pipeline is robust under new CEO John Ternus.

The last few weeks have been difficult for technology investors. This is indicated by the 12% decline of the Vanguard Information Technology ETF since the start of October (as of Nov. 11).

Investors can blame the recent slide on Nvidia shares tanking. In addition to the chief executive officer's latest troubles, the market worries about the sustainability of the artificial intelligence (AI) trade. Even the smallest shift in sentiment can have a pronounced impact given how important these sorts of businesses are right now.

Amid the turmoil, Apple (AAPL +0.43%) looks like a winner. It's currently trading in record territory, with shares up 31% this year. The $5.2 trillion market capitalization easily makes it the world's most valuable company. It makes sense if investors view this as more of a consumer staples stock rather than a pure tech stock.

The business continues to operate as it always has, with fresh product releases a cornerstone of the strategy. The iPhone Duo and HomeView are the most notable launches. Will these innovations give Apple shares more room to run?

The stock's recent strength underscores its unique AI strategy. This business has not participated in the extraordinary infrastructure boom that's seeing its tech peers spend hundreds of billions of dollars on data centers, often raising external capital to do so. Capital outlays of this size naturally increase the financial risk investors must deal with.

And this puts the AI trade on fragile ground. The market wants rapid growth to continue for hyperscaler cloud platforms and the leading AI labs. No one can accurately predict what the industry will look like in two to three years, which is the problem that introduces immense uncertainty.

Apple is standing by itself. It collected $136 billion in free cash flow in fiscal 2026 (ended Sept. 26), thanks to just $9 billion in capital expenditures. Investors have always appreciated the financial strength this business operates with.

Even during a major technological wave that encourages heavy spending, its profitability has held up. Management's discipline is why certain Apple bulls might be comfortable calling this a safe-haven stock.

A premium brand defined by pricing power, customer loyalty, and an unmatched ecosystem make Apple an elite company. And the constant focus on innovation keeps consumers interested. But investors face a trade-off. In exchange for owning such a high-quality business, you give up the potential to capture outsize returns.

The valuation could be a headwind. Shares are at a forward price-to-earnings ratio of 36.4, which doesn't present investors with a margin of safety. You essentially have to believe the market will continue to assign a high multiple to the stock.

Growth will also naturally slow. According to sell-side analyst estimates, Apple's revenue and earnings per share are expected to increase by just 27% and 31%, respectively, over the next three years.

This is accounting for the impact new products will have. The iPhone Duo immediately saw incredible demand. But the consensus view is that it will only represent 7% of iPhone revenue this fiscal year.

And it's too early to draw any conclusions about HomeView. However, this will have a tiny near-term financial impact even if tens of millions of units are sold.

These could end up being extremely successful products. The issue is that Apple is so massive, with fiscal 2026 net sales coming in at $479 billion. It's virtually impossible to move the financial needle at this size.

This isn't to say that the stock can't continue its ascent. It's just important that investors set the right expectations if they do choose to buy Apple.

#3062. Top Wall Street analysts like these 3 stocks for the long haul

Global stock markets have been volatile as investors assess high bond yields and continued tensions between the U.S. and Iran. There are concerns about the impact of uncertainty in the Middle East on oil prices and consequently on inflation.

Investors who prefer to look beyond short-term noise and buy stocks with attractive long-term growth potential can follow the recommendations of top Wall Street analysts. The ratings of these experts are backed by in-depth research of a company's financials and thorough analysis of the factors affecting its business performance.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Semiconductor giant Nvidia (NVDA) reported stellar fiscal second-quarter results and reassured investors about continued strength in AI-driven demand for its chips. Moreover, Nvidia's FY28 revenue growth outlook of 70% was well above the Street's expectations.

Reacting to the Q2 print, Morgan Stanley analyst Joseph Moore reiterated a buy rating on NVDA and increased his price target to $300 from $288. "This is our Top Pick in the semis group, with a compelling product cycle, exceptional growth and valuation below peers," said the 5-star analyst.

Nvidia delivered impressive quarterly results despite supply constraints, Moore said, but he believes that the FY28 revenue and gross margin commentary mattered more, with a favorable outlook for both measures.

Moore said Nvidia's 70% revenue growth guidance for FY28 was above his own estimate of 52% and the consensus of about 40%. The top-line growth estimate was a "remarkable figure" given supply issues and Moore said he expects Nvidia to continue to address bottlenecks to deliver rapid growth.

Among other takeaways, the analyst said performance metrics for Vera Rubin chips were strong, with Nvidia emphasizing a 30x higher throughput per megawatt and 35x lower token cost compared with Grace Blackwell Ultra.

Moore ranks No. 159 among more than 12,490 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 60% of the time, delivering an average return of 24.60%. See Nvidia Statistics on TipRanks.

Ride-hailing and delivery platform Uber Technologies (UBER) was recommended by BMO Capital analyst Brian Pitz, who discussed the company's opportunity in the autonomous vehicle (AV) market in a recent research note that reiterated a buy rating and price target of $119.

The 5-star analyst highlighted Uber's transition into a diversified marketplace, with investors now focusing on its AV growth prospects. The key question is how much value can Uber generate as AVs become commercialized, Pitz asked.

"We continue to believe that Uber's AV strategy is evolving and will become a significant driver of revenue and profitability," the analyst said.

Importantly, Pitz believes that Uber can establish itself as a preferred mobility platform for AV makers, supported by its growing AV infrastructure capabilities and an expanding partner base. This would enable Uber to win a significant share of value as the AV market is commercialized.

Additionally, Pitz noted Uber's enhanced position in the AV value chain, with the company shifting from serving as a distribution channel for robotaxis to becoming a broader platform for AV mobility. The analyst discussed Uber's efforts to capture growth opportunities in the AV market, including higher capital spending, expansion into new markets and building partnerships beyond Alphabet's Waymo.

Pitz ranks No. 574 among more than 12,490 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 61% of the time, delivering an average return of about 12.20%. See Uber Technologies Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Semiconductor company Marvell Technology (MRVL) recently announced better-than-expected results for the second quarter of FY27. But the company's increased FY28 guidance fell short of investors' high expectations.

In reaction to the Q2 FY27 results, KeyBanc analyst John Vinh reaffirmed a buy rating on Marvell Technology, with a price target of $400. The analyst noted that Marvell's results and outlook were driven by strength in its data center business. The Q2 FY27 data center business grew 46% year-over-year, to $2.17 billion, far above KeyBanc's estimate of $2.09 billion.

Management "highlighted accelerating AI networking demand across scale-out, scale-across and scale-up architectures, supported by strong 800G optical DSP demand, rapid 1.6T ramp, broader 51.2T switch deployments and higher TIA/driver demand," Vinh said.

Consequently, Marvell raised its data center revenue guidance, now expecting 60% growth in FY27 and more than 60% in FY28.

Regarding the recently announced $120 billion warrant arrangement with Google, Vinh said management argued that programs tied to the warrant are already priced into the custom outlook through FY28. Meanwhile, new programs and those in execution are expected to drive significant upside to the previous FY29 custom XPU revenue estimate of over $10 billion, Vinh said, citing management.

#3061. Merchandising For Bots: The Critical Questions Retail Leaders Aren't Yet Asking

Zohar Gilad is cofounder and CEO of Fast Simon.

In a recent survey, 24% of shoppers said they would be comfortable letting an AI agent shop for them. While AI-driven traffic to online stores is still a small share of visits, it’s growing quickly.

These numbers will undoubtedly increase. So what happens when AI becomes the intermediary between a brand’s catalog and the shopper?

Because the technology is developing so rapidly, anyone claiming to have a definitive answer or playbook on merchandising for AI is suspect. The more useful exercise is to ask harder questions about what happens to product discovery when an AI system starts deciding which products a shopper sees.

Here are four issues that AI-savvy retailers should be thinking about now.

Traditional SEO taught marketers to compete for visibility inside a ranking system: Identify search intent, optimize pages, earn authority and improve your position.

Now, a new system has emerged: AI (or generative) engine optimization (AEO/GEO).

Unlike keywords, AI prompts are essentially infinite and personalized, and countless different prompts can be clustered around the exact same consumer intent. Consider these requests:

• Find me a breathable, floral dress for under $200.

• Give me a romantic, cottage-core dress appropriate for a guest at a summer wedding.

• I’m going to an outdoor wedding next month, and it’s going to be 85 degrees; what dress will look elevated but won’t show sweat?

Each prompt could lead AI to recommend the same product or three different ones. With open-ended prompts, there’s little predictability.

The answer isn’t to optimize for every possibility, but to make the underlying product information understandable to AI. Then it can connect a product to the many different ways a shopper might describe it (fabric, weight, seasonality, occasion, etc.).

That creates a new merchandising question: How do you structure your catalog to map to these infinite systems? And when the system deciding what gets surfaced is contextual and constantly changing, how do you reliably earn product visibility?

AI agents are well-suited for the shopping that consumers view as bothersome chores: groceries, household essentials and even the frustrating process of finding a holiday gift for Aunt Mathilda.

Conversely, shopping for high-consideration items, like a designer handbag, involves a desire to dream, be inspired and browse. Many shoppers actively enjoy this process and wouldn’t want to hand it off to an AI agent.

That prompts the question: Which parts of the shopping experience would a consumer actually want to delegate?

AI’s impact will likely be most immediate in the “chores” space. Merchandisers selling commodities should consider what happens when AI can compare products on price or availability without exposing the consumer to the brand experience.

Which parts of your catalog become commodities once an AI agent strips away the marketing and compares products side by side? Even for commoditized items, how will descriptions, origin countries and imagery influence the agent’s final selection?

The more important question might be what happens on the consumer’s end. If discovery is part of your value proposition, what does an AI agent remove from the brand experience?

Traditional search lets consumers scroll through and click on numerous options. But as AI search models try to catch up to human discovery habits, they distill endless results down to three to five recommendations.

When presented with AI’s narrowed choices, humans default to convenience, accepting what AI shares. If ChatGPT presents three options for the best running shoes, the shopper is unlikely to look elsewhere for a fourth option.

So how does a brand secure its place in the coveted golden set of results when AI is significantly limiting consumer choice?

In traditional search, a retailer competes for page-one visibility and relies on the shopper to browse. In AI search, the retailer may need to earn consideration before the shopper ever sees the product.

That could create a new competitive real estate, a small shelf of products that AI deemed worth showing. And if AI systems are increasingly determining which products matter, how will this narrowing of options alter market competition?

Agentic commerce is making it easier for AI systems to access structured product information, compare products and eventually complete transactions on a shopper’s behalf.

That creates an uncomfortable question for merchandisers: If AI controls the interface, what happens to the careful curation they’ve built into the catalog?

We’re already moving in this direction. For example, Shopify’s model context protocol (MCP) server displays a brand’s entire catalog to AI agents, leaving all the product discovery power in the hands of AI platforms.

In this instance, a retailer’s products may be more accessible to AI, but accessibility isn’t the same as merchandising.

Merchandising is about deciding which products to emphasize, how to group them and what story to tell around them. An AI agent can potentially bypass much of that curation and make its own decisions about what to show the shopper.

To exert more influence over the shopping process, brands may need to deploy their own AI to interface with the shopper’s AI.

But what will that look like? Will merchants use their own AI to negotiate with or even manipulate the consumer’s shopping agent? How can retailers use technology to restore their product discovery power?

The transition toward merchandising for bots is imminent, but a one-size-fits-all approach will fail.

The first question for retailers is what they’re willing to give up when AI becomes the intermediary between their catalog and the customer.

As AI shopper agents threaten to remove some of the merchandiser’s control, retailers should resist the urge to immediately rewrite their strategies based on unproven theories. The brands poised for success are those that are actively interrogating their catalog’s purpose, data structure and defensive posture against AI intermediaries.

#3056. Why AI Could Change The Way Utilities Modernize Their Customer Information Systems

Steve Dawson is the founder and CEO at POWERCONNECT.AI.

​For decades, electric, water and gas utilities have approached Customer Information System (CIS) modernization with a familiar strategy: When technology becomes outdated, replace it.

That decision can initiate lengthy procurement processes, complex data migrations, investments and years of implementation. Yet artificial intelligence (AI) offers another possibility: improving how employees and customers interact with enterprise systems without necessarily replacing the platforms responsible for billing millions of customers.

The next generation of CIS modernization may focus less on replacement and more on unlocking the value already embedded in utility technology.

Many utilities operate CIS platforms that have served them reliably for decades. These systems manage customer accounts, billing, payments, meter readings and service orders. They also contain rate structures, business rules and integrations that would be costly to replicate.

Nevertheless, customer service representatives often navigate multiple screens and applications to answer a question. The billing engine may work correctly while its user experience falls short of modern expectations. That distinction matters. Does a utility need an entirely new CIS or a better way to interact with the one it already has?

Application programming interfaces (APIs) let applications exchange information and perform authorized operations. A modern user interface can use APIs to retrieve billing histories, account balances, payment records, meter data and service requests, while the existing CIS remains the system of record.

The Model Context Protocol (MCP) adds another capability. This open standard enables AI applications to connect with external tools and data sources through a consistent interface. APIs supply underlying system functionality; MCP can expose approved capabilities to AI assistants and agents.

For example, a utility might offer tools for checking an account balance, reviewing consumption or retrieving service order status. An AI copilot could use those tools, subject to the employee’s permissions, without requiring a custom connection for every new AI experience.

MCP is not a replacement for APIs, and neither guarantees interoperability or security by itself. Utilities still need integration architecture, authentication, authorization, monitoring and clear ownership of each transaction.

Imagine a customer calling because a service transfer has not been completed. Today, a representative may need to open the CIS, locate a service order, check field activity and contact another department.

With a copilot embedded in a redesigned interface, the representative could ask: “What’s delaying this customer’s service transfer?”

The copilot could gather authorized information from connected systems, identify an outstanding inspection or pending work order and present the next step for review. It could also explain which source supports its answer, making the response easier to verify.

This approach can support billing inquiries, payment questions and high-consumption investigations. The objective is not to let AI invent answers or bypass established workflows. It is to reduce navigation and give employees useful context when needed.

Copilots generally help people interpret information and prepare actions. Agentic AI can go further by coordinating steps across systems. A governed agent might collect information needed for a service request, validate required fields, prepare an update and route it for approval. Connected through APIs or MCP tools, it could help bridge billing, payment and field operations applications.

But greater capability brings greater responsibility. High-impact actions should require approvals, with safeguards against incorrect data, unauthorized requests and unexpected behavior. Human oversight must match the operational consequences of the task.

Utilities handle sensitive customer information and operate critical services. Introducing AI should not mean copying entire customer databases into another platform.

Where architecture allows, an intelligent interface can request only the information necessary for an authorized task, leaving core records within existing systems. This may reduce duplication, although temporary processing, logging, vendors and model-provider retention policies still need careful review.

Security controls should include encryption, role-based access, audit trails, tool permissions, prompt-injection defenses and transaction validation. The same employee who cannot change an account in the CIS should not gain that power simply by asking an AI assistant.

A general-purpose chatbot may write fluent answers without understanding utility operations. Accurate billing explanations can depend on rate schedules, meter intervals, estimated reads, seasonal usage and local policies.

That is why AI must be grounded in authorized source data and utility-specific rules. Responses should distinguish verified facts from suggestions, and representatives should be able to inspect the evidence behind recommendations.

A polished answer is not necessarily a correct answer. For utilities, trust depends on traceability and operational accuracy.

Timing also matters. By the time an organization releases an RFP for a new CIS, leadership may already have determined that replacement is the answer. Alternative approaches can be harder to consider once requirements and budgets reflect that assumption.

Utilities should carefully assess API readiness, integration constraints, user experience, security and AI opportunities before deciding whether replacement is necessary. Some aging platforms genuinely lack vendor support, essential functionality or a viable security posture. Those systems may need replacement.

Others may support incremental improvements that preserve billing logic, minimize migration risk and provide value sooner. A disciplined assessment should compare both paths against outcomes, cost and maintainability.

The traditional CIS will likely remain central to accurate billing, financial transactions and customer records. What changes is the way people access those capabilities. Modern interfaces can simplify everyday work. APIs can connect systems. MCP can standardize access to approved AI tools. Copilots can explain information, while agentic workflows can coordinate controlled tasks.

Success should be measured by resolution times, accuracy, customer satisfaction, employee productivity and reliability, not by how much software gets replaced. Before asking, “When should we replace our CIS?” Utility leaders should ask, “What could we improve with systems we own?”

Modernization doesn’t begin not with replacement but rather with a more intelligent way to use what is already there.

#3055. $500 Invested in Boeing Today Could Be Worth This Much in 3 Years

Achieving production and certification milestones by 2029 could drive a 46% share price increase in the aerospace and defense giant's stock price.

If Boeing (BA -0.29%) hits a few milestones, the company could look much stronger in a few years, potentially driving a substantial rise in its share price. Here's what that could mean for investors dipping their toes into the market with a $500 investment in Boeing.

Management has set a medium-term target of $10 billion in free cash flow (FCF), and hitting it would show the company's returning to what might be called "normalized operations." In other words, it would mean ramping up production and deliveries of its narrow-body 737 MAX and its established wide-body 787, while certifying and making the first delivery of its new wide-body, the 777X.

Unfortunately, the company disappointed on that front recently by pushing back expectations for 737 MAX production, and the stock sold off accordingly. Still, I think there's reason to be optimistic based on Boeing management's new guidance.

CFO Jesus Malave outlined that the "building blocks of a $10 billion" in FCF "on a normalized basis" means a delivery rate of 52 a month on the 737 MAX, a delivery rate of 10 a month on the 787, and "a delivery rate and positive cash generation on a 777X program."

All those building blocks could fall into place and, in fact, be surpassed by 2029. First, management expects the 777X to receive certification, make its first delivery next year, and become cash-flow positive in 2029. Meanwhile, management expects to reach a production rate of 52 per month on the 737 MAX in the second half of 2027 and to transition to a production rate of 10 per month on the 787 this year.

While deliveries usually lag production by a few months, these operational targets suggest the "building blocks" of $10 billion in FCF could be in place by the end of 2027 -- implying Boeing could hit its FCF target earlier than in 2029.

As for valuation, $10 billion in FCF and a 20x price-to-FCF multiple imply a $200 billion market cap, or a 46% increase, meaning $500 invested now could be worth $730 in three years, with upside potential from a more aggressive ramp in 737 MAX production.

#3052. How To Do Better Influencer Marketing

Agung Dwi Sandi is the Founder and CEO of rankpillar Group, an expert marketing and leading digital marketing agency in Southeast Asia.

Influencer marketing has stopped being an experiment. According to the World Federation of Advertisers’ 2025 research, 99% of member brands work with influencers, 66% do so frequently and global creator partnership spend is expected to pass $39 billion this year. The discipline has professionalized quickly on the compliance side: 70% of brands say they always sign contracts, and 66% have a written influencer policy.

What has not professionalized at the same rate is what brands actually ask for.

I run a digital marketing agency in Southeast Asia. Across the region, a large paid-amplification economy has grown up. In Indonesia, it is commonly called the buzzer industry: loosely organized networks of social accounts, assembled by coordinators, that will post on a brand’s behalf for a fee priced per account, per post, per day.

Comparable structures exist in most high-engagement social markets. Indonesia’s is simply unusually visible and unusually cheap. Enforcement is now almost entirely automated: KOL.ID’s read of transparency data puts TikTok’s proactive removal rate in Indonesia at 99.3%.

The briefing conversations I have with brands buying this kind of amplification follow one script. How many accounts can you mobilize? How many posts a day? Can we be trending by Friday? What almost never comes first is the only question that decides whether any of it works: What should a specific person believe or do differently after they see this?

That gap is structural rather than ethical. Read what this part of the market charges for, and you have read its self-definition: accounts, posts, days and impressions. Nothing on that price list is an outcome.

Buyers are not innocent either. In the same WFA research, reach (94%) and engagement (89%) dominate success measurement, while only 23% of brands use conversions or sales. Marketing Dive reported this month that about two-thirds of marketers surveyed by the Association of National Advertisers call measurement the hardest part of influencer work.

When a buyer cannot measure an outcome, they buy what they can count. Volume is countable, so volume becomes the product.

There are four key changes that I believe will separate brands that buy influence from brands that buy noise.

A brief is not a document; it is a set of decisions: Who are we talking to, what do they currently believe, what do we need them to believe instead, what are we asking them to do and what evidence would we accept to show that it worked?

Hand a vendor 200 posts to place, and you have given them a logistics problem. Logistics firms are good at logistics. What they cannot do is tell you that your positioning is the thing that is broken. If the first thing you receive back is a rate card, you were never buying marketing.

The WFA’s own recommendation is to look past follower counts and look toward audience alignment and genuine product affinity.

In our creator work, candidates pass three gates before price is discussed: whether their audience actually sits in the geography the client sells in, whether their recent short-form content earns real view-through rather than borrowed spikes and whether the content would survive being placed next to the brand’s own. We source candidates through application-based creator hubs, where creators opt in to a campaign rather than being bulk-booked by a coordinator, which makes consent and fit inputs to the brief instead of afterthoughts.

Matching is slower and far harder to sell than mobilizing a hundred accounts. It is also the only part of the process that compounds.

The ANA’s guidance is to set an end goal, whether it’s sales, conversion or awareness, before launch. Go one step further and define the disproof. A credible answer involves a holdout group, a before-and-after measure or, at minimum, a number agreed upon in writing that nobody can reinterpret afterward.

Ask a prospective partner what they would accept as evidence the campaign failed. The quality of that answer tells you more than any case study.

The ANA recommends assigning a single internal group to manage creator relationships, and the WFA finds marketing departments leading this work in 54% of organizations.

The question to settle internally is simple: Who writes the brief? “Send us the brief, and we will execute” is a supplier’s answer. That may be exactly what you need, but do not then expect the result to be a strategy. Ask a partner, too, what they would remove from your plan. A labor vendor will add scope because scope is revenue. A partner will subtract whatever does not serve the objective.

Generative tools have collapsed the marginal cost of producing and distributing content with streamlined drafting, variation, translation, scheduling and just sheer volume. A business whose value proposition is the supply of execution labor is being repriced in real time, and a brand whose strategy is the purchase of execution labor is buying an input its competitors can now acquire for almost nothing.

What does not commoditize is judgment about an audience, a point of view worth amplifying and the instrumentation to know whether either worked. In the campaign data I see, the distance between paid volume and genuine attention has widened over the past two years, which makes a warehouse of accounts a depreciating asset rather than an unfair advantage.

The amplification economy did not grow because marketers in my market are careless. It grew because it answers, cheaply and instantly, a question that is easy to ask: How much noise can I buy?

The harder question has no rate card: What is the one thing people need to understand, and how will we know they understood it? Write that down before you brief anybody. If a vendor cannot argue with your brief, they cannot improve your marketing. They can only make it louder.

#3049. Why The AI Race Is Also A Chemistry Race

Dr. Ahmed Ismail, founder of Dunia Innovations and Aquature, builds AI-driven autonomous labs and technologies for energy and chemicals.

​Here is a trivia question for your next board meeting: Which company holds a near-monopoly on a material that sits inside the package of almost every advanced AI chip? If you said Nvidia or TSMC, you are in good company, but you are wrong. It is Ajinomoto, the Japanese company best known for MSG.

Ajinomoto Build-up Film, or ABF, insulates the dense wiring in the substrate that an AI processor is fixed on. Since a leading CPU maker adopted it in 1999, ABF has reportedly captured more than 95% of its market. In 2026, riding the AI boom, the electronic-materials business built around it reported operating margins above 50%.

I love this story because it exposes a blind spot. We talk about AI as models and parameters, yet every GPU is a stack of chemistry, engineered atom by atom for over 70 years, and that chemistry is fast becoming the hardest part of the race.

In the 1980s, chip technology was based on about 12 chemical elements. By the 2000s, it used more than 60 elements. A 2025 preprint estimates that the industry now relies on over 85% of all non-radioactive elements.​

Some of AI’s material problems, like ABF supply, are about scale, and capital may fix them. The harder problems are physics walls, where the materials we need do not exist yet.

These physical walls include wires, heat and space.​

As chips shrink, copper wiring starts to behave like a resistor. Below roughly 17 by 17 nanometers, ruthenium’s resistivity drops below copper’s.

In 2024, Nvidia’s thermal engineers reportedly saw two-phase direct-to-chip cooling as the preferred route for next-generation accelerators. Its classic fluids, however, belong to the PFAS family of forever chemicals. 3M completed its exit from PFAS manufacturing at the end of 2025. Someone has to invent the replacement.

On October 1, 2026, Google launched a prototype satellite carrying four of its AI chips to test how they handle radiation and the thermal extremes of orbit. With no air or water to carry heat away, space behaves like an insulator, and heat can only leave by radiation. Without better coatings to radiate that heat, an orbital data center will be a materials problem first before it solves a computing problem.

Nvidia has committed to what Jensen Huang calls a “one-year rhythm” for new AI chips. A new material, by contrast, commonly needs 10 to 20 years to go from discovery to deployment. So we may soon see a big mismatch between an industry moving at software speed that is building on a foundation that moves at chemistry speed.

The good news? The same technology hungry for new materials is finally good enough to help find them. Better materials make better chips, better chips train better models and better models can search the chemical space faster than any human team. That loop makes the AI race and the chemistry race one and the same.

In November 2025, the U.S. launched the Genesis Mission, a Department of Energy effort to link national-lab supercomputers and datasets with autonomous, AI-directed experiments. In July 2026, the White House said more than $5 billion in federal commitments were being made to expand it. The AI labs are moving, too. In December 2025, Google DeepMind said that it would open a UK lab in 2026, where robots could make and test hundreds of materials a day.

Frontier models have already read much of the internet’s text and code. The next great dataset has to come from the lab, failed experiments included. Governments and AI labs are betting that the most valuable data comes from touching the physical world.

In 2023, Google DeepMind’s GNoME model predicted 2.2 million new crystal structures. When two materials chemists at UC Santa Barbara examined a sample in 2024, they found “scant evidence for compounds that fulfill the trifecta of novelty, credibility, and utility.”

I know this simulation-to-reality gap firsthand. During my graduate research at synchrotron facilities in France, Germany, the U.S. and Japan, I watched catalysts at work under X-ray beams, and these materials rarely stayed the same during the chemical reaction. Inside the reactor, they kept reshaping themselves until they settled into a final form that was different from the original one. How could a computer simulation have predicted this?

A computer can propose a thousand candidates, but only a furnace, a reactor or an X-ray beam can tell you which candidate is real. Building an autonomous lab taught my team the same lesson: The scarcest and most valuable resource is the capacity to make and measure candidates quickly, under realistic conditions.

What does this mean for technology leaders today? Start with a few first steps:​

• Map your AI roadmap down to its materials. For every plan, ask what it physically needs. If the answer is a material that does not exist yet, it goes on your risk register, right next to your supply chain risks.

• Treat experimental data as a strategic asset, failures included. The failed experiments in lab notebooks are useful data, too. In a 2016 Nature study, a model trained on a research group’s archived lab notebooks, including failed reactions, recommended experiments that worked 89% of the time. Experienced chemists got 78%.

• Judge AI claims by the physical proof in the lab. When someone announces a million new materials, ask how many were actually made and how many were stable under realistic conditions. One million predicted candidates is just a list of hypotheses. A novel material that survives weeks inside a real reactor is what we could call the start of a materials breakthrough.

The take-home message is simple: AI’s next big step depends on materials that don’t exist yet. The leaders who plan for that now will be the first to benefit when those materials arrive.​

#3047. $1,000 Split Across These 3 Cybersecurity Stocks Could Be Worth This Much in 5 Years

AI is making hackers faster and more dangerous, but Fortinet, Cloudflare, and CrowdStrike have shown investors how they plan to win as the technology advances.

Here's a simple way to think about cybersecurity stocks right now: Companies are handing more of their work to artificial intelligence, and hackers are using AI more, too. That turns robust cybersecurity from a nice-to-have into the essential lock on the front door, and three companies in that industry just shared news that hints at where they'll be in five years.

Split an investment of $1,000 evenly across Fortinet (FTNT -2.10%), Cloudflare (NET -1.96%), and CrowdStrike (CRWD -4.31%), and you'd put about $333 into each. In my base cases for how those stocks will perform, that $1,000 would grow to roughly $1,500 by late 2031. These are my own scenarios, built on how fast I expect their sales to grow each year and what valuations investors will be willing to pay for them.

Fortinet sells firewalls -- the boxes that decide what gets into a company's network. On Oct. 28, it reported third-quarter revenue up 20% to $2.08 billion, with product sales, mostly hardware, up 37%.  But it trimmed its forecast for service revenue -- the subscriptions that customers continue to pay after they buy the boxes -- to a range of $5.15 billion to $5.17 billion, down from $5.18 billion to $5.22 billion three months earlier. The stock sold off hard and now trades around $122, down from a 52-week high near $197.

I think the market is reading that backward. CFO Christiane Ohlgart said big new customers switch on their subscriptions in stages, and pointed out that service billings, which offer an early look at future subscription revenue, grew by 26%. Management also expects service revenue to grow faster in 2027 than in 2026.

My five-year scenario: Fortinet sales will grow at an annualized rate of 12% a year, well below this year's pace, and investors will pay 20% less per dollar of sales than today. That would turn a $333 stake into one worth roughly $470.

Cloudflare runs a giant network in front of millions of websites, blocking attacks and speeding up traffic. On Nov. 5, it reported third-quarter revenue up 39% to $782.5 million, its fastest growth rate in nearly four years. It added more than 158,000 paying customers in the quarter, and developers building on its platform jumped from 7 million to 10 million in three months.

What I like most is how it wins them. CEO Matthew Prince said that when AI labs find new software flaws, they quietly tell Cloudflare, which protects customers "whether they pay us or not." He described small banks facing a "tsunami of threats" being pointed by peers and others to Cloudflare. The company also expects to deliver a GAAP profit this quarter, well ahead of its 2028 target.

The catch for investors is the stock's valuation. At about $294 a share, Cloudflare trades at around 32 times this year's expected sales. However, if sales grow 28% a year (slower than they have recently) and that P/s multiple gets cut in half, $333 will still become about $573.

CrowdStrike's Falcon software monitors laptops, servers, and logins for break-ins. Now it's also watching AI agents, programs that complete assigned tasks on their own. On Nov. 3, it agreed to buy Fiddler AI, which tracks what AI agents did and why, and CEO George Kurtz called Fiddler "the flight recorder for every agent."

Here's why that matters. When a plane goes down, investigators pull the black box. When an AI agent sends money to the wrong place, boards and regulators will want answers, and I'd bet the vendor that can produce them will win cybersecurity contracts. Annual recurring revenue, the yearly value of its subscriptions, grew 25% to $5.84 billion last quarter, and CrowdStrike raised its outlook for new subscription growth.

CrowdStrike is also the priciest of the three, trading at about 37 times this year's expected sales. If sales grow 22% a year and that multiple is cut in half, $333 will turn into about $450. That's my lowest scenario, not because the business is weakest, but because investors today have to pay the largest premium up front. If that worries you, building your stake in CrowdStrike by buying shares gradually over time is a reasonable way in.

In my scenarios, the $333 in Fortinet grows to about $470, Cloudflare's to about $573, and CrowdStrike's to about $450, so the original $1,000 would be worth roughly $1,500 in five years. That's not a guarantee, but if AI keeps making security a must-have, splitting your investment across these companies gives you portfolio exposure to a cheaper comeback story, a fast grower, and a leader in AI security.

#3046. Top Wall Street analysts recommend these 3 dividend stocks for higher returns

Dividend-paying stocks can enhance portfolio returns by providing a steady income stream to investors and the potential for capital appreciation. Several investors seek exposure to dividend stocks in a volatile backdrop to ensure stable income.

In this regard, recommendations of top Wall Street analysts can help shortlist attractive dividend stocks, backed by strong financials and cash flows to support consistent dividend payments.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Energy Transfer (ET) is a diversified midstream energy company with about 140,000 miles of pipelines and associated energy infrastructure in 44 states. The company made a quarterly cash distribution of 34 cents per common unit for the second quarter of 2026. At an annualized payment of $1.36 per common unit, ET offers a yield of 6.3%.

Recently, JPMorgan analyst Jeremy Tonet reiterated a buy rating on Energy Transfer stock and slightly raised his price target to $25 from $24. The analyst noted that the company delivered a strong Q2 beat across all key metrics.

The 5-star analyst added that the company "tightened" its capex to $5.6 billion–$5.9 billion and expects capex above $5 billion annually through 2029, given solid opportunities.

About the Hugh Brinson pipeline, Tonet noted Phase 1 (1.5 bcfd) reaching full capacity by September 1 and Phase II capacity (0.7 bcfd) going online by Q1 2027. The analyst also noted completion of a 14-mile Hugh Brinson lateral in Abilene, Texas, and final stages of talks for an additional 250 mmcfd of Oklahoma power plant demand.

"All in, we see ET well positioned to continue capitalizing on organic growth opportunities across the value chain," said Tonet.

Tonet ranks No. 922 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 57% of the time, delivering an average return of 9%. See Energy Transfer Financials on TipRanks.

This week's second dividend pick is independent oil and natural gas company Permian Resources (PR). The company declared a base dividend of $0.16 per share for the third quarter, payable on September 30, 2026. At an annualized dividend of $0.64 per share, PR offers a yield of about 2.7%.

The 5-star analyst noted that while PR stock has outperformed its peers so far in 2026, he sees further upside. Mehta is confident about the company's operational improvements and ability to deliver incremental free cash flow per share through the strategic ground game program. He added that the program has completed about $1.05 billion in bolt-on deals this year (as of August 5).

"We see the continued success of PR's ground game as constructive toward future inventory replenishment and increasing working interest over time," said Mehta.

The analyst also emphasized Permian Resources' solid track record of generating growth in free cash flow (FCF) per share. Mehta expects the company's free cash flow per share to grow at a 20% CAGR (compound annual growth rate) from 2025–2028.

Moving on to Sempra Energy (SRE), a utility holding company. Earlier this month, the company declared a quarterly dividend of $0.6575 per share, payable on October 15. At an annualized dividend of $2.63 per share, SRE stock offers a yield of about 3.1%.

Recently, Jefferies analyst Julien Dumoulin-Smith upgraded SRE stock to Buy from Hold but slightly lowered the price target to $97 from $101. The 5-star analyst noted that Sempra stock is trading at a price/earnings multiple that is 14% below its electric peers due to concerns about Texas transmission capital expenditure and the failure of California legislation.

Smith noted that the derating of weaker California peers PG&E (PCG) and Edison International (EIX) is weighing on the sum-of-the-parts (SOTP) valuation but is becoming less relevant given their materially higher fire risk compared to Sempra Energy.

Furthermore, Smith expects Sempra Energy's Texas capex plan to stay in place despite the opposition to transmission and data centers, though delays to 765-kV projects are likely. While a full recovery for SRE stock will need a clearer outcome from the Texas legislative session, Smith believes that the stock may already be an early opportunity for patient investors.

"We are early on the upgrade but see limited downside at current levels as the market is pricing in little transmission upside," said Smith.

Smith ranks No. 945 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 61% of the time, delivering an average return of 8.2%. See Sempra ETF Exposure on TipRanks.

#3043. Could $5,000 Invested in Nvidia Today Make You a Millionaire?

The scandal-rocked AI bellwether has a long way to go to become a 200-bagger from here. It can still crush the market.

Sometimes opportunity knocks. Other times, it just knocks you out. Nvidia (NVDA -13.63%) shed its market cap crown two weeks ago, and the gap continues to widen. The leading maker of AI chips and other high-level computing solutions has shed nearly 30% of its value since peaking last month.

The stock is surprisingly cheap right now. With a critical earnings report now less than a week away, is this a good place for your next investment? Delivering on the headline that brought you here, can laying $5,000 on Nvidia now make you a millionaire?

You probably know the answer. I have some hard truths if you don't.

Turning any $5,000 investment into a cool million will require patience. Nvidia stock would have to become a 200-bagger to hit seven figures. Put differently, Nvidia commands a market cap just above $4 trillion. There is only one other company on the planet with a higher price tag. Nvidia would have to top $800 trillion for a 200-bagger, and that is if Nvidia's share count doesn't increase over that time.

There is some good news on that front. Nvidia's been an aggressive buyer of its shares. It's been retiring more shares than it's been issuing for stock-based compensation or acquisitions. Its fully diluted share count is declining for the fifth consecutive fiscal year, a nearly 4% reduction over that time.

Nvidia is just getting started. Its board of directors recently announced a corporate record $150 billion in repurchase authorizations, boosting its buyback authorization to $235 billion. If Nvidia were to buy back $235 billion of its shares at current prices, it would reduce its share count by more than 5%.

Nvidia is making a lot of money. Next week we might find out that Nvidia is making even more money. It wouldn't be a surprise. It's been fairly consistent in delivering "beat and raise" performances, even if the shares initially move lower on the strong financials.

You're here for the long haul if you're expecting a 200-bagger to turn $5,000 into $1,000,000. The stock is cheap enough to beat the market for here. Nvidia is trading for less than 11 times next year's earnings estimates, as back-to-back scandals weigh on CEO Jensen Huang. It's going to be an interesting earnings call on Nov. 17, that's for sure.

Even bears have to agree that a company growing its business at a 90% clip -- the way its guidance calls for revenue and earnings growth in its upcoming fiscal third quarter -- is a compelling value. Growth will slow. Nvidia's chunky margins will contract. It's still a good price for what has historically been a great company.

So, can Nvidia really turn $5,000 into a million bucks? It's not a realistic expectation unless you have a decades-long investment horizon. However, it's a great start at an opportunistic time. Investing $5,000 today and continuing to add to that position over time can get you there. It's not the answer you wanted, but it's the response you needed to hear.

#3038. If You Buy and Hold Just 3 ETFs, History Says This Combination Is Your Best Bet

Two of these ETFs probably won't be surprising. The third one might be.

When most people think about investing in stocks right now, they think of the S&P 500 (^GSPC -0.69%) and tech stocks. These groups have driven stock market returns for several years, and most people's portfolios are probably heavily concentrated in them right now.

But that doesn't always work out over the course of decades.

In the 2000-2009 decade, a pair of generational bear markets resulted in a lost decade for the S&P 500, where it lost 9% over that 10-year period. But some areas of the market didn't perform nearly as poorly. Financials and tech were clearly the biggest losers, but energy, consumer staples, small-caps, and emerging markets all posted strong returns.

It may not seem like it lately, but diversification still pays off. Investing in multiple areas of the market at once may not maximize your return potential at all times, but it'll probably smooth out the ride and limit some downside risk along the way.

You don't need a dozen ETFs to achieve diversification, either. In fact, you can hit almost everything you need with just a few of them. But if you're going to own just three ETFs to create a full portfolio, you have to make sure these funds are broadly diversified, cheap, and work well together.

Looking back over several decades of history, here's a three-ETF portfolio that might be your best bet as a long-term holding.

Any time I talk about portfolio construction, I usually start with the Vanguard Total Stock Market ETF (VTI -0.59%) as my core position. A lot of people will understandably use the Vanguard S&P 500 ETF (VOO -0.58%) for this purpose. But I prefer VTI's inclusion of mid-caps and small-caps to cover the entire U.S. equity market.

The potential issue with this fund, of course, is its heavy concentration in tech stocks, which currently account for around 43% of the portfolio. That's clearly on the high side and exposes investors to some extra downside risk right now. If you want an alternative, consider the iShares MSCI USA Equal Weighted ETF (EUSA -0.25%), which has allocations of between 12% and 18% in four different sectors for better diversification.

International equity investing hasn't been popular for some time. U.S. stocks have outperformed them by a large margin, and the extra risk that comes from less developed markets just hasn't been paying off. That doesn't mean that international investing should be written off.

If you go back to the start of the 1970s, you'll see that U.S. and international leadership has rotated in multi-year cycles. Much of the 1970s and 1980s belonged to international. U.S. stocks outperformed in the 1990s, and international recaptured leadership in the 2000s. Since 2010, U.S. stocks have led in almost uninterrupted fashion.

The point is that international stocks are overdue for an extended stretch of outperformance, and they have a long history of doing that from time to time. The Vanguard Total International Stock ETF (VXUS -0.91%) is my pick for this. It invests in nearly 9,000 stocks across both developed and emerging markets, so you're capturing exposure to everything.

I'm guessing that the Vanguard Small-Cap Value ETF (VBR -0.23%) won't be a popular pick, given how poorly this segment has performed in recent years. But small-caps demonstrated how relatively well they can do in severely adverse markets from 2000-2009. That won't always be the case, but its potential for diversification and above-average long-term returns makes it worth considering.

This ETF is the way I'd play it. The undervalued nature of the portfolio helps limit some downside risk, and the sector composition offers a nice complement to what you'd get from the Vanguard Total Stock Market ETF. Quality is usually an issue in the small-cap value category, but this portfolio is forecasted to deliver double-digit earnings and free cash flow growth, a good sign that fundamentals are in decent shape.

Together, these three ETFs should be able to deliver in multiple environments moving forward. They pair together well and should be able to provide a smoother ride in particularly volatile periods.

#3036. Why Fragmented Data Estates Can Drain Leaders' AI Budgets

“Context” is now the most overworked word in enterprise AI. Everyone uses it, but few mean the same thing. I use a narrow definition: Context is what an agent has to know before it can act. That means knowing what data exists, what it means, how it relates to everything else, when it’s valid and where to find it. That doesn’t come with the model. It must be built into the data estate, because an agent can’t ask the person who built the table.​

Skipping that work costs money. Without context, an agent will fall back on raw intelligence. It will search, guess and infer what it could have been told directly. You will pay in tokens for every wasted step, and each wasted step is another chance to get it wrong.​

Most enterprises aren’t there yet, which is why the bottleneck is in the data estate, not the model. In a 2026 survey Teradata commissioned from Wakefield Research, more than 75% of senior technology and data leaders estimated that 20% or less of their data is “ready for AI agents to use reliably.” Roughly the same percentage said they struggle to unify data and knowledge across business functions well enough for agents to reason across the enterprise.​

Those gaps don’t disappear when an agent gains access to the data; they become operational risks.​

Giving agents access to data without context to understand it is called context fragmentation: data that lacks the meaning, lineage and governance an agent needs to act reliably across an organization. Unlike humans, agents fail quietly. An analyst handed a revenue figure that looks wrong may stop and ask someone. An agent working from the same figure may act on it, and the mistake may surface weeks later.​

Organizations need to make sure their data carries enough meaning for agents to reliably utilize it. The more time an agent searches for an answer, the more time it has to hallucinate.​

If you’re relying solely on powerful models and prompt engineering to get the most out of your data, you’re doing it wrong.​

A brilliant model may not know which customer table finance trusts, but the same is true of a brilliant new hire. Point one at piles of paper and say, “Find me this answer.” They would have to read every pile before they could answer.​

Now put a Post-it note on top of each pile, describing what’s in them, what you won’t find, how the piles relate and which documents to pull first. The same person can find the right answer in a fraction of the time.​

Agents need the same notes. Without them, agents will explore every schema, guess at joins, run queries that fail or return the wrong thing, then try again.​

Context gets built in two layers. The bottom-up layer describes each source: what it holds, what it doesn’t, how it should be used and which other sources it joins to. That is one of a data product’s jobs: telling an agent what a dataset is before the first query.​

The top-down layer is an ontology. It defines the business: things it cares about, how they relate and what constraints they obey, regardless of which system holds the data. Neither layer works alone. Descriptions without an ontology give an agent well-labeled piles but no picture of how the business fits together. An ontology without descriptions has the opposite problem. It knows a customer should exist and how a customer relates to an order, but it doesn’t know where customer data lives, which data is correct for this use or whether anyone still maintains it.​

Take a simple question about last quarter’s revenue, for example. Ask sales, finance and billing, and you’ll receive three different numbers. Each team is right about its own number. The ontology says what revenue is and how it relates to customers and contracts. The descriptions say where each number lives and how current it is. Neither one tells the agent which number to use for the question in front of it.​

Building either layer by hand doesn’t scale. Both have to span every system the business runs, without moving the data first, because an agent’s question doesn’t stop at a platform boundary. Nobody is going to write notes for decades of tables. Much of the bottom layer can be discovered from the estate itself: what each source holds, where it came from, how it’s used and what it connects in practice. The top layer doesn’t have to start from a blank page either, because how a bank or a retailer works is already well understood broadly.​

But no amount of discovery or industry knowledge can supply what people in the business have never written down: which number counts and for which question.​

The returns from enterprise AI come when agents act on the business, not just answer questions about it. Governance is what lets you give them that authority. Someone must decide what an agent may do, what data it sees and on whose behalf, how long it runs, how much it spends and when a person has to sign off. Those limits can’t live in the prompt. A model can be talked out of an instruction, so the controls belong outside it, in the systems the agent acts through. And the enterprise should hold its own off switch rather than depend on a provider’s.​

Models can and should be interchangeable. Context and governance should stay with the enterprise when the model changes.​

The advantage is moving away from models, which everyone can rent, and moving toward giving each decision the context it needs and leaving out what it doesn’t. The advantage will go to the enterprises that put notes on the piles and agree on what the notes mean. Every definition they settle on is reasoning their agents no longer pay for, whichever model they run. Everyone else will keep paying the context tax.​

Forbes Technology Council is an invitation-only community for world-class CIOs, CTOs and technology executives. Do I qualify?

#3031. Wall Street's Next Blockbuster Stock Split Could Come From This Chip Equipment Giant

ASML posted record sales in its latest quarter, and some customers are already eyeing orders for delivery in 2029.

ASML Holding (ASML +1.76%) has been one of the biggest beneficiaries of the artificial intelligence (AI) boom, thanks to its role in advanced chipmaking. In its third-quarter earnings announcement on Oct. 14, ASML reported record quarterly sales, with management citing continued strength in AI investments and customer equipment demand. With AI fueling another wave of demand for its equipment, the company's rising stock price could position it to conduct Wall Street's next major stock split.

ASML holds one of the most important positions in the AI supply chain. It's the world's only commercial supplier of extreme ultraviolet (EUV) lithography machines, which are necessary to manufacture all of the most advanced semiconductors.

Its customers include chip industry heavyweights like Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing, Samsung, and Intel.

On top of that, ASML isn't a stranger to stock splits. It completed three during its earlier years: a 2-for-1 split in 1997, another 2-for-1 split in 1998, and a 3-for-1 split in 2000.

But it's been a while since ASML's last split, suggesting another one could be long overdue as its growth accelerates and pushes its share price higher.

When it comes to growth, ASML's management said that its current supply plans for 2027 and 2028 would not be sufficient to meet expected customer demand. In light of that, some customers are already expressing interest in placing orders for equipment deliveries as far out as 2029 and 2030.

And that brings us to another potentially important growth driver: ASML's newest generation of extreme ultraviolet lithography technology.

ASML's existing EUV technology is already nearly impossible for competitors to replicate -- if rivals do, it won't happen for years. However, the company isn't standing still. Specifically, it has developed High Numerical Aperture (High NA) EUV lithography equipment, which can print even smaller transistors and features, and thus fabricate more component-dense and powerful chips.

And one of the most encouraging developments is that High NA EUV technology is moving beyond experimentation.

For example, early adopter Intel has already processed more than 1 million wafers using the technology across equipment qualification, research and development (R&D), and production applications.

Meanwhile, other manufacturers are moving toward adoption.

For instance, Samsung Electronics and SK Hynix have outlined plans to introduce High NA EUV technology into their memory production beginning in 2028, while TSMC, the world's largest third-party chip foundry by far, intends to use it in high-volume manufacturing of advanced chips starting in 2030.

That matters because the future of ASML's next-generation technology doesn't depend on a single customer or application. Some of the world's biggest semiconductor manufacturers are already preparing to integrate it into their long-term production plans.

But despite its competitive advantages and technological monopoly, ASML still faces risks. For one, the chipmaking industry it supports is cyclical. If AI spending slows, chipmakers could delay their foundry expansion plans, potentially reducing demand for expensive EUV lithography equipment.

Plus, geopolitical restrictions and export controls, especially those that currently block the sales of advanced semiconductor manufacturing equipment to customers in China, are ongoing headwinds to its sales growth. In addition, the company's reliance on highly specialized supply chains complicates production of its machines.

Regardless, given ASML's dominant position in chipmaking technology and the AI boom providing a strong growth catalyst, there are plenty of reasons why its stock belongs on retail investors' radars.

Analysts on Wall Street remain optimistic about ASML stock. In fact, the vast majority of analysts covering it rate it a buy or strong buy, with an average 12-month price target of over $2,100.

Meanwhile, the stock trades at around $1,550 per share and at a forward price-to-earnings (P/E) ratio of about 23, which looks like a reasonable valuation, especially considering its competitive moat and long-term AI growth opportunities. That could leave room for further upside if earnings growth beats expectations.

Overall, ASML has a lot going for it, from its role in advanced chipmaking to the growing demand for AI infrastructure. If its growth continues to accelerate and pushes the stock higher, its first stock split in decades could be on the table, too.

#3027. Chevron's Production Fell. Its Profit Nearly Tripled. Here's How.

Fewer barrels, bigger profits: Chevron's latest quarter highlights the strength of its integrated business model.

Since the start of the year, the conflict in Iran has resulted in disruptions in the Strait of Hormuz, through which roughly 20 million barrels of oil pass daily. Oil prices have jumped. More importantly, diesel fuel and other refined products have surged.

Chevron's (CVX +0.58%) third-quarter adjusted profit nearly tripled year over year to $10.4 billion, despite production declining 2.6%. The seemingly contradictory move illustrates the advantages of Chevron's integrated business model. Here's what investors should know.

As an integrated oil and gas company, Chevron's business spans upstream exploration and production of crude oil and natural gas, as well as downstream refining and fuel sales. By operating across the value chain, Chevron has a natural hedge that helps it weather major fluctuations in crude oil prices and fuel and other refined products.

In the third quarter, production dropped by roughly 106,000 barrels of oil equivalent per day, or roughly 2.6%. This was driven in part by fewer physical barrels under production -- sharing contract entitlements and by disruptions in the Middle East. While production fell, upstream earnings surged 72% to $5.7 billion.

On top of this, downstream refining margins exploded as global refining capacity took a hit amid the Iran conflict, with strikes hitting facilities in the Middle East and Ukrainian attacks on Russian refineries taking roughly 10% of the world's fuel-making capacity offline. As a result, Chevron's refineries operated at 98% capacity, and downstream earnings surged 333% year over year to $4.9 billion.

Chevron is a top integrated oil and gas company vital to the energy complex, and this year illustrated the strengths of its integrated business model and capital discipline, showing why it continues to deliver for long-term investors with 39 consecutive years of dividend increases.

#3026. Top Wall Street analysts find these 3 stocks attractive as long-term investments

Investors have been grappling with stock market volatility, weighing elevated oil prices, persistent geopolitical tensions, the Federal Reserve's rate hike and a debate about AI safety. For all that, it helps to look beyond short-term noise and focus on stocks with strong long-term growth potential.

Top Wall Street analysts' recommendations can help investors gain clarity, as these experts conduct in-depth research to pick stocks with attractive growth prospects.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Enterprise software and cloud infrastructure company Oracle (ORCL) recently announced better-than-expected results for the first quarter of fiscal 2027. The quarter benefited from solid growth in cloud infrastructure revenue.

After hosting a series of investor meetings with Ken Bond, Oracle head of investor relations following Q1 earnings, Guggenheim analyst John DiFucci reiterated a buy rating on the stock with a price target of $400. Oracle remains a Guggenheim Best Idea, he said.

"We continue to believe ORCL is a decade stock, driven by the massive (and profitable) opportunity ahead for AI training and inferencing," said DiFucci. He also cited the traditional Public Cloud business and additional AI-enabled cloud database and cloud applications for his bullish stance. DiFucci expects all the factors to drive revenue and profit acceleration over the next few years.

While Oracle delivered 850 MW of data center capacity in Q1 FY27, Bond cautioned that Infrastructure as a Service revenue in any quarter depends more on the timing of when that capacity comes online, than on the headline MW number. DiFucci expects IaaS growth to stay strong, with full-year growth expected above Q1 FY27's 120% constant currency rate. Management expects total revenue growth to accelerate sequentially throughout fiscal 2027, as the faster-growing infrastructure business makes up a larger share of the mix.

DiFucci addressed concerns regarding customer concentration, saying OpenAI's share of total remaining performance obligations has dropped from 80% last June to about 50% now, and is expected to keep falling as new contracts are signed with new and existing customers.

DiFucci ranks No. 263 among more than 12,490 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 62% of the time, delivering an average return of about 17.5%. See Oracle Insider Trading Activity on TipRanks.

Space systems and launch services provider Rocket Lab (RKLB) was recently initiated as a buy with an $80 price target by Raymond James analyst Brian Gesuale.

"RKLB has assembled launch, spacecraft, components, payloads and optical communications, with Neutron and Iridium completing medium-lift and applications/spectrum in 2027," said Gesuale.

The 5-star analyst highlighted that with the completion of the Iridium acquisition, Rocket Lab will become the only vertically integrated space company with positive free cash flow (FCF). The deal will add a deployed constellation valued at more than $3 billion, globally coordinated L-band spectrum and about $500 million in earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation, and amortization (EBITDA) and $300 million in free cash flow (FCF).

Gesuale highlighted RKLB's strong revenue and backlog growth and progress toward positive EBITDA and FCF in 2027/2028. Rocket Lab is moving from a heavy investment phase into monetization, with estimated revenue growth of 59% in 2026, the analyst said, noting that RKLB's backlog has more than doubled to almost $2.4 billion from $1.1 billion in Q3 2025.

Given strong growth projections, the analyst expects RKLB's gross margins to expand from about a 35% trough in 2027 to about 47% by 2030, while R&D spending is estimated to decline from ~35% of sales in 2026 to about 10% in 2028, creating substantial operating leverage as the platform matures.

Gesuale ranks No. 369 among more than 12,490 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of about 17%. See Rocket Lab Statistics on TipRanks.

J.P. Morgan analyst Doug Anmuth recently upgraded Meta Platforms (META) to buy from hold and raised his price target to $820 from $640. Despite the year-to-date underperformance of META, the 5-star analyst still sees significant upside, as Meta is in the early stages of launching frontier models and AI-powered offerings beyond advertising, particularly the Muse AI agent and Meta Model API access.

"Frontier models are at the core of Meta's product and monetization pipeline over a multi-year period, and its path toward superintelligence," said Anmuth.

In the summer of 2025, Meta set a goal to rebuild its Meta Superintelligence Labs team and deliver frontier AI models within a year, the analyst said. The team appears to have met its goal, rapidly advancing from the rollout of Muse Spark 1.1 in July to Muse Spark 1.3, which is competitive with Claude and GPT models, Anmuth said.

Meanwhile, Meta's Watermelon product will expand opportunities in consumer products, business intelligence, and Family of Apps engagement, the analyst said. The product will enhance internal operations and overall efficiency, said Anmuth, who's confident of Meta's ability to bring consumer-driven AI products to its extensive user base of 4 billion and views its distribution network as a competitive advantage.

While Meta has focused on frontier models and AI product development, Anmuth also sees solid opportunity in its AI infrastructure capacity, which he believes will support future compute needs.
