#3022. History Says the Nasdaq Will Soar Once Again in 2027 After the Sell-Off. The Best Growth Stock to Buy Before That Happens

Seagate's impressive growth and attractive valuation could send it on a bull run over the next three years.

Technology stocks have been crushed lately, which explains why the tech-laden Nasdaq Composite (^IXIC -1.46%) index has slid by more than 5% since the beginning of October.

The meltdown in tech stocks can be attributed to several factors. From the Federal Reserve's interest rate hike in September to the likelihood of further rate hikes to control inflation to the outcome of the midterm elections, investors have become risk-averse. They are booking profits in tech stocks following a strong run over the past year and a half.

However, what's worth noting is that this isn't the first time the Nasdaq Composite index has witnessed such volatility.

The tariffs announced by President Donald Trump last year crushed tech stocks. Investors panicked amid fears that the proposed tariffs would hurt demand for chips, components, smartphones, and other things. As a result, the Nasdaq Composite slid 10% between February and April 2025. In fact, the index was down over 20% at one point in early April 2025, compared with the beginning of February.

However, the artificial intelligence (AI) gravy train shielded tech companies from any potential slowdown. This explains why the Nasdaq Composite has rallied by an impressive 44% over the past year and a half despite the recent setback. I won't be surprised to see history repeat itself, as AI's productivity gains and healthy investments in this space will ensure robust earnings growth for tech companies.

For instance, a survey conducted by the consulting giant McKinsey earlier this year found that 80% of respondents who use AI at work reported higher productivity. Meanwhile, 50% of the respondents admitted that AI is improving their decision-making capability. As a result, the adoption of AI and AI agents in enterprises is growing.

This is great news for Seagate Technology (STX -5.56%), which is benefiting big time from AI adoption and has seen a substantial slide in its stock price lately.

The past three months have been brutal for Seagate stock investors. It has lost 31% of its value over this period. However, the company is capitalizing on the deployment of AI data centers.

Seagate manufactures data storage solutions, including hard-disk drives (HDDs), solid-state drives (SSDs), and high-capacity storage solutions for enterprises and data centers. The company's storage solutions have been supporting the global AI boom, helping hyperscalers and AI labs store massive data sets for training AI models and running inference applications.

Not surprisingly, Seagate has been enjoying stunning growth. The company's revenue in the first quarter of fiscal 2027 (which ended on Oct. 2) jumped 61% year over year to $4.23 billion. Meanwhile, the strong demand and short supply of HDDs sent Seagate's non-GAAP operating margin to 50.7% in the previous quarter, up from 29% in the year-ago period. The company's earnings per share (EPS) nearly tripled year over year to $7.68.

The data center business accounted for 81% of the top line, up 63% from the year-ago period. Seagate is confident of maintaining its impressive momentum. The company will finalize the configuration and pricing for its 2028 storage devices over the next few quarters, noting that the pricing environment continues to improve.

What's more, Seagate management noted on the latest earnings call that it expects further expansion in revenue and margins this year, driven "by an extremely strong demand environment with visibility extending beyond 2030." The company's guidance reflects management's claim.

Seagate expects $4.8 billion in revenue in the current quarter, implying a 70% jump over last year. Even better, its EPS is poised to nearly triple once again to $9.20. Given that Seagate sees strong demand visibility through the end of the decade, it is easy to see why analysts are projecting healthy EPS growth over the next three years.

STX EPS Estimates for Current Fiscal Year data by YCharts

Specifically, Seagate's earnings are predicted to jump by 157% this year, and I won't be surprised to see it outperform expectations over the next couple of years. Moreover, Seagate trades at just 14.6 times forward earnings, a discount to the tech-laden Nasdaq-100 index's forward earnings multiple of 20. Assuming Seagate trades in line with the Nasdaq's forward earnings multiple after three years and its EPS jumps to $87.28, its stock price will skyrocket to $1,745.

That's just over 3x where this AI stock is trading right now. So, buying Seagate right now could be a profitable move, especially given that a potential rally in the Nasdaq Composite next year could lift the stock out of its rut and send it on a bull run.

#3020. Post-Quantum Cryptography Is No Longer The Future. Post-Cyber Is.

Distinguished Engineer at a global bank, ambassador for Microsoft & The Linux Foundation, transforming the future one giant step at a time.

​The biggest mistake we can make about quantum security is to think the problem is quantum.

Post-quantum cryptography (PQC) is no longer a research curiosity. In the fall of 2024, NIST finalized its first three (later more) post-quantum cryptographic standards, with a migration roadmap targeting the removal of quantum-vulnerable public-key cryptography from its standards by 2035. The message is clear: Start migrating now. But I believe we are missing a larger conversation.

Quantum-safe cryptography addresses a specific mathematical vulnerability. It does not make our systems resilient. And resilience, not encryption alone, will define the next era of digital trust.

In the Snowflake customer breaches, attackers weren’t breaking encryption. They were logging in using stolen credentials. Mandiant identified approximately 165 potentially exposed organizations, with compromised accounts lacking multifactor authentication.

Then came the CrowdStrike outage. A faulty software update disrupted approximately 8.5 million Windows devices worldwide. Airports, financial services and critical infrastructure experienced disruptions.

Neither incident required a quantum computer. One involved compromised identities. The other involved a failure in trusted software. Both exposed an uncomfortable truth: We can have perfectly secure cryptography and still have catastrophically fragile systems.

Meanwhile, AI is making the situation more complicated. Threat intelligence from Google in 2026 documented increasingly sophisticated uses of AI in vulnerability research, exploitation and social engineering.

The attack surface is expanding faster than our traditional security boundaries.

A sufficiently powerful quantum computer running Shor’s algorithm could compromise RSA and elliptic-curve cryptography, undermining much of today’s digital infrastructure. Nobody knows when that computer will arrive. In 2025, Google’s Craig Gidney published research suggesting RSA-2048 could theoretically be factored with fewer than one million noisy physical qubits under specific engineering assumptions, a dramatic reduction from previous estimates. And now we are down to a mere 10,000. This was not a prediction of imminent quantum capability. But it demonstrated how quickly assumptions can change.

And cryptographic migration takes years, sometimes decades. The “harvest now, decrypt later” threat makes waiting especially dangerous. Sensitive information intercepted today may become readable tomorrow. We absolutely need PQC.

But replacing one cryptographic algorithm with another cannot be our entire strategy.

History provides an important warning. The SIKE post-quantum candidate was undermined by classical cryptanalysis in 2022. The lesson wasn’t that post-quantum cryptography had failed. It was that we must design systems capable of changing their cryptography when assumptions fail.

Crypto agility matters as much as cryptographic strength.

When I say post-cyber, I don’t mean cybersecurity is disappearing. I mean cybersecurity as an isolated technical discipline is no longer sufficient. Through my work across enterprise technology, regulators and open-source communities, I’ve increasingly seen the difference between securing individual components and engineering trustworthy systems.

The former focuses on controls. The latter asks whether the business can survive when those controls, or the components they protect, fail. A post-cyber operating model connects six essential properties:

These aren’t new concepts. NIST CSF 2.0, zero-trust architecture, cyber-resilience engineering and CISA’s Secure by Design principles already address much of this territory. The missing piece is often organizational. We continue treating cryptographic migration, identity management, AI governance, software supply chains and operational resilience as separate initiatives. Attackers and failures don’t respect those boundaries. Neither should our architectures.

Apple’s PQ3 protocol for iMessage demonstrates an important architectural principle. Rather than simply replacing classical encryption, Apple adopted hybrid cryptography, periodic rekeying and mechanisms designed to recover from certain key compromises.

That’s the mindset we need beyond cryptography. Software updates should be staged and reversible. Dependencies should be visible. Identities should be continuously verified. Recovery should be engineered and tested, not assumed.

The first identifies cryptographic exposure: RSA, elliptic-curve cryptography, certificates, VPNs, applications, hardware and third-party dependencies requiring PQC migration. The second identifies systemic fragility.

What happens if privileged credentials are stolen? If a trusted supplier distributes defective software? If an AI agent operates beyond its intended authority? If a critical cloud dependency disappears? Knowing where vulnerable cryptography exists is essential. Knowing what can stop your business even when cryptography works perfectly is equally important.

Tracking the percentage of systems migrated to PQC is useful but insufficient. Leaders should also ask:

These questions turn security from a compliance exercise into a strategic capability.

Some will argue that post-cyber is simply another label for existing cybersecurity and resilience practices. That’s fair criticism.

We don’t need another acronym, compliance framework or organizational silo. What we need is a different way of connecting the disciplines we already have. PQC is one of the most consequential infrastructure migrations of this decade. It deserves urgent investment and serious engineering attention. But it cannot become another exercise in checking boxes while ignoring systemic weaknesses.

PQC asks whether our mathematics can survive a new kind of computer. Post-cyber asks whether our institutions, architectures and critical services can survive a world in which the assumptions beneath digital trust keep changing.

The first question now has standards, algorithms and a migration calendar.

#3018. Bitdeer: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Nov. 10, 2026, Bitdeer (BTDR -9.25%) announced an 89% year-over-year revenue increase to $320.7 million as it transitions power assets into high-performance computing data centers. The company is pivoting from volatile Bitcoin mining to long-term contracted artificial intelligence (AI) cloud infrastructure. Three things management said stand out.

Bitdeer is securing multiyear contracts for its high-performance computing (HPC, data centers built for intensive AI tasks) infrastructure and cloud services, giving the business visible cash flows that don't depend on cryptocurrency price swings.

The company aims to maximize returns by deploying power where economic value is highest, balancing short-term crypto earnings with long-term data center revenue.

#3017. 16 Space-Based Technologies That Could Solve Business Problems

Advances in satellite technology, connectivity and data analysis are creating new ways for businesses to address challenges here on Earth. From monitoring infrastructure and tracking environmental risks to improving communications in remote locations, space-based capabilities are becoming increasingly practical across industries.

For businesses, the challenge isn’t simply gaining access to these tools—it’s identifying where they can improve decisions, boost efficiency and strengthen resilience. Below, members of Forbes Technology Council highlight space-based capabilities that are becoming practical for business use and explain the problems they could help solve.

Satellite radar can detect subtle ground movement around railways, pipelines and industrial sites, giving businesses early warning of infrastructure risks across vast networks. Paired with ground inspections, it helps operators target maintenance before damage escalates. The payoff: fewer costly shutdowns, smarter use of maintenance budgets, and better protection for people, assets and revenue. Its real value is turning unseen risks into actionable priorities. - Shelli Brunswick, SB Global LLC

Space-based weather and climate intelligence is becoming practical enough to shape operational decisions across industries. Hyper-local forecasts, severe weather tracking and climate risk modeling now draw on satellite data at resolutions that support utility grid planning, agriculture, insurance underwriting, logistics routing and renewable energy forecasting, leading to meaningful business and financial impact. - Nitin Agarwal, Luminace

Forbes Technology Council is an invitation-only community for world-class CIOs, CTOs and technology executives. Do I qualify?

Satellite-based methane detection is becoming a practical business capability. Advanced orbital sensors can identify emissions at individual industrial facilities, enabling companies to detect costly leaks without extensive ground inspections. Integrating this intelligence with enterprise analytics transforms environmental monitoring into actionable insights that reduce operational losses and strengthen regulatory compliance. - Gouri Sankar Dash, Tata Consultancy Services

On-orbit AI processing is becoming practical enough to change how businesses use satellite data. Instead of sending massive amounts of raw imagery to Earth for analysis, satellites can process data in orbit and transmit relevant insights. That can reduce latency and bandwidth while enabling faster responses to disasters, infrastructure issues and other events. - Karan Alang, Versa Networks Inc.

Satellite-based Earth observation is becoming a powerful business tool these days. Using frequent imagery and AI analytics, companies can monitor crop health, detect supply-chain disruptions, assess natural-disaster damage and evaluate infrastructure risks. What once required costly, time-consuming field inspections can increasingly be done remotely, helping businesses make faster, data-driven decisions and build resilience. - Deepali Kaushik, NBCUniversal

Satellite-based flood monitoring can help companies protect construction sites and supply routes. Combine updated imagery with site and road data to identify exposure and prioritize inspections or rerouting. The value comes from turning that information into an operational decision, with people on the ground confirming conditions. - Shahen Minasyan, Twin Peaks Inc. (TPI)

Satellite-based maritime connectivity is finally becoming practical enough to keep ships connected far from shore. Having worked aboard cruise ships, I know how quickly isolation complicates operations. Reliable links can support live equipment diagnostics and faster medical consultations. Start with one critical shipboard system. Space technology earns its keep when it solves problems at sea. - Joel Frenette, TravelFun.ai

Space-based data centers are an exciting emerging capability. Companies like TakeMe2Space are exploring how computing infrastructure in orbit could address some of Earth’s data center challenges. While technical and economic hurdles remain, this approach could create new possibilities for processing data in space and reducing dependence on resource-intensive ground infrastructure. - Prashanthi Kolluru, KloudPortal Technology Solutions Pvt Ltd.

There’s satellite-verified proof of origin for supply chains. New rules on deforestation and forced labor require companies to prove where raw materials such as cocoa, palm oil or timber came from, down to the plot. Linking satellite imagery of those plots to supplier records and purchase orders in the ERP turns a paper declaration into evidence an auditor or customs officer can check. - Hari Sonnenahalli, NTT Data Business Solutions

Satellite broadband is now practical. It gives remote sites like mines, ships, farms and disaster zones low-latency links to the cloud where fiber can’t reach. Those edge locations can stream Internet of Things data, run cloud-based analytics, and stay online when local networks fail, so remote operations become connected and data-driven. - Srinivas Chippagiri, Salesforce Inc.

High-precision satellite positioning is becoming practical for construction, agriculture and autonomous operations. With real-time correction services, centimeter-level positioning can help businesses coordinate equipment, automate physical work and reduce costly errors. Location is becoming an operational signal, not just a point on a map. - Vibhor Kumar, EDB Postgres AI

Direct satellite connection to ordinary phones—which allows workers to stay reachable outside cell coverage—used to mean buying satellite hardware and training people to carry it, so most crews didn’t leverage it. Now a standard phone can send a message through a satellite from a dead zone. For the utilities, logging, mining and construction industries, that means a lone worker in a canyon can reach someone, and a supervisor always knows where the crew is. - Kriti Faujdar, Yelp

There’s satellite-based asset tracking for remote operations. Low-orbit connectivity now makes it affordable to monitor equipment, shipments and field crews in places cellular networks never reached. For businesses with assets at job sites, rail lines or rural facilities, that turns blind spots into live data on location, condition and usage, which means fewer lost assets and faster response. - Steven Singer, Arbill

Satellite monitoring of construction progress can help lenders decide when to release project funds. Imagery can verify whether a road, plant or housing phase has advanced as reported, flag stalled work, and direct inspectors to locations that need a closer look. That gives credit teams a cross-check between site visits, reducing delays in drawdowns without treating pixels as proof of workmanship. Each alert can be tied to human inspection and a documented payment decision. - Jagadish Gokavarapu, Wissen Infotech

Low-Earth-orbit satellite internet’s lower latency and falling costs now support video visits, remote monitoring and image transfer in rural clinics that fiber never reached. Hospitals are also adding it as backup connectivity when storms or cable cuts take down terrestrial lines. For health systems, it closes access gaps and keeps clinical systems reachable during disasters. - Will Conaway, Tuxedo Cat Consulting

Satellite monitoring of harmful algal blooms is becoming practical for aquaculture and water-dependent businesses. Ocean color sensors can identify changes in water conditions that may indicate developing blooms. Fish farms and coastal operators can use these alerts to prioritize water testing and take protective action when needed. This helps reduce unexpected losses and supports better management of marine operations. - Salice Thomas, Wipro Limited

#3014. What Makes A VC Say Yes To A Founder

Mary Minno is a Founding Partner at Treehub.

Over the past year, I’ve backed 43 startups. This fall, I’m teaching Stanford School of Medicine’s AI Lab master’s course, training medtech entrepreneurs. Between investing at Treehub and teaching at Stanford, I spend my time thinking about what makes a founder successful. At Treehub, we’ve interacted with more than 700 startups over the past year—from polished pitches to brilliant scientists who have never pitched before and founders who can sell a vision but haven’t figured out how to build it.

There’s no one playbook to raise venture capital. Every investor has a different thesis and definition of potential. But reflecting on my time on both sides of the table, four qualities stand out to me.

And they aren’t necessarily what you’d expect.

The best founders can move between the big picture and the smallest details, explain how their company will transform an industry and then walk me through a technical challenge or what they learned from a failed experiment.

Vision without execution is just an idea. Execution without vision can lead to building something that never becomes a meaningful business.

This is particularly important in healthcare and AI. Scientists, physicians and researchers often understand a problem more deeply than anyone else. But building a company requires connecting that expertise to a bigger picture. Who needs this? Why now? How will it reach them? And how big could it become?

I don’t expect founders to know everything. I look for people who can connect what they know to what they still need to learn.

The best founders have an intangible quality that makes you want to keep listening. They paint a picture of what the world can become. They communicate in a simple, poetic way that captivates listeners, often with infectious energy.

It isn’t about being the loudest person in the room or having the slickest presentation. It’s about having contagious conviction that draws people in.

Building a company means convincing employees to join, convincing customers to try an unproven product and convincing investors to back a future you can’t guarantee. That takes the ability to communicate why an idea matters.

This storytelling is a skill founders can develop. What can’t be manufactured is genuine conviction. The best founders have an unmistakable connection to the problem they’re solving. They don’t just want you to understand their idea; they need you to understand why it matters.

Every founder has a vision. What separates those who build companies from those who don’t is how they respond when that vision collides with reality.

When a customer says no, a technical breakthrough takes longer than expected or a business model fails in practice, I want to know whether a founder can get creative, find another path and adapt without losing sight of the underlying problem.

Grit isn’t stubbornly pursuing the same strategy regardless of the evidence. It’s refusing to abandon an important problem just because the first solution didn’t work.

In my work, I often meet founders before they’ve created a pitch deck. Without revenue or customer traction to evaluate, I look at what they’ve already done with limited resources. Have they investigated the problem, challenged their assumptions and found ways to make progress?

Those actions reveal how they might navigate uncertainty when the stakes get higher. Resourcefulness before a company exists can be just as telling as traction after launch.

Building a company takes extraordinary time, attention and emotional energy. I don’t mean founders should sacrifice their health or relationships. Burnout isn’t a business strategy.

But there’s a difference between liking an idea and feeling compelled to solve a problem. Investors want to see the commitment to push through difficult stretches after the excitement of starting something new wears off.

This is especially relevant for scientists and clinicians. Turning years of research or firsthand experience into a business requires taking on challenges beyond your original expertise. Why this problem? Why are you the person to solve it? What have you seen that others haven’t? And why spend the next several years figuring it out?

A genuine connection between the founder and the problem can keep them moving when progress is slow and the path forward is unclear.

One of the biggest mistakes investors can make is confusing a founder’s ability to pitch with their ability to build.

Traditional venture capital relies on signals like revenue, growth and customer adoption. Those metrics matter, but they can cause investors to overlook people earlier in their journey. Waiting for them can mean overlooking exceptional founders before they’ve had the opportunity to prove themselves.

Some of the most consequential healthcare companies may come from researchers developing scientific breakthroughs, physicians who’ve witnessed persistent failures in patient care or technical founders who recognize opportunities others haven’t yet. They may not have a perfect pitch or an established network. That doesn’t mean they lack potential.

Of course, charisma, grit, vision and commitment don’t guarantee success. A founder still needs a real market, a viable business model and a solution that works.

But when I think about what makes me say yes, I return to four questions: Can this person connect the details to the vision? Can they bring others along? Can they navigate obstacles? And are they deeply committed to solving the problem?

Sometimes, the founder worth betting on isn’t the one who already knows how to build a company. It’s the one who has found a problem worth solving and can learn everything else along the way. As investors, our job is to recognize that potential before everyone else does.​​​

Forbes Technology Council is an invitation-only community for world-class CIOs, CTOs and technology executives. Do I qualify?

#3011. Nvidia vs. AMD: Which Is the Better Artificial Intelligence (AI) Stock for 2027 After the Tech Sell-Off?

Investors have an easy choice to make if they are looking to buy one of these two semiconductor stocks right now.

Artificial intelligence (AI) chip stocks have witnessed a brutal sell-off lately, as evidenced by the 20% dip in the PHLX Semiconductor Sector index over the past month.

Not surprisingly, semiconductor stalwarts Nvidia (NVDA -9.82%) and Advanced Micro Devices (AMD +1.58%) have also been under pressure. While AMD has retreated 15% over the past month, Nvidia has dropped 26%, relinquishing its status as the world's largest company in the process.

However, the solid financial performance of both these companies doesn't justify their recent slide. Both Nvidia and AMD have been clocking robust growth, and they are poised to continue their momentum into the New Year. That's why I will take a closer look at the prospects and valuations of both companies to determine which one is the better semiconductor stock to buy for 2027.

Nvidia remains the dominant player in AI chips despite intensifying competition. Semiconductor supply chain data provider Silicon Analysts estimates that Nvidia will control 75% of the AI chip market by the end of 2026.

The company has been pulling the right strings to maintain its status as the top AI chip player. It is now designing server systems aimed at enhancing inference performance while reducing costs simultaneously. Nvidia claims that its latest Vera Rubin chip systems consume twice as much power as the previous-generation Blackwell processors but deliver 10x higher performance-per-watt.

Nvidia has begun shipping its Vera Rubin systems in volume to customers. It expects the platform to account for 20% of its data center revenue in the third quarter of fiscal 2027. The company should see a significant ramp in Vera Rubin sales in 2027, which will be a tailwind for its revenue and earnings growth, given the significantly higher revenue it expects from its latest systems.

It is worth noting that Nvidia has already guided for a 70% increase in its fiscal 2028 revenue (which will end in January 2028 and coincide with the majority of calendar 2027). Analysts are forecasting a 71% increase in Nvidia's earnings per share (EPS) in fiscal 2028, following an impressive 95% jump that's projected for the current fiscal year.

So, there is a solid chance of Nvidia stock stepping on the gas once again in the New Year.

Silicon Analysts notes that AMD controlled 6%-8% of the AI chip market last year. However, the company's latest quarterly results indicate that it is gaining ground in this market. AMD's data center revenue surged 97% year over year in Q3 to $8.5 billion, driven by the growing adoption of its server processors, graphics cards, and rack-scale systems.

For some perspective, Nvidia's data center revenue surged by 106% year over year in the last reported quarter to $96.2 billion. So, while Nvidia remains the significantly larger player in this segment, AMD's accelerating growth rate suggests that it is gradually catching up. Importantly, AMD expects its data center growth to accelerate.

The Q4 revenue guidance of $17 billion implies a year-over-year jump of 66%, higher than the 45% growth it reported in Q2. The data center business produced 64% of AMD's revenue last quarter, and it expects the segment to account for more than 80% of its top line in 2027. Also, AMD expects total revenue to increase by more than 80% next year, an acceleration over the 50% top-line growth it is expected to deliver in 2026.

What's more, AMD's EPS growth rate is projected to accelerate from 93% this year to 108% in 2027, according to Yahoo! Finance consensus estimates. Given that the data center segment will account for the majority of AMD's top line next year, it is evident that the company will gain more share in AI chips. So, is AMD a better AI semiconductor stock to buy compared to Nvidia?

We have seen that both Nvidia and AMD are on track to deliver impressive earnings growth in 2027. However, AMD's EPS growth is poised to be higher than Nvidia's, suggesting that it may be the better growth stock to buy.

But the valuation tilts the balance in Nvidia's favor, as evident from the following chart.

Nvidia is significantly cheaper than AMD, suggesting it may have greater upside potential once the market rewards it with a premium valuation. This is probably why Nvidia's median 12-month price target of $318 implies a potential jump of 88%, higher than AMD's projected 12-month gains of 26%.

So, Nvidia looks like an ideal investment for investors seeking a chip stock trading at attractive levels. However, they won't go wrong with AMD either, as its accelerating growth will help it justify the valuation and send the stock higher in 2027 and beyond.

#3010. The Hidden Risks In Wealth Platform Migrations

Sharat Priya is a Senior Manager in EY’s U.S. Wealth & Asset Management Technology practice, leading wealth platform modernization.

The best migration is the one your clients never notice. Clients should see the benefits, not the disruption.

​Wealth platform migrations rarely fail on conversion weekend. The cutover finishes, reports turn green and problems appear later once real activity begins. A tax lot shows the wrong cost basis. A sweep runs an hour late. An outside manager calls to ask why a trade file never arrived.

In my 22 years in wealth technology, I’ve found that the most serious migration risks are often the least visible. They’re easy to miss during testing and only show up once the platform is live. Knowing where to look makes the difference.

Copying records from one database to another is the easy part. The real work is transforming them so they behave accurately under the new platform’s business logic. That takes business transformation rules, and a business owner must sign off on each of these mapping/transformation rules.

Critical data includes client and account records, relationships, standing instructions, transactions, cash balances, positions and tax lots. Even a single tax lot migrated incorrectly can change a client’s realized gain or loss—and with it, their tax obligation.

Complex instruments should be looked at more carefully. For mortgage- and asset-backed securities, check original face, current face and paydown factors. For Treasury Inflation-Protected Securities (TIPS), confirm the index ratio and inflation-adjusted principal. For pooled funds, verify units and unit values. Then run accretion, amortization, inflation adjustments, interest and paydowns through a full cycle and compare against the old system. These errors don’t appear on day one. They appear on the first factor date.

Every legacy platform holds years of settings: firm and region structures, users and entitlements, fee rules and schedules, ACH and wire cutoffs, sweep run times, corporate action recipients and approval thresholds. Most of it lives in the system, and very little of it is documented anywhere.

An inventory of such configurations should be prepared and compared line by line. Then decide what carries over, what changes and what goes away. Get entitlements wrong, and users can see client data they shouldn’t, which is a regulatory problem. Miss one approval step, and a large transaction executes without sign-off.

Before cutover, map each function on the old platform to its place on the new one. Some map directly. Some need a workaround. Some get retired. The gaps that cause problems are usually small, such as a custom fee discount or a report operation run every month.

New capabilities need the same attention: data, configuration and testing before they’re switched on.

A new platform changes who does what. For example, in one of the implementations I’ve seen, back-office operations moved to the platform provider. That shift needs a close look at what the firm still owns and which teams, roles, features, reports and controls change hands.

Then map current and future ownership; a RACI matrix helps. When no one owns the exception queue, unresolved items pile up, and clients are often the first to notice.

Vendor manuals and job aids explain the platform, not the use cases and custom scenarios specific to your firm. They should be tailored to the firm and built for the new operating model.

Timing can vary, but they must be ready before go-live so that staff know the new process on day one.

Controls don’t migrate automatically. Each existing control needs to be reviewed and set up again on the new platform.

Inventory current controls, including those covered in SOC reports. Decide which ones to retain, which ones to retire and which ones the new platform handles differently. Then enable any new ones it offers. Engage risk, compliance and internal audit early.

Advisors use client statements and performance reports. Operations uses exception reports. Finance uses billing reports, and compliance uses regulatory reports.

List the reports each team uses currently, and check how the new platform will produce each one: built-in, from a data warehouse or not at all. Run important reports on both platforms until the numbers match. Client statements must be right from day one.​

Integrations don’t carry equal risk. I sort them into three groups.

Core integrations, such as CRM, financial planning and trading systems, shape the advisor’s working day. External manager integrations matter wherever a third-party manager holds investment authority. Models, trade files and position feeds must keep flowing, and many of these relationships are shared with the firm’s clients, so a broken feed quickly becomes a client conversation.

Market data is the third group. Know which vendors will supply pricing, reference and corporate action data, and whether those feeds come ready-made or need new build and licensing work.

For each integration, know the owner, the testing window and the client sensitivity. Engage third parties early; they have their own release schedules.

Sometimes, a firm changes its custodian, clearing broker or cash agent during a platform migration. Find this out early.

In such cases, the firm may need to plan for client communication, updated account agreements and new reconciliation processes. These scenarios may also need additional steps during the conversion event.

Reconciliation is how the firm confirms that its records match those of its custodians and cash agents. It needs to work from day one.

Decide what will be reconciled (positions, transactions and cash), how often and what differences are acceptable. Decide who owns it: internal operations, the platform vendor or an outsourced provider. Set clear rules and tolerances for how long a mismatch can stay open before it is escalated.​

A migration isn’t just a technology project. It changes how the firm operates. Firms that get it right give each area a clear owner and test through full processing cycles so that the platform is accurate and meets the firm’s objectives before the go-live decision is made.

The best migration is the one your clients never notice. Clients should see the benefits, not the disruption.

#3008. Top Wall Street analysts like these 3 dividend stocks for steady income

Stock markets remain volatile as investors weigh uncertainty in the Middle East, rising interest rates and concerns about the artificial intelligence (AI) trade.

Given this volatile backdrop, investors seeking a stable income stream can enhance their portfolios with the addition of dividend stocks. In this regard, tracking recommendations of top Wall Street analysts can help shortlist attractive dividend stocks that are backed by strong financials.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Integrated oil and gas giant Chevron (CVX) is the first dividend pick. Earlier this month, the company paid a quarterly dividend of $1.78 per share. At an annualized dividend of $7.12 per share, CVX offers a dividend yield of about 3.5%.

After a meeting with senior leadership, Goldman Sachs analyst Neil Mehta reiterated a buy rating on CVX and raised his price target to $240 from $225. Among the key takeaways Mehta emphasized was that Chevron is building a solid pipeline of promising international exploration projects across Latin America, the Middle East, West Africa and the Eastern Mediterranean. In particular, he mentioned the focus on Venezuela, where Chevron expects to more than double gross production to 600 kbd from about 280 kbd by 2031, using three joint ventures.

Mehta also noted Chevron's efforts to deploy new technologies, especially in shale and tight-oil operations. CVX is focused on boosting productivity and efficiency to maximize free cash flow, he added.

Initiatives such as artificial lift optimization, AI and machine learning, and advanced chemical treatments are expected to improve recovery by about 10% in new wells. Consequently, capital spending per barrel in U.S. shale is projected to fall 25% in 2026, with total Permian spending dropping below $3.5 billion.

Mehta also said that Chevron sees power as a growth opportunity and is using its U.S. natural gas portfolio, key equipment and partnerships to meet rising demand. Notably, Chevron recently signed a 20-year power purchase deal with Microsoft (MSFT) to supply 2.67 GW of behind-the-meter capacity. This project, called Kilby, is expected to see its first power delivery in 2028 and generate mid-teens returns and long-term cash flows.

Mehta ranks No. 536 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 61% of the time, delivering an average return of 11.3%. See Chevron Statistics on TipRanks.

Enterprise Products Partners (EPD) is a master limited partnership (MLP) that provides midstream energy services to producers and consumers of natural gas, natural gas liquids (NGLs), crude oil, refined products and petrochemicals. With a quarterly cash distribution of 56 cents per common unit ($2.24 per common unit annualized), EPD offers a yield of about 6%.

Following a recent call with the company, RBC Capital analyst Elvira Scotto reiterated a buy rating on EPD with a price target of $42. The analyst slightly lowered her estimates for the second half of 2026, citing margin and volume normalization, though demand dynamics remain strong.

While the 5-star analyst expects a sequential decline due to normal seasonality and normalization following high spreads in the first half of the year, she still expects EPD to report another solid quarter (Q3 2026) and enter 2027 with a favorable setup. Ahead of Q3 earnings, Scotto expects investors to focus on export demand durability, EPD's ability to profit from spot cargo sales and growth in Permian volumes.

Scotto continues to expect $150 million of common unit buybacks per quarter in the second half of 2026. She expects buybacks to increase to $200 million per quarter in 2027, as capex spending declines and free cash flow improves.

In total, Scotto is bullish on EPD and views it as a core MLP holding that has both offensive and defensive characteristics. The analyst praised EPD's diverse asset base as ensuring stable cash flows. "Furthermore, the partnership's multi-year organic growth backlog helps provide visibility on long-term distribution growth," Scotto said.

Scotto ranks No. 199 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. Her ratings have been successful 67% of the time, delivering an average return of 15.9%. See Enterprise Products Partners Financials on TipRanks.

Brookfield Infrastructure Partners (BIP) operates a diversified portfolio of high-quality utilities, transport, midstream and data assets. The company declared a quarterly distribution of 45.5 cents per unit, payable on September 29. At an annualized distribution of $1.82, BIP offers a yield of 5.2%.

Recently, BMO Capital analyst Devin Dodge reaffirmed a buy rating on Brookfield Infrastructure with a price target of $47. The analyst cited improved visibility into BIP's growth and views its corporate simplification as a potential catalyst.

"Layering in the benefits from the proposed corporate simplification, a multiple well below historical levels and an attractive yield, we believe BIP offers a compelling risk/reward and it remains a preferred idea," said Dodge.

With more organic growth projects in the pipeline and new partnership investment opportunities, Dodge believes that BIP has clearer visibility into where it will deploy capital. That should support continued double-digit growth in funds from operations (FFO) per unit.

Dodge said the semiconductor foundries that BIP is developing with Intel (INTC) are expected to be fully commissioned by the end of 2026, with returns improving through Q4 2026. Dodge expects the joint venture to boost FFO by 300-400 basis points.

Dodge ranks No. 476 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 68% of the time, delivering an average return of 13.5%. See Brookfield Infrastructure Partners KPIs on TipRanks.

#3005. History Says Investors Who Make This 1 Move During Bear Markets Always Win Out

It sounds counterintuitive, but doing nothing might be the best thing you can do.

When stock prices are falling, investors usually react in one of two ways. They decide to move to cash and wait for things to get "better," or they consider buying the dips. The problem is that both strategies require you to make a prediction, and predictions are often wrong.

Investors can't predict the future, so there's really no sense in even trying it. Staying the course regardless of what the market's doing in the short term is usually the best thing to do. That includes keeping your automatic investments right on schedule.

Unfortunately, many investors react emotionally to market developments. They see their investments declining in value, and their instinct is to stop the bleeding before it gets worse. The problem is that nobody knows exactly the right time to get in or out.

Investors usually sell only after losses have already occurred, and then buy back in only after the rebound is well underway. In either case, it usually ends up damaging long-term returns.

Buying the dip can seem wise in practice, but it still carries risks because it requires you to pick an entry point. If you overthink it, you might wait too long or not long enough. Either way, it requires you to decide what the best time is to trade.

The best idea, as it turns out, might be to do nothing at all.

Whether it's into a workplace retirement plan, such as a 401(k), or just a regular brokerage account, maintaining your automatic investment schedule is the best way to take advantage of volatile markets.

It requires no timing, no market watching, and really no action whatsoever. Continuing to invest at regular intervals allows you to buy the dips whenever they occur or accumulate long-term wealth when stock prices are near highs. Fixed contributions help lower your overall cost basis and improve your overall returns.

Some of the best options for this strategy are the Vanguard S&P 500 ETF (VOO -0.58%) or the Vanguard Total Stock Market ETF (VTI -0.59%). But really, any diversified equity or fixed-income fund is ideal for this. The key to success is avoiding the temptation to time the market and pick your entry and exit points. By focusing on long-term wealth accumulation instead of short-term market machinations, you have a much greater chance of riding out and even taking advantage of bear markets when they arrive.

#3003. Lazio e Roma, undici gare senza ko: l’Inter guida il differenziale xG

La squadra di Chivu registra un valore di +18,20; i biancocelesti segnano più di quanto producano secondo l’indicatore.

Lazio e Roma sono ancora imbattute dopo undici giornate e, nell’analisi della corsa scudetto, l’Inter appare la rivale più attrezzata per inseguirle. Secondo quanto riportato da Il Messaggero, I nerazzurri uniscono nove vittorie, un pareggio e una sconfitta al miglior differenziale di expected goals del campionato.

CLICCA QUI E SEGUI LAZIOPRESS.IT ANCHE SU INSTAGRAM

Le squadre di Gattuso e Gasperini hanno raggiunto il vertice attraverso percorsi diversi. Milan e Como restano nella corsa, mentre Juventus e Napoli attraversano difficoltà che, al momento, rendono più complicato considerarle antagoniste delle due romane.

Il primato delle due squadre della Capitale non era atteso in questi termini. Gasperini e Gattuso propongono un calcio per alcuni aspetti simile, pur guidando formazioni diverse. Entrambe sono rimaste imbattute nel confronto con le grandi del Nord e con la grande del Sud, trasformandosi in protagoniste della Serie A nel giro di undici giornate.

È la prima volta nella storia della Serie A che due club della stessa città restano imbattuti per undici partite. I loro percorsi differenti hanno prodotto lo stesso risultato in classifica. Alle loro spalle, però, le inseguitrici attendono scontri diretti e possibili passi falsi: anche gli infortuni possono pesare sulla tenuta delle squadre in vetta.

Il quadro comprende la Juventus, lontana dalle attese, e il Napoli, campione d’Italia due volte nelle ultime tre stagioni ma ora a 14 punti dalla capolista. Il Milan è invece a tre lunghezze dalla Roma e a cinque dalla Lazio.

Tra le candidate a contrastare il primato romano, l’Inter emerge per risultati, organico e guida tecnica. La squadra di Chivu ha segnato 28 gol: soltanto la Roma, con 31, ha fatto meglio. Il dato più significativo nell’analisi delle occasioni è però il differenziale di expected goals, che confronta la qualità delle opportunità create con quella delle occasioni concesse.

Secondo i dati di xgEdge, l’Inter guida questa graduatoria con +18,20 xG, circa sei punti più della Roma e un valore quasi cinque volte quello della Lazio. La squadra di Pio Esposito produce dunque occasioni pericolose in misura nettamente superiore a quelle che lascia agli avversari, al di là dei risultati già ottenuti.

La Roma segue a quota +12; vengono poi Como, Milan e Lazio. I biancocelesti rappresentano un caso particolare: segnano più di quanto producano secondo questo indicatore. La squadra di Gattuso sfrutta le opportunità offensive e ne ricava punti.

Il Milan di Amorim resta vicino alle romane, ma deve fare a meno di Gabbia, Tomori e Saelemaekers, tre elementi importanti della rosa, oltre a Chukwueze e Loftus-Cheek. Il Como, costruito per salire in classifica, è l’unica italiana tra le prime otto in Champions League. In campionato, però, ha incontrato difficoltà contro Inter e Frosinone.

Dopo la sconfitta per 2-0, Fabregas ha riconosciuto la superiorità dei nerazzurri. La sua squadra dispone di individualità forti, ma deve ancora accumulare esperienza per competere per lo scudetto. Chivu, invece, attende un passo falso delle romane per tornare capolista.

#3002. Fantastic News for CoreWeave Stock Investors

Wall Street may have been wrong about the company after all.

Despite starting the year on a strong note, shares of CoreWeave (CRWV -13.91%) are down by nearly 13% year-to-date, as of writing. What's going on with the company? On the one hand, CoreWeave is cashing in on massive demand for artificial intelligence (AI) compute. However, there are several issues with the company, including the massive debt it is now carrying on its balance sheet. That said, CoreWeave recently reported its third-quarter results, and they were full of bullish signs for the company. Let's dig in.

The market had high expectations for CoreWeave ahead of its third-quarter earnings, and the neocloud company blew them out of the water. Revenue increased by 168% year over year to $3.7 billion. The top line grew much faster than operating expenses, resulting in strong growth in operating income.

CoreWeave's operating income for the period was $207 million, substantially better than the $52 million recorded in the year-ago period. Its operating margin also increased to 6%, up from the 4% reported in the prior-year quarter. CoreWeave remains unprofitable, and the net loss per share widened to $1.27 in the period, compared to $0.22 in the prior-year quarter.

However, the fact that CoreWeave's revenue growth accelerated for the first time in a while suggests there is still strong demand for the company's services, which helps justify the massive spending contributing to the worsening bottom-line numbers. We can also look at CoreWeave's revenue backlog, which was $132.5 billion for the period, up 138% year over year.

CoreWeave also raised its guidance for the fiscal year 2026. It now expects revenue between $13 billion and $13.3 billion, up from its previous range of $12.4 billion to $13.2 billion. The company is now also projecting it will finish the year with a revenue run rate of $20 billion to $21 billion; CoreWeave's trailing-12-month revenue is $9.89 billion. It's not surprising that the stock soared on the heels of another excellent quarterly performance.

Can CoreWeave maintain its momentum? The company faces at least three major risks. First, during the recent U.S. midterm elections, voters overwhelmingly elected officials who ran on pumping the brakes on data center expansion. If regulatory uncertainty in the AI industry increases over the next few years, it could weigh on CoreWeave's financial results.

Second, the company's significant debt and net losses remain an issue. Third, there is customer concentration. According to some reports, Microsoft (MSFT +0.10%) could opt to work with fewer neocloud partners. This single customer accounted for about 67% of CoreWeave's revenue last year, so if Microsoft cuts ties with CoreWeave, it would be catastrophic for the company.

Can CoreWeave perform well despite these problems? In my view, it could, although the stock remains somewhat risky. Consider the first risk. CoreWeave's management does not expect a significant impact from increased regulatory scrutiny on the business. And the company has also been building capacity outside the U.S., which helps mitigate its regulatory exposure in the country.

Second, CoreWeave's significant debt is necessary for the company to continue meeting demand for its services. In fact, CoreWeave is largely sold out through 2027, according to management, while there is already strong demand for 2028. What about customer concentration? If Microsoft decides to work with fewer neoclouds, it could be very bad for CoreWeave, or it could be very good.

CoreWeave is one of the largest neoclouds, and if it remains in Microsoft's smaller list of partners as the tech giant continues to demand more services, that would mean a higher budget distributed among fewer providers, potentially leading to higher revenue for CoreWeave. Customer concentration remains a meaningful risk for investors to monitor, but for now, CoreWeave appears likely to continue performing well despite it. And even after its post-earnings jump, the stock looks attractive, particularly for investors comfortable with volatility.

#2998. Scandal Forces Jensen Huang Out at Nvidia. Here's How Investors Should Consider Nvidia Stock Now.

What makes Nvidia hard to beat doesn’t live in the corner office.

Nvidia (NVDA -13.63%) has spent the past several years establishing itself as the dominant supplier of artificial intelligence processors. Now the company faces a problem that has nothing to do with its chips, its competition, or the enormous demand for AI computing power.

It's a problem of replacing its founder and CEO, Jensen Huang.

Huang resigned Thursday from Nvidia, four days after Nvidia's board of directors opened an independent investigation into reports that a foreign intelligence operation had targeted Huang. The announcement followed Huang's admission that he had been unfaithful to his wife, raising questions about his relationship with a woman suspected of working for Chinese intelligence.

Nvidia stock fell more than 12% in premarket trading on Thursday.

It is an extraordinary development for one of the world's most influential technology executives. And it raises a legitimate question for investors: How should investors evaluate Nvidia stock now that Huang is no longer at the helm?

Jensen Huang has resigned as Nvidia CEO. Image source: Getty Images.

According to reporting by The New York Times and other news organizations, U.S. intelligence officials suspect that Huang had a relationship with Alice Huang, a Chinese businesswoman and former executive at Singapore-based Megaspeed International, who was recruited to gather information for Beijing.

The allegations have not been independently established, and China has rejected them. In addition, The New York Times reported that Huang had a separate affair with a Nvidia employee.

Kress, who is stepping in as interim CEO, has been Nvidia's executive vice president and chief financial officer since she joined the company in September 2013. Nvidia also named its first-ever board chairman, Harvey Jones, a former CEO of Synopsys.

"The board has full confidence in Colette and the leadership team, and we expect the company's operations, customer commitments and product roadmap to continue without interruption," Jones said.

Back in July, I wrote about the three "Magnificent Seven" companies that remained founder-led: Nvidia, Meta Platforms, and Tesla. I argued that Nvidia was the best long-term investment of the three, largely because of Huang's strategic vision and the competitive advantages his company has built.

Huang co-founded Nvidia in 1993, initially focusing on graphics processors for video games. But one of his most consequential decisions was to create CUDA, a software platform that enabled developers to program Nvidia's graphics processing units for applications far beyond gaming.

That decision helped establish Nvidia's dominance in AI computing.

Today, Nvidia's advantage extends beyond its processors. The combination of the company's networking technology, CUDA platform, and integrated computing systems makes it difficult for its established customers to switch to competing hardware platforms.

Those advantages weren't created overnight, and they won't disappear overnight either.

Huang deserves enormous credit for building Nvidia into the company it is today. But thousands of engineers, researchers, and executives are responsible for developing its products and maintaining its customer relationships.

The company's technological expertise doesn't reside exclusively in the CEO's office.

There's plenty of precedent for successful technology companies prospering after their founders step aside.

Steve Jobs was instrumental in turning Apple into a consumer technology powerhouse. But under the 15-year leadership of Tim Cook, Apple quadrupled its annual revenue, expanded its services business, and became one of the world's most valuable companies. The company is still going strong today with new CEO John Ternus at the helm.

Then there's Microsoft: Bill Gates relinquished the CEO position in 2000, but Microsoft kept moving forward. Under Satya Nadella, who became CEO in 2014, Microsoft became a dominant cloud computing provider and a major force in artificial intelligence.

Jeff Bezos is still synonymous with the company he founded, Amazon, but it has been five years since he handed over the CEO role to Andy Jassy. Under Jassy, Amazon has grown its cloud computing, advertising, and retail businesses, and the company's market cap is approaching $3 trillion.

There's no doubt that Huang's technical expertise, customer relationships, and influence over the company's direction will be difficult to replicate. But a company's long-term success clearly doesn't have to depend on one individual, even when that individual founded it.

The investigations into this situation introduce uncertainty that shareholders shouldn't dismiss. Beyond the change in leadership, any evidence of compromised information or violations of U.S. export restrictions could have serious consequences. But there is currently no established evidence that Nvidia's secrets were compromised.

Meanwhile, demand for AI computing remains the central driver of Nvidia's business. Its customers still need powerful processors, and competitors still face the difficult task of matching Nvidia's vaunted product line.

"Nvidia's strength has always come from its people, and together with our leadership team, I am committed to building on the extraordinary foundation Jensen created over the last 33 years," Kress said in a statement released by Nvidia today. "We will continue advancing the work that has made Nvidia a leader in accelerated computing and AI, and execute on the opportunities ahead. Our focus remains on serving our customers, partners, developers and shareholders."

I still think Nvidia is an outstanding company to own for the long term. The board's investigation deserves attention, and its findings could change the assessment of leadership risk.

For now, however, my investment thesis remains intact. Nvidia's greatest asset isn't any one executive, even Jensen Huang. It's the business he helped build, and will continue without him.

#2996. Top Wall Street analysts are upbeat about the prospects for these 3 stocks

September ended on a mixed note, with the S&P 500 and Dow Jones Industrial Average falling 0.5% and 4.3%, respectively, while the Nasdaq Composite climbed 1.9%. Markets remained on edge with higher oil prices and Treasury yields weighing on investor sentiment and triggering fears of additional interest rate hikes.

In this climate, investors with a long-term horizon can track recommendations from top Wall Street analysts to find stocks with solid growth prospects.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Neocloud company CoreWeave (CRWV) is benefiting from robust AI-driven demand for cloud computing.

Recently, JPMorgan analyst Samik Chatterjee upgraded CoreWeave to buy from hold, raising his price target to $125 from $120. The analyst cited favorable pricing, driven by solid demand, for the upgrade.

Chatterjee said CoreWeave is now more willing to sign premium priced, shorter-term contracts, which lift his revenue and margin estimates. CRWV stock performance has been underwhelming as investors worry about high capital spending, the analyst noted. That said, he believes that higher prices and increased margins will more than offset the rise in debt to fund capital spending, improving the stock's valuation.

Chatterjee noted the 25% price hike in July across CoreWeave's product offerings and frequent price increases by rivals like Nebius (NBIS), adding that some peers are charging nearly three times more for short-term compute contracts than what CoreWeave charges on longer-term deals.

"CoreWeave's leverage of the opportunity [is] evident in its recent press release highlighting contracts signed in F3Q at ~$40M/MW," said Chatterjee. JPMorgan said higher pricing is already contributing 5-10 percentage points to contract margins versus earlier deals.

Chatterjee ranks No. 4 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 75% of the time, delivering an average return of 55.3%. See CoreWeave Statistics on TipRanks.

Palo Alto Networks (PANW) is a provider of cybersecurity solutions across network, cloud, security operations, AI and identity. Following a discussion with management, BTIG analyst Gray Powell reiterated a buy rating on PANW and raised his price target to $425 from $404.

After a review of Palo Alto's product-level growth catalysts, Powell sees clear upside to both revenue and next-generation security (NGS) annual recurring revenue (ARR) in fiscal 2027. He expects PANW to deliver pro forma revenue growth of more than 17%, above the consensus estimate of 14.6%, and NGS ARR growth of more than 26% versus the Street's expectation of 24.5%.

Over the past five years, the Palo Alto business mix, has moved to higher-growth product areas and new markets. from what was earlier viewed as a hardware-centric network security company, Powell said. The 5-star analyst estimates that hardware firewalls and attached subscriptions now account for less than 50% of revenue.

Meanwhile, high-growth solutions, including Prisma SASE, software firewalls, Cortex XSIAM, Chronosphere and Idira now represent more than 35% of PANW's revenue. "Investors may not fully realize the growth potential within PANW's platform story," Powell said.

BTIG is optimistic about the prospects of Chronosphere in observability, CyberArk in identity and software firewalls and Prisma AIRS in the network and AI security business.

Powell ranks No. 320 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 61% of the time, delivering an average return of 17.3%. See Palo Alto Networks KPIs on TipRanks.

In a research note on September 30, Rosenblatt analyst Scott Devitt reiterated a buy rating on e-commerce and cloud computing platform Amazon (AMZN), raising his price target to $360 from $335. Fears that agentic commerce poses a major threat to Amazon's advertising business seem "overstated," he said.

Devitt noted that the growing adoption of personal agents and AI shopping assistants has sparked concern about displacement of Amazon's retail media business. But the 5-star analyst remains bullish, given Amazon's ability to adapt to changing consumer behavior, as it's done in the past.

Additionally, Devitt argues that even if an AI agent helps a shopper discover a product, Amazon can still handle the purchase and delivery, keeping much of the transaction value. He highlighted that management believes that existing Amazon shoppers will likely continue to start their shopping journey on the platform because it has better personalization, accurate pricing and inventory information that third-party agents still lack.

Overall, Devitt expects Amazon's retail business to prove a net beneficiary of agentic commerce. Fewer clicks would not hurt the company if the value of each remaining click and overall volumes keep rising, he said. The argument is that even if Amazon loses some sponsored ad revenue over the next few years, it would likely be only a small part of the company's total business and could be offset by growth in other areas.

"Consistent with our AI framework, we continue to view Amazon as one of the most resilient leaders in our coverage, combining owned compute infrastructure with a physical fulfillment moat," said Devitt. His bullish view is supported, Devitt said, by expectations that Amazon Web Services (AWS) will exit the year at a more than 45% growth rate with continued margin improvement.

Devitt ranks No. 765 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 49% of the time, delivering an average return of 12%. See Amazon Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

#2994. DPC Holdings (DPC) Q3 2026 Earnings Call Transcript

Five long-term partnerships and a Montana Precision stake shape the growth story.

Need a quote from a Motley Fool analyst? Email [email protected]

DPC Holdings PLC (DPC +4.36%) reported record financial results for the third quarter of 2026, led by a 39% increase in revenue and a 56% rise in adjusted EBITDA. Management attributed the performance to strong demand in the aerospace and industrial gas turbine (IGT) end markets, which both grew over 40% during the period. The company emphasized its strategic transition toward long-term customer partnerships, announcing a fifth major agreement that includes significant customer funding for capacity expansion. These partnerships, combined with the acquisition of a 50% stake in Montana Precision Products, are intended to provide long-term revenue visibility and enhance returns on invested capital. The divestment of the noncore Ivostud business further focuses the portfolio on high-growth engine products and superalloys.

#2990. Alphabet and Meta Platforms Pay Dividends. Is Either of Them Worth Owning for Income?

Both of these internet heavyweights announced quarterly dividends in the first half of 2024.

Both Alphabet (GOOGL +0.68%) (GOOG +0.38%) and Meta Platforms (META +0.01%) instituted a notable change to their capital allocation policies in 2024. The social media giant moved first. In February of that year, it introduced a $0.50 quarterly dividend. The Google parent followed suit, announcing a quarterly payout of $0.20 in April 2024.

These companies have been wonderful to their shareholders over the years. Both have outperformed the S&P 500 index in the past decade. Capital appreciation was the main driver of returns in investment portfolios.

But now that dividends are a mainstay, do either of these "Magnificent Seven" stocks make sense for investors seeking income-generating opportunities?

These companies have raised their dividends in the past couple of years. Alphabet currently pays $0.22 per share, up 10% from the initial distribution. Meta Platforms' dividend is up 5% from the beginning to $0.525 today. While the growth is certainly small, these leadership teams are at least hinting to shareholders that slowly increasing the payouts might be a permanent feature.

There is minimal risk that these dividends come under any pressure. Both businesses have historically operated from a position of financial robustness. In the past five years, Alphabet's quarterly operating margin averaged a fantastic 30.4%. At 36.4%, Meta Platforms' average margin is even better. And combined, they have $67.5 billion in cash and cash equivalents on their balance sheets.

Dividends are well covered by their earnings streams. Through the first nine months of 2026, Alphabet's net income was almost 25 times higher than its dividends, while Meta Platforms' bottom line was nearly 13 times greater than what it paid in distributions to shareholders. From this vantage point, it looks like the payouts are safe.

However, investors shouldn't rush to buy these businesses if all they're after is a meaningful income stream. The dividend yields are tiny. Alphabet, at a dividend yield of 0.31%, and Meta Platforms, at 0.33%, aren't attracting any attention in this regard. These figures are significantly lower than the inflation rate.

There are superior choices out there. The Vanguard S&P 500 ETF, for example, offers a much better dividend yield of 1.07%. More stable consumer stocks, like Coca-Cola, Procter & Gamble, and Altria Group, pay investors sizable amounts. And it's hard to ignore the 10-year Treasury, which currently yields 5%.

Given how small their dividend yields are, Alphabet and Meta Platforms don't exactly cater to income seekers. On the other hand, they still target investors who want to add growth opportunities to their portfolios. These businesses offer the potential for market-beating returns over the next five or 10 years.

Their scale is not an inhibitor. Alphabet posted 25% year-over-year revenue growth in Q3, similar to Meta Platforms' 26% gain. This points to long expansionary runways ahead. These companies are direct beneficiaries of the internet's proliferation.

These management teams have been directing their time and resources to artificial intelligence (AI), which is sucking up capital that might otherwise have gone to raising dividend payouts. Both Alphabet and Meta Platforms will invest hundreds of billions of dollars this year in technical infrastructure to expand compute capacity and support their AI initiatives.

And they're raising external funding to power these ambitions, in turn pausing share buybacks, which historically played a key role in returning capital to investors. Free cash flow has also turned negative. This development will test the market's patience because nobody knows the kinds of returns these substantial AI-related investments will bring. It's impossible to predict when the data center spending boom will peak or who will capture the excess profits.

On an earnings basis, however, these two leading technology stocks trade at very compelling valuations. Market sentiment reflects elevated levels of uncertainty.

When our analyst team has a stock tip, it can pay to listen. After all, Stock Advisor's total average return is 835%* — a market-crushing outperformance compared to 208% for the S&P 500.

They just revealed what they believe are the 10 best stocks for investors to buy right now, available when you join Stock Advisor.

#2989. Lazio di Gattuso, 29 punti in 11 giornate: è record biancoceleste

I biancocelesti guidano la classifica nonostante l’Olimpico vuoto per la protesta dei tifosi contro Lotito.

La Lazio è prima in classifica con 29 punti dopo undici giornate: non aveva mai raggiunto questa quota nella sua storia. Secondo quanto riportato da il Giornale, La squadra di Gattuso ha superato il precedente record di 28 punti, stabilito nel 2017-18, dopo un inizio di stagione accompagnato dallo scetticismo.

CLICCA QUI E SEGUI LAZIOPRESS.IT ANCHE SU INSTAGRAM

All’Olimpico, dove la protesta contro il presidente Lotito lascia vuoti gli spalti, i biancocelesti hanno conquistato 13 punti sui 15 disponibili. Il rendimento in trasferta è di 16 punti su 18: la squadra sembra non risentire dell’assenza del pubblico nelle gare casalinghe.

Il primato arriva dopo due settimi posti e un nono posto. Gattuso ha trasformato quelle delusioni, insieme al fallimento della propria esperienza alla guida della Nazionale italiana, in una forte spinta motivazionale per la squadra.

La Lazio continua a sorprendere con i suoi risultati. Ha battuto 3-0 la Juventus a Torino, poi ha rimontato il Cagliari e ha conquistato punti anche a Napoli.

Contro il Napoli i biancocelesti hanno dovuto fare i conti con assenze importanti: Zaccagni, Gudmundsson, Rovella, Cataldi e Marusic. Durante la partita si sono fermati anche Sutalo e Tavares.

#2979. Junk bonds are 'flashing yellow.' Watch these warning signs

Cracks are forming in the junk bond market as investors demand higher payouts for owning the market's riskiest debt. It isn't time to ditch high-yield bonds, but investors should pay attention to the warning signs.

High-yield bonds now yield 8.1%, up from 7.22% a month ago. The increase reflects a jump in yields across the curve as investors bake in more inflation from high energy prices and other pressures, including concern about the deficit — hitting nearly $2 trillion in the fiscal year that ended Sept. 30.

The high-yield market is also showing stress on the credit side with spreads recently widening to levels not seen since April, according to the Federal Reserve Bank of St. Louis. Credit spreads are the difference in yield between the bonds and Treasurys of similar maturities. Wider spreads mean investors are demanding higher yields for holding corporate debt, viewing it as riskier.

Spreads are at 315 basis points in the overall high-yield market, higher than a year ago but still below levels in March when they reached 346 bps. One basis point equals one one-hundredth of a percent, or 0.01%.

The high-yield market consists of bonds rated BB+ by S&P and Fitch and Ba1 and under by Moody's. The lowest-rated cohort, CCC and below, has seen the most movement with spreads climbing dramatically over the past year to roughly 1,250 bps.

Right now, the high-yield market is "flashing yellow" but is "far from red," said Michael Arone, chief investment strategist at State Street Investment Management.

It makes sense that investors are demanding more compensation for taking on additional credit risk as borrowing costs rise, he said.

Yields are elevated across the board with the 10-year Treasury reaching its highest level since 2002 earlier in the week.

"The bigger question is whether this is simply a repricing of interest rate risk, or the beginning of a more fundamental reassessment of credit quality," Arone said.

He's in a wait-and-see mode since earnings are still growing, interest-coverage ratios remain good, and while default rates have ticked up some, he believes it is not concerning.

That said, the starting point in spreads is likely weighing on investors' psyche, since they are still low by historical standards.

"There's a small margin of error here, which I also think raises the anxiety level," Arone explained. "The compensation that investors are receiving for taking on this credit risk isn't overwhelming relative to history, and therefore subtle changes in credit spreads can be concerning."

While there may be concerns about part of the lower-rated market, the overall high-yield market is in good fundamental shape.

In fact, credit quality is at a record high, with BB bonds making up over 60% of the market compared with 38% prior to the global financial crisis, said Kelley Gerrity, a fixed income strategist at Morgan Stanley Investment Management.

"We've had higher-quality companies coming in, and with higher rates now, you also have more discipline from companies that are more indebted ... just because of the higher cost of capital, so that actually is creating a bit of a healthier picture as well," she said.

The lowest tier of high yield has always been more speculative, since the bonds have a higher default risk. Therefore, it's no surprise that they are the ones seeing the most widening in spreads, said Collin Martin, head of fixed income research and strategy at the Schwab Center for Financial Research.

The overall move in high-yield spreads have been "orderly," he said. "Are there cracks forming? I think the cracks are logical cracks with the lowest-rated bonds and it's too early to say that it's spreading to the broad credit market."

In addition, fluctuations within the CCC-rated and lower spreads have been idiosyncratic, Gerrity added. Morgan Stanley recently broke down the spreads in the lowest cohort into two buckets: performing assets and non-performing assets, which are defined as spreads above 1,000 bps.

The non-performing spread to worst is 2,818 bps, while the performing bucket — the biggest piece of the CCC market — is 461 bps, she said.

"It is isolated," she said. "I'm not necessarily sure that that will go away anytime soon, but it doesn't tell the story of overall concern and flashing caution for credit markets right now."

For now, investors should remain selective within higher yield, Gerrity advised.

"We're looking for the best relative value opportunities, and we think that exists within that single-B cohort, and so we're leaning into that," she said.

Investors should be concerned if there is a steep widening in the broader high-yield market.

But there's scant evidence of stress in the BB cohort. Spreads in that group are at 194 bps, up from 179 bps a year ago — although the move hasn't been in a straight line.

"We're going to be focusing our attention on what are considered some stronger businesses," Martin said. "If we start to see the markets demand higher spreads there also, that's what we'll be looking at to see if risks are really rising."

It's also important to understand that the Federal Reserve is raising rates in a strong economic environment, said R.J. Gallo, chief investment officer of global fixed income at Federated Hermes. The central bank is concerned about an inflation problem amid high fossil fuel prices due to the Iran war, while growth has been stronger than expected, he noted.

"With growth being good as the reason that the Fed is hiking, then you wouldn't expect high yield to blow out because growth means revenues stay up, means cash flow and profitability stays up," he explained.

High yield becomes a disaster when the economy is heading into a sharp economic downturn, Gallo said.

"That's when spreads really widen out," he said. "But a recession is not the odds-on bet. Now, talk to me in six months if the Fed keeps hiking over and over, if oil prices stay high for longer, well, then maybe we will start to wonder."

#2977. FIGC, Galliani e Malagò: «un’elezione nuovamente annullabile»

Il legale Lattanzi chiede di sospendere il provvedimento: le verifiche tornerebbero al Segretario Generale.

Adriano Galliani ha presentato ricorso al Tribunale Federale Nazionale della FIGC contro l’istituzione della commissione incaricata di esaminare le candidature alla presidenza federale. Secondo quanto riportato da Onefootball, La commissione è stata voluta dal commissario Giovanni Malagò, a sua volta in corsa. La contestazione riguarda anche il sostegno del Genoa: se la firma del club a favore di Malagò fosse annullata, il commissario non raggiungerebbe la quota minima richiesta tra le società di Serie A.

CLICCA QUI E SEGUI LAZIOPRESS.IT ANCHE SU INSTAGRAM

Il ricorso mette in discussione l’affidamento delle verifiche alla commissione istituita su iniziativa di Malagò. I dettagli, riferiti da Gianluca Di Marzio e ripresi dall’ANSA, sono riportati da Onefootball.

Secondo il legale Lattanzi, che in mattinata ha rassegnato le dimissioni dalla commissione agenti FIGC, il ricorso richiede un intervento prima che il procedimento elettorale prosegua. L’eventuale annullamento del sostegno del Genoa a Malagò avrebbe un effetto sulla sua candidatura: senza quella firma, il commissario non arriverebbe alle undici sottoscrizioni minime dei club di Serie A. Il termine per presentare le candidature è fissato al 14 novembre.
