#2975. American soldier arrested on suspicion of spying for Russia

A senior soldier in the U.S. Army has been arrested in the U.K. on suspicion of spying for Russia, London's Metropolitan Police said on Tuesday evening.

The unnamed 41-year-old man was arrested near Gloucester, a city in western England, on Tuesday as part of an investigation by the U.K.'s Counter Terrorism Policing. British police worked with U.S. authorities, including the military and the FBI, in their investigations.

A senior NATO military official confirmed on Tuesday that the American service member was assigned to the military alliance's Allied Rapid Reaction Corps (ARRC).

ARRC, headquartered in the English county of Gloucestershire, is a  3-star NATO headquarters with more than 400 permanent staff from more than 20 nations ready to deploy land forces where they are needed.

Kremlin spokesman Dmitry Peskov told reporters on Wednesday that Moscow had "absolutely nothing to do with" the case in the U.K.

The individual at the center of the case, detained in London, is suspected of committing offenses under Sections 1 and 3 of the U.K.'s National Security Act.

Section 1 offenses relate to obtaining or disclosing protected information, while Section 3 involves assisting a foreign intelligence service.

The maximum penalty is life imprisonment for a Section 1 offense and 14 years' imprisonment for a Section 3 offense. A fine may also be imposed for either offense.

Police officers are carrying out searches at two locations in Gloucestershire, the Metropolitan Police added.

The arrest comes as Washington is seeking to broker an end to Russia's war in Ukraine. U.S.-mediated talks between Russian and Ukrainian officials began in Abu Dhabi on Tuesday, the first such meeting since February, while Kyiv and its European allies continue to press for credible security guarantees as part of any settlement.

President Donald Trump, a longstanding critic of NATO and its members' defense budgets, said on Monday that there appeared to be a growing willingness on both sides to end the almost five-year war.

Russia has long threatened retaliatory action against an expansion of NATO's eastern flank and has previously demanded guarantees that Ukraine will never be allowed to join the alliance. Finland and Sweden became NATO's newest members in 2023 and 2024, respectively.

Since Russia and Ukraine have been at war, Russian activity has also been reported deeper into European and NATO territory, with regional authorities investigating whether Moscow could be involved in some of the recent disarray seen across the continent.

FBI Director Kash Patel said in a statement on Tuesday evening that the allegations against a U.S. serviceman were "of the utmost concern to the United States and our allies."

"I want to send a clear message – this FBI has no tolerance for anyone who breaks their oath to the United States and whose actions endanger our national security and that of our partners," he said.

Sean Stinchon, the commanding general of U.S. Army Counterintelligence Command, said in a statement that "the alleged conduct of a senior solider, entrusted with sensitive information, is a sobering reminder that insider threats are real and persistent."

Authorities said Tuesday that the investigation is not related to any other ongoing Counter Terrorism Policing investigations.

On Oct. 27, British prosecutors said they had charged a man with assisting a foreign intelligence service over an incident at a military base used by the U.S. for strikes on Iran. The base in question, RAF Fairford, is in Gloucestershire, around 30 miles from ARRC's headquarters at Imjin Barracks.

#2973. Zelenskyy warns Ukraine is 'not real estate' as experts say a Russia peace deal remains elusive

Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy warned that his country "was not real estate," as a second and final day of U.S.-brokered peace talks wrapped up.

Speaking to reporters in Denmark late Tuesday, Zelenskyy said he was "happy" with what he was hearing from the peace talks in Abu Dhabi between Russian and Ukrainian representatives, but said he had been clear with U.S. envoys Steve Witkoff and Jared Kushner that "this is a country of our nation, it is not real estate."

His comments come amid discussions on the future borders of Ukraine and post-war security guarantees, issues where the countries remain far apart.

"Everybody is ready for dialog. First, stop the war in Ukraine. Second, security guarantees for Ukraine. Third, maybe some dialog about these topics of security," Zelenskyy said. "This sequence is very, very important."

It comes as the first trilateral peace talks between Russia, Ukraine and the U.S. since February concluded on Wednesday.

Speaking post-meeting, Kushner called the talks "productive" and said the parties had also discussed the difficult issue of territorial boundaries. The Ukrainian negotiator meanwhile said momentum was good and a solution could be reached before the end of the year.

Russia chief negotiator and Putin aide Vladimir Medinsky said the parties on Wednesday had reached an agreement on a large-scale prisoner exchange, and that details would be announced soon.

Another round of exchanges of prisoners of war would be welcomed, Mykola Bielieskov, senior analyst at Ukrainian NGO Come Back Alive, told CNBC Tuesday. "Otherwise there is no room for compromise," he said, adding, "I don't have any expectations of major progress towards settlement."

According to Bielieskov, Ukraine could accept a ceasefire along the current front lines, but would never formally recognize Russian sovereignty over occupied territory. He argued that Zelenskyy has neither the legal authority nor public support to surrender Ukrainian land, even if Kyiv acknowledges it cannot reclaim those territories militarily in the near term.

Russia's demands for additional territory, restrictions on NATO membership and limits on Ukraine's armed forces remain fundamental obstacles to a deal, he added.

Russia has publicly maintained its demand that Ukraine surrender the entire eastern Donbas region, including territory Moscow has not captured.

While Ukraine and European allies push for a ceasefire as an initial step, before moving on to issues such as security guarantees, borders and reconstruction, Russia has signaled it considers security guarantees necessary before ending fighting.

"It looks like because Russia has objected to a ceasefire proposal that Ukraine and the U.S. earlier last year made, the Trump administration is no longer trying to achieve a ceasefire, even though that's logically the first step," former U.S. ambassador William Courtney told CNBC early Tuesday.

Courtney said Russia has shown no signs of seriously pursuing a ceasefire in Ukraine, despite Kyiv's willingness to freeze the conflict along current battle lines.

He argued that Moscow's territorial demands and continued attacks suggest its objectives remain unchanged, making meaningful negotiations unlikely without a shift in Russia's position or battlefield conditions.

Maximilian Hess at political risk firm Enmetena Advisory told CNBC's Dan Murphy Wednesday, ahead of the second day of talks, that little has been achieved in the latest round of meetings so far.

While Zelenskyy has urged leaders to speak directly, Hess said it's unlikely the Ukrainian leader will be willing to meet with Putin without meaningful concessions from Russia, given the risk of domestic backlash.

He argued that Putin could use a potential meeting with Trump and Xi Jinping in China to seek further U.S. sanctions relief, weaken Washington's support for Ukraine, and deepen divisions within the West.

On a potential Zelenskyy-Putin meeting, Kushner said the Russian position is that "they'd like to see more progress made before that happens".

#2969. How Democrats could snarl Trump administration stakes in private sector companies

With Republicans in charge of the White House and Congress, President Donald Trump has embedded the federal government in private sector companies' affairs in unprecedented ways. That could change once Trump's Democratic adversaries take over the House and Senate following the blue wave midterm.

Democrats, planning to root out alleged corruption involving Trump, his family and their business associates, have signaled they will closely scrutinize companies that have curried the president's favor.

Their investigations may extend to Intel and the dozens of other companies that have clinched equity stakes with the government during Trump's second term. Democratic lawmakers in July asked law firm Skadden Arps for details about its involvement in the Intel deal.

The dynamic could affect how Trump approaches relationships with private sector companies, after spending two years involving the government in their affairs through stock agreements or, in the case of U.S. Steel, acquiring a "golden share" when the company was acquired by Nippon Steel.

The golden share, which allows a holder to outvote other shareholders, was negotiated as part of the June 2025 deal. The highly unusual arrangement gives Trump veto power over several key areas of the steelmaker's business, including those related to employee salaries, capital investment and even the company name.

In a unique twist, the company's amended charter specifies that after Trump leaves office, the veto power passes not to his successor, but to the Treasury and Commerce departments as representatives of the U.S. government.

U.S. Steel CEO David Burritt said Wednesday that the government's representative on its board, Commerce Department official David Shapiro, typically abstains from voting but has access to company discussions.

"He gets to hear everything we're doing," Burritt told CNBC's Megan Cassella at the U.S. Chamber of Commerce's B20 summit in Washington, adding that the arrangement has not constrained the company's operations.

"The government has not stepped in and said 'you can't do this or can't do that,' but we know what the rules are," Burritt said.

The U.S. Steel intervention turned out to be just the beginning of the U.S. government taking stakes in companies, a highly unusual move stateside. A month after acquiring the golden share, the Trump administration agreed to a $400 million stock deal with rare earths company MP Materials. The month after that, Trump announced the U.S. would take a 10% stake in chipmaker Intel.

By January, one year into Trump's second term as president, the administration had established some form of equity position in 14 companies, according to the CATO Institute's tracker. That number skyrocketed to 41 by November, including nine such announcements in May alone, the tracker shows. The holdings include both publicly traded and privately held companies, and CATO tracks deals that are closed as well as others that have been made public but are still being negotiated.

Following Republicans' decisive defeat in the Nov. 3 midterm election, the federal government has not revealed any new stock positions. But Trump, entering lame-duck status and on the verge of losing political power over Congress, could keep trying to steer his government to forge more equity deals with companies, said Indiana University associate professor Sarah Bauerle Danzman, who researches the intersection of national security and investment policy.

"He might feel he has less to lose," Bauerle Danzman, who is also a senior fellow at the Atlantic Council, told CNBC.

White House spokesman Matthew Middleton told CNBC in a statement, "Through voluntary equity investments in willing companies, the Trump Administration has partnered with the private sector to rebuild America's industrial base and bring critical production back home.""Moving forward, the President is willing to work with anyone to advance solutions that strengthen our economy and put more money back in the pockets of American workers," Middleton said.

But Trump's so-far unimpeded efforts may face sudden resistance once Democrats take majority control of the House and Senate in January.

Democratic lawmakers are openly gearing up to score wins against Trump and his associates as they vow to crack down on alleged rampant government corruption.

Those probes are expected to center on tech giants like Apple, Meta Platforms and Elon Musk's companies, whose executives have spent millions cozying up to the administration. Democrats are also poised to dig into potential conflicts related to the bevy of critical minerals deals Trump has struck. Lawmakers of both parties are also looking at Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang, the close Trump ally engulfed in scandal after admitting an extramarital affair with a suspected Chinese spy.

The administration's equity interventions could become a target in that mission, said Bauerle Danzman, predicting that Democrats might seek to boost oversight efforts even if they don't try to cancel the existing stock arrangements.

The next Congress could try to ensure the government is taking stakes that are "forwarding genuine national security priorities" and that do not provide "special favors to political and family connections," she said.

"The story there is more about kind of tying these issues to ones of accountability and anti-corruption, as opposed to necessarily cost," Bauerle Danzman said.

Asked whether U.S. Steel was preparing for potential congressional investigations after Democrats take control, Burritt said he was unaware of any investigation but would cooperate if one materialized.

"I am not aware that they're investigating, but if they are, we're happy to work with them," Burritt said. "It's not a big deal for us because we know we did the right thing."

It's not clear how strongly Republicans would try to defend Trump's golden share or his government's equity stakes should they become a Democratic target.

Conservatives traditionally espouse free markets and denounce excessive government meddling in the private sector. Republicans, including Trump, spent much of the midterm campaign warning that Democrats aim to destroy the U.S. capitalist system and impose economy-killing big government socialism.

Some Republicans have criticized the Trump administration's corporate interventions, but the moves have received little pushback from the president's party overall.

But in the wake of a crushing electoral defeat, some Republicans "might feel emboldened and actually join with the Democrats" in trying to clamp down on Trump, Bauerle Danzman said.

Burritt, despite embracing the government's involvement in U.S. Steel, said corporate leaders should retain responsibility for running their businesses.

"We need to keep accountability at the right place. We need to keep it with, with the leaders of companies and with the companies individually," he said. "We need to be capitalists."

#2962. Prediction markets tied to clinical trials and drug approvals draw lawmakers' ire

Prediction market event contracts on both clinical drug trials and Food and Drug Administration regulatory approvals are drawing attention from two Democratic senators, according to a letter obtained first by CNBC.

Sen. Angela Alsobrooks, D-Md., and Sen. Ron Wyden, D-Ore., expressed concern over the related event contracts in a Wednesday letter to Department of Health and Human Services Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr. The senators asked what measures HHS has taken to mitigate any risks that may arise from the markets.

"Allowing individuals to bet on the success of these trials and drugs undermines the scientific process, creates perverse incentives for researchers, harms trial participants, and could lead to dangerous medical outcomes for untold numbers of Americans," the senators wrote.

Alsobrooks and Wyden repeated concerns raised by some healthcare professionals that the markets could alter patient thinking when considering whether to participate in a trial and expressed concern over potential insider trading risks from researchers and regulators who may have access to nonpublic information.

The senators requested HHS provide all internal communication regarding staff participation in prediction markets tied to matters governed by the department. They also requested all communication between the department and the prediction market platforms themselves, the Commodity Futures Trading Commission — which regulates event contracts — and any internal analyses that have been done regarding the potential impact of the markets.

"HHS, FDA, and [the National Institutes of Health] must uphold the integrity of the drug development process and maintain public trust in the public health by putting essential guardrails in place to prevent individuals profiting on the success and approval of drugs that the American people depend on," Alsobrooks and Wyden said in the letter.

Both leading prediction market platforms, Kalshi and Polymarket, offer the contracts, although in Polymarket's case the markets can only be found on its international exchange, which isn't regulated in the U.S.

When Kalshi launched its related markets in July, the company said it put in place the guardrails, such as only allowing markets related to late-stage trials, in order to limit the risk of trading. Kalshi also vowed to list contracts only after a trial had completed enrollment and to use employee verification technology for all traders participating in the markets.

"Drug development is one of the most important and most information-constrained industries on earth," Kalshi CEO Tarek Mansour said in July. "The data that determines which drugs advance and which don't is largely locked away from the people who need it most. Surfacing information is what Kalshi is for, and we are committed to doing it right."

Kalshi noted that investors also buy shares in companies in fields including biotechnology and pharmaceuticals that are awaiting regulatory approvals.

"The hypothetical concerns of the Senators apply equally to the equities market, which appears to be left out of their letter.," Kalshi spokesperson Jack Such told CNBC. "The only difference is that the size — and incentive for bad activity — is significantly higher in the equities market than it is on Kalshi."

Polymarket and the Department of Health and Human Services didn't immediately respond to CNBC's requests for comment.

Disclosure: CNBC and Kalshi have a commercial relationship that includes customer acquisition and a minority investment.

#2960. Top Wall Street analysts prefer these 3 dividend stocks for stable income

Uncertainty in the Middle East, elevated Treasury yields and fears about an AI bubble continue to keep global markets on edge. Given this volatile backdrop, investors seeking stable income can consider enhancing their portfolios by adding dividend-paying stocks.

However, the vast universe of dividend stocks can make selection challenging. Tracking the ratings of top Wall Street analysts can help shortlist attractive dividend stocks, as their recommendations are backed by in-depth research.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Chord Energy is an independent exploration and production company with long-lived assets primarily in the Williston Basin. With a quarterly base dividend of $1.30 per share, CHRD offers a dividend yield of 3.67%.

In a research note on third-quarter expectations, RBC Capital analyst Scott Hanold reiterated a buy rating on CHRD stock with a price target of $175. "We model production near the top of guidance and continue to see strong performance from new wells and base production," said Hanold.

Specifically, the five-star analyst expects Q3 2026 production at 283 thousand barrels of oil equivalent per day, or Mboe/d, in line with the Street's consensus and near the high end of the 278 to 284 Mboe/d guidance. Hanold said his Q3 2026 earnings per share estimate increased to $5.29 due to lower non-cash depreciation, depletion and amortization.

Meanwhile, cash flow per share estimate moved lower to $13.59 to reflect Hanold's update based on the current backdrop and commodity mark-to-market. He expects Chord Energy to report capital spending of $375 million for Q3 2026, slightly above the Street's estimate of $369 million.

Overall, Hanold remains bullish on CHRD, given its best-in-class balance sheet. He expects a free cash flow payout of 80% in the third quarter, with the possibility of an upside. Hanold expects investors to focus on management commentary on 2027 capital plans, Williston M&A opportunities, and long-term performance of 4-mile wells.

Hanold ranks No. 310 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 14.9%. See Chord Energy KPIs on TipRanks.

Williams, an energy infrastructure company that delivers natural gas, is the next dividend pick. The company recently completed the $5.5 billion acquisition of Momentum Midstream, which will expand its natural gas infrastructure in the Haynesville to meet growing Gulf Coast LNG, power and industrial demand. At a quarterly dividend of $0.5250 per share, Williams offers a yield of 2.89%.

In her latest research note, RBC Capital analyst Elvira Scotto reiterated a buy rating on Williams stock with a price target of $87. Ahead of Q3 2026 earnings, the analyst said that she expects WMB to announce strong quarterly performance.

Scotto raised her estimates to reflect insights from the quarterly catch-up call, the completion of the Momentum Midstream acquisition, and mark-to-market quarter-to-date commodity prices. She expects WMB to report Q3 2026 adjusted earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation and amortization of $2,036 million, which is modestly above the Street's consensus estimate.

Furthermore, Scotto expects Williams' Transmission, Power & Gulf segment to increase quarter-over-quarter, driven by the startup of the Socrates power project and favorable natural gas liquids, or NGL, margins.

Scotto noted that WMB stock has underperformed due to backlash related to data centers and investor anticipation of another Power Innovation project. She expects the company to announce at least one more project before the end of this year, potentially with a new customer and a contract duration of 15 to 20 years, which would be a positive catalyst for the stock.

Scotto ranks No. 175 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. Her ratings have been profitable 67% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.2%. See Williams Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

EOG Resources, a crude oil and natural gas exploration and production company, will hold its third-quarter earnings call on Nov. 6. At a quarterly dividend of $1.02 per share, EOG offers a dividend yield of 2.75%.

Heading into Q3 earnings, Jefferies analyst Lloyd Byrne reiterated a buy rating on EOG Resources stock and increased his price target to $185 from $175. The analyst expects the company's Q3 cash flow per share to exceed the Street's consensus by 13%, driven by better-than-expected production.

Byrne expects crude oil production, excluding the UAE, to reach 551 Mboe/d, which is at the high end of guidance (546-551 Mboe/d) and above the Street's projection of 548 Mboe/d, driven by continued growth in the Utica.

Furthermore, Byrne expects cash operating expenses to come in at $10.27 per barrel of oil equivalent, below the midpoint of guidance of $10.05 to $11.35, helped by lower lease operating expenses. Overall, the analyst expects EOG to deliver free cash flow of $2.65 billion, supporting another quarter of buybacks near the minimum 70% shareholder return threshold and implying about $1.5 billion in share repurchases.

"As a first indication of the '27 plan, we expect EOG will message another year of mid-single-digit (~3.5%) growth on a flat capital budget (~$6.55bn), which is above the Street modeling 1% oil growth at $6.6bn," said Byrne.

Byrne ranks No. 303 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 58% of the time, delivering an average return of 18.5%. See EOG Resources Statistics on TipRanks.

#2956. Bouncing Wynn Shares might be primed for the 'wheel strategy'

In Barry Levinson's classic film Rain Man, Dustin Hoffman's character leans over to his brother in the middle of a blackjack hand with a simple piece of advice: bet two for good, one for bad.

In the case of Wynn Resorts shares, it might be time to bet two for good.

Steve Wynn hasn't exactly been on a winning streak. No longer running his namesake company, he finally sold his 50 Central Park South penthouse after years on the market. WYNN shares haven't really been on a winning streak either. The stock is down more than 27% year-to-date as I write this. It was down more than 46% from the 2025 highs within the past couple of weeks. But it has bounced a bit since then, and perhaps the tables are turning.

Fundamentally, the business is demonstrating exceptional strength, particularly in the United States. Wynn's Las Vegas EBITDA margin has actually surpassed that of its Macau resorts. Recognizing this, management has accelerated capital spending in Las Vegas, where the company's profit has doubled from pre-pandemic levels. Meanwhile, the Asian market is also showing signs of life. Strategic property upgrades in Macau are successfully drawing lucrative premium mass play, which is directly supporting the company's ambitious expansion plans for the Enclave at Wynn Palace.

From a valuation standpoint, WYNN is trading very close to its five-year historical averages across key metrics, including P/E, EV/EBITDA (currently around 9x to 10x), and Price-to-Book Value. Although this represents a slight premium compared to the broader gaming industry mean, Wynn's justifies a premium. (The Encore, the newer of the two adjacent Las Vegas properties, is one of my preferred hotels on "The Strip"). Technically, the stock has admittedly been in a downtrend, but this recent sharp bounce off the late October lows may indicate the run is over. Several technical indicators—specifically the MACD, Fear & Greed Index, Commodity Channel Index (CCI), and several key Moving Averages—are now aligned, suggesting it may be time to go long.

With the next earnings report not scheduled until February, the stock has a clear runway free of binary fundamental catalysts, yet implied volatility remains surprisingly high. This environment makes buying options expensive and short premium strategies more attractive.

Rather than betting on the roulette wheel, traders should look once again to "the wheel" strategy. Something I discussed earlier this week on United Rentals. The strategy? Step one, sell cash-covered puts below the current market price to collect that inflated premium. If WYNN stays above your strike price through expiration, you keep the cash and repeat the process. If the stock dips and you are assigned, you acquire shares at a discount. Once assigned, you move to step two: selling covered calls against your new stock position to generate continuous income until the shares are eventually called away, after which you revert back to step one and play again. A methodical, high-probability way to get long a world-class operator while getting paid to wait.

#2953. Federal Reserve Board requests public comment on two proposals related to establishing a regulatory framework for Board-supervised payment stablecoin issuers under the GENIUS Act

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 2:30 p.m. EDT Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Thursday requested public comment on two proposals related to establishing a regulatory framework for Board-supervised payment stablecoin issuers under the GENIUS Act.

The first proposal would require that Board-supervised payment stablecoin issuers fully back their stablecoins with certain permissible reserve assets, such as short-term Treasury bills and certain other high-quality, liquid assets. The proposal would also establish standardized capital requirements to address certain credit and operational risks of payment stablecoin activities, as well as risk management standards, in accordance with the law. Separately, the proposal would introduce rules for Board-supervised firms that safekeep the assets backing payment stablecoins. It would also clarify the permissibility of stablecoin and related activities for Board-supervised banks.

The second proposal would establish a tailored application process for Board-supervised banks applying to issue payment stablecoins. These applicants would be required to submit a business plan and financial information, among other documents. The proposal would also create a process governing appeals, hearings, and final determinations for applications.

The comment period will close 60 days after publication in the Federal Register.

#2947. CFTC investigates if Kalshi improperly self-certified event contracts

The Commodity Futures Trading Commission is investigating whether prediction market platform Kalshi failed to properly inform the agency of proposed event contracts, Kalshi confirmed to CNBC.

Kalshi called it a minor issue in how the contracts were filed and said it is working with the CFTC to address the matter. The CFTC flagged weather-related contracts, Kalshi said. The leading prediction market platform didn't say when the agency began investigating Kalshi. CFTC Chairman Michael Selig told CNBC that the agency does not comment on investigations.

Politico first reported on the probe on Tuesday, noting charges could happen soon, based on the CFTC's private notification to Kalshi. The platform declined to comment on any possible charges.

Prediction market platforms are allowed to self-certify event contracts without the federal agency's prior approval, as long as they comply with the Commodity Exchange Act. The self-certification allows for faster introduction of new contracts.

"The notice is about a procedural error involving the submission of a handful of contracts, many of which weren't live," said Elisabeth Diana, head of communications at Kalshi. "We are already addressing the issue."

The move is the latest by the CFTC as it seeks to rein in prediction markets, which face growing scrutiny in Washington from regulators and members of Congress.

In August, the CFTC ordered Kalshi to take down sports-related "mention markets" — where speculators can trade on what individuals will say in a broadcast or speech — while it looked into the event contract type.

The agency is also conducting a broad probe into competitor Polymarket, which includes reviewing if the company violated a 2022 settlement with the CFTC to prohibit U.S. traders from accessing its international trading platform. Polymarket's domestic exchange operates separately from the global platform.

Prediction markets are also expected to face heightened scrutiny once the Democratic Congress is sworn into office in January. Rep. Ro Khanna, D-Calif., in a Tuesday social media post on X, called for a ban on sports prediction markets — which account for the overwhelming majority of volume on leading platforms.

— CNBC's Talia Kaplan contributed to this report

Disclosure: CNBC and Kalshi have a commercial relationship that includes customer acquisition and a minority investment.

#2944. Top Wall Street analysts are bullish on the growth potential of these 3 stocks

Fears around the artificial intelligence trade and higher pressure on oil prices are weighing on global markets, but a few stocks offer investors solid growth prospects even in choppy times.

Investors with a long-term horizon can look beyond the ongoing volatility and track the recommendations of top Wall Street analysts to pick attractive stocks with strong long-term growth potential.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Health insurer UnitedHealth delivered better-than-expected results for the third quarter of 2026. The company also raised its full-year earnings outlook, reflecting continued progress on its turnaround plan.

Following the Q3 print, Morgan Stanley analyst Erin Wright reiterated a buy rating on UNH stock with a price target of $529. "UNH's beat & raise reinforces turnaround momentum, with improving MA [Medicare Advantage]/Optum Health supporting a higher '26 EPS baseline & upside to '27 consensus," said Wright.

The five-star analyst added that better-than-expected performance in the UnitedHealthcare and Optum Health businesses supported another upgrade to the company's 2026 adjusted earnings per share outlook to at least $20.50. Wright noted that UNH raised the earnings guidance despite incremental investments planned for the fourth quarter of 2026.

Furthermore, Wright highlighted management's view that the consensus 2027 EPS of $22.63 was too conservative. Reaching even the low end of the company's 13% to 16% long-term EPS growth target would imply an EPS of $23.17.

Wright raised her 2026 EPS estimate to $20.65 from $19.82 and 2027 EPS to $23.24 from $22.54, reflecting the latest upside and an improving 2026 medical loss ratio to 87.4% from 88.1%. Overall, she believes that UNH's operational performance continues to improve, increasing her confidence in its UHC strategy and Optum Health turnaround execution.

Wright ranks No. 264 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. Her ratings have been successful 64% of the time, delivering an average return of 15%. See UnitedHealth Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Social media giant Meta Platforms has been making headlines since the launch of its Muse AI agent, which is seeing solid traction. Investors look forward to updates on the company's AI strategy when it announces its third-quarter results on Oct. 21.

Heading into Q3 earnings, Guggenheim analyst Michael Morris maintained a buy rating on Meta stock and increased his price target to $850 from $700, reflecting higher estimates for 2027. He expects Muse usage momentum and engagement levels to shape investor sentiment into the Q3 earnings call.

Specifically, Morris highlighted that, according to Apptopia, Muse reached 1.3 million daily active users in five weeks versus 300,000 in the first week. Given that Muse's current adoption represents less than 0.05% of Meta's 3.6 billion daily active user base, the analyst expects management to prioritize boosting usage over near-term monetization.

"Over time, we believe Muse monetizes primarily through businesses paying to reach consumers through their agents, consistent with the economics of Meta's core advertising business, rather than consumer wallets," said the five-star analyst. He added that the checkout integrations with Shopify and PayPal reflect this framework.

While there are concerns that a rival could overtake Muse or the recently launched Muse Wonder and push Meta into a prolonged cycle of overinvestment, Morris remains bullish due to the company's strong record in scaling WhatsApp and competing strongly with TikTok via Reels as well as free distribution to an extensive user base.

Morris ranks No. 629 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 57% of the time, delivering an average return of 13.2%. See Meta Platforms KPIs on TipRanks.

Next is semiconductor company Marvell Technology. Earlier this month, Marvell held its Investor Day, where it raised its total addressable market estimate to $400 billion by 2030, reflecting solid AI-led demand tailwinds.

After hosting a non-deal roadshow with Marvell's senior management following the Investor Day, Stifel analyst Tore Svanberg reiterated a buy rating on the stock with a price target of $370.

The five-star analyst said that a major focus of the roadshow was the XPU and XPU Attach mix, as Marvell's Custom business expands toward its FY31 revenue target of $30 billion. In this regard, management reiterated that it expects a balanced mix of XPU and XPU Attach revenue in FY31, though it noted that either segment could grow rapidly enough to shift the mix toward 60/40 or 40/60.

While XPU drove most of the nearly $1.5 billion Custom revenue in FY26, Svanberg expects Marvell's XPU Attach business to grow substantially through the end of the decade, leading to a more balanced mix in this business. At the Investor Day event, the company raised its FY29 Custom target from more than $10 billion to over $12 billion, mainly driven by strength in the XPU Attach business.

Svanberg said that another key topic in the roadshow was whether Marvell can secure enough capacity to meet its raised targets. Management said that it met with suppliers ahead of Investor Day to share its five-year goals and received commitments to support these targets. The analyst believes that Marvell's supply chain commitments give it a competitive edge over rivals as customers seek reliable partners for large-scale infrastructure buildouts.

Svanberg ranks No. 91 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 58% of the time, delivering an average return of 31.8%. See Marvell Technology Statistics on TipRanks.

#2941. Federal Reserve Board finalizes changes to enhance the transparency and public accountability of its stress test and reduce volatility in its stress test-related capital requirements

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 9:00 a.m. EDT Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Wednesday finalized changes to enhance the transparency and public accountability of its stress test and reduce volatility in its stress test-related capital requirements. The two final rules are largely similar to the proposed rules from 2025. The Board also requested comment on a proposal to better capture differences in banks' business models to generate fee income.

The Board conducts stress tests to ensure that large banks are sufficiently capitalized and able to lend to households and businesses even in a severe recession. In December 2024, the Board announced it would modify the test to improve its resilience.

"The stress test is an essential component of our regulatory capital framework," said Vice Chair for Supervision Michelle W. Bowman. "Today's changes preserve its resilience by ensuring that it is transparent, granular, and risk-sensitive. The public will now have greater assurance that the risks banks take will be reflected appropriately in their stress test losses and their capital requirements."

The first final rule requires the Board to invite public input annually on the stress test scenarios and any material model changes. It also updates the framework that guides the design of the hypothetical scenarios and adopts the models that will be used for the 2027 stress test. In addition, the final rule makes several adjustments to the stress test calendar. The final rule also updates the global market shock component, which is a set of shocks to specific market components applied to banks with large trading books. These banks will now be tested against two global market shock components each year, and the Board will use the shock that produces the largest losses for each firm to calculate the firm's stress test results.

The second final rule requires the Board, when calculating stress capital buffer requirements, to average the results from the two most recent annual supervisory stress tests for firms subject to the stress test in both years. The Board will begin averaging stress capital buffer requirements in 2028 to ensure that only models incorporating public input are used in the calculation.

The proposal would request public comment on a revision to the Board's noninterest income model that would improve the stress test's ability to capture business model diversity across firms. If adopted, these adjustments would replace the current noninterest income model that projects each bank's fee income under stress. Comments are due 60 days after publication in the Federal Register.

Together, the changes are likely to reduce year-over-year volatility in capital requirements by approximately 50 percent and are not expected to materially affect aggregate capital requirements.

For media inquiries, please email [email protected] or call 202-452-2955.

#2936. 10-year Treasury yield jumps back above 5% as oil gains and more Fed rate hikes expected

U.S. Treasury yields jumped on Thursday, sending the 10-year note above 5% alongside a move higher in oil prices and expectations of further Federal Reserve hikes.

The yield on the 10-year U.S. Treasury note — the benchmark for mortgages, auto loans and credit card debt — rose 3.1 basis points to 5.02% after earlier hitting 5.0583%, the highest since Oct. 22.

The 2-year Treasury note yield, more sensitive to short-term Fed interest rate decisions, jumped 7 basis points to 4.797%. The longer-dated 30-year Treasury bond yield 1.4 basis points to 5.363%.

One basis point equals 0.01%, or 1/100th of 1%, and yields and prices move inversely to one another.

The rise in oil prices came after President Donald Trump said, "we don't need a deal with Iran," raising concern that the war in the Middle East will continue indefinitely. West Texas Intermediate futures gained 0.4% to $86.86 a barrel, while Brent crude was ahead about 1% to $93.60 per barrel.

Tuesday's hotter-than-expected consumer price index reading has investors anticipating more rate increases from the Fed. Goldman Sachs forecast another rate hike to its forecast on Wednesday, now expecting an additional quarter percentage point increase in March. Traders are pricing in 100% odds of an increase in December, with the chance of a half percentage point increase standing at about 13%, according to the CME Group's FedWatch tool.

Another inflation report is due Friday, when the October producer price index, which tracks wholesale prices, is expected to show a 0.4% rise in October, down from the 1.3% surge in September.

On Thursday, investors are looking for October's home sales report from the National Association of Realtors. Existing home sales rose 1.5% in September to 3.99 million, up from a two-year low in August, and are expected to have reached 4 million last month, according to Trading Economics estimates.

#2934. Ukrainian defense tech startup hits $6 billion valuation as investors bet on battle-tested drones

Ukrainian defense technology company UFORCE has raised $700 million in a funding round valuing it at $6 billion, as investor interest grows in autonomous weapons systems tested on the battlefield in Ukraine.

UFORCE, which develops unmanned aerial, maritime, and land systems as well as AI software used to coordinate them, raised $50 million at a valuation of around $1 billion in March.

The Series A round was co-led by Valor Equity Partners and the U.K.'s National Wealth Fund, with participation from investors across Silicon Valley, Europe and Asia, the company announced Thursday.

UFORCE CEO Oleg Rogynskyy told CNBC that the company's bookings have more than doubled over the past year, from a base of several hundred million dollars, but declined to disclose more exact figures.

He said the company's financial performance justified its latest valuation, adding that UFORCE had received offers from investors at even higher valuations.

"We were getting offers with much higher valuation, but we chose this one because of Valor and the rest of the investors," Rogynskyy said. “In terms of valuation to bookings, valuation to revenue, this is actually not an aggressive valuation.”

The company plans to use the new funding to scale manufacturing in Ukraine and in allied countries, including the U.S., Europe, Asia-Pacific and the Middle East.

Startups across NATO and allied countries raised $27.1 billion through September, nearly double the total for all of 2025, according to an October report from Dealroom and Resilience Media.

Rogynskyy said UFORCE had already manufactured ten times as many unmanned surface vessels as the competitors it typically measures itself against, and was looking to increase manufacturing capacity substantially. CNBC could not independently verify the comparison.

The expansion comes as militaries around the world look to learn from the war in Ukraine, where relatively inexpensive drones are challenging the role of traditional military equipment like crewed tanks or fighter jets.

UFORCE says its NEMESIS drones have completed more than 300,000 combat flights, while its MAGURA naval vessels have contributed to the destruction of more than a dozen Russian warships in the Black Sea.

Rogynskyy said the company was already working with several NATO countries, but declined to identify its customers or disclose how much of its production was being supplied to Ukraine versus overseas markets.

Asked whether growing international demand could divert supplies away from Ukraine, he said the company was committed to meeting Ukrainian military requirements first.

"We are very careful about that, and we make sure that we deliver on all Ukrainian needs first and foremost," he said.

Like many of its peers, UFORCE is seeking to challenge the economics of conventional warfare, arguing that large numbers of inexpensive autonomous systems can provide a significantly cheaper way of defense than more traditional weapons.

Rogynskyy said Ukrainian defense tech companies have an advantage over established Western manufacturers because their engineers receive continuous feedback from military personnel using their equipment on the battlefield.

"Speed of innovation, of course, is much faster with Ukrainian defense technology companies as compared to primes and Western defense techs," he said.

Looking further ahead, Rogynskyy predicted that autonomous systems would gradually replace much of the military equipment currently operated by humans, including aircraft and naval vessels.

He said he could not envision militaries continuing to purchase new crewed aircraft or frigates 10 years from now, although he acknowledged that conventional equipment such as F-35 fighter jets would remain important in the near term.

#2930. WNBA Finals MVP Gabby Williams: 'Our existence is political'

Sign up today for the CNBC Sport Newsletter

Golden State Valkyries star Gabby Williams doesn't expect the political and cultural issues swirling around the WNBA to go away anytime soon.

Fresh off the Valkyries' first championship and winning the Finals MVP award, Williams spoke with CNBC's Alex Sherman about the league's place in those conversations. The WNBA has been at the forefront of several political and cultural issues this season, ranging from race to sexuality.

"I think as a female athlete, just our existence is political," Williams said. "Being a female athlete just automatically becomes politicized because we're in a space where maybe we weren't welcome before."

Williams said that she and many of her fellow athletes understand the fact that that playing professional basketball has brought them a larger platform.

"I think it's something that we embrace as athletes," Williams said, pointing to players' past involvement in political and social causes, including the 2020 Georgia Senate runoff and the Black Lives Matter movement. "I think that'll continue," she said.

The 30-year-old basketball player, born in Nevada to an American father and a French mother, said she grew up watching the Black Lives Matter movement while a student-athlete at the University of Connecticut.

"Realizing, oh, wow, these aren't just basketball players. They're leaders in our community. I was actually looking forward to that part of the W," she said.

Williams has used her platform to speak out in support of transgender rights, a topic that became a flashpoint for the league this season after Indiana Fever player Sophie Cunningham argued that transgender women should not compete in women's sports.

Williams has maintained that sports should be open to everyone, and she's revealed that's she gotten hateful messages and death threats because of her views.

Williams said the Valkyries’ home arena, the Chase Center, which has become known as "Ballhalla" for its electric atmosphere, helped her feel welcome and gave her the confidence to thrive this season.

"Being in Ballhalla allowed me to be myself," Williams said. "As soon as I got there, I felt so welcomed. In the preseason game, I didn't even play, and people were like screaming for me and welcoming me. And it just gives me a confidence, that I think I can only get from Ballhalla."

#2919. Top Wall Street analysts prefer these 3 dividend stocks for stable income

Global stock markets remain volatile amid persistent geopolitical tensions and concerns about the sustainability of AI trade. Given this backdrop, investors seeking steady income can add dividend-paying stocks to their portfolios.

Tracking recommendations from top Wall Street analysts can help investors pick attractive stocks, as these experts assess a company's ability to pay dividends consistently.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Integrated oil and gas giant Chevron (CVX) is a dividend king. The company has increased its dividend for 39 consecutive years. At a quarterly dividend of $1.78 per share (annualized dividend of $7.12 per share), CVX offers a yield of 3.14%. Chevron will announce its third-quarter results on October 30.

In an earnings preview note on U.S. oil majors, Goldman Sachs analyst Neil Mehta reiterated a buy rating on Chevron with a price target of $241. The analyst is focused on the company's international growth prospects across Latin America, the Middle East, West Africa, and the Eastern Mediterranean.

Specifically, Mehta highlighted management's commentary on solid execution in Venezuela, with gross production expected to more than double to nearly 600 kbd (kilo barrels per day) by 2031 via three joint ventures. He also noted a recent oil and gas condensate discovery in offshore Angola and is awaiting commercial updates on the West Qurna 2 and Nasiriyah projects in Iraq.

Furthermore, Mehta expects CVX to report improved productivity and capital efficiency across domestic shale assets. He expects the improvement mainly in the Permian Basin, where Chevron projects advanced chemical treatments to drive about a 10% recovery uplift in new wells and bring down capital spend per barrel by about 25% year-over-year. Overall, Mehta expects Chevron to report Q3 2026 EPS of $4.95, above the consensus of $4.89.

"While unfavorable timing effects and volatile commodity prices may present quarterly earnings headwinds, we highlight healthy U.S. refining margins and strong asset utilization as partial offsets," said Mehta.

Mehta ranks No. 381 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 12%. See Chevron KPIs on TipRanks.

Kinetik Holdings (KNTK) is a fully integrated, pure-play, midstream company operating in the Delaware Basin. Earlier this month, the company declared a dividend of $0.81 per share, payable on October 30, 2026. With an annualized dividend of $3.24 per share, KNTK offers a yield of 5.82%. Kinetik will announce its Q3 earnings on November 4, with a conference call scheduled the next day.

Ahead of Q3 2026 earnings, Goldman Sachs analyst John Mackay reiterated a buy rating on Kinetik stock and slightly raised his price target to $58 from $57. He expects the company to report Q3 2026 EBITDA of $279 million, higher than the Street's expectation of $270 million but lower than the second quarter's EBITDA of $281 million.

The 5-star analyst expects Kinetik's Q3 performance to gain from higher gas volumes after notable curtailments in the second quarter. Mackay also expects the quarter to benefit from higher production and steady commodity margins, even though some lower-margin contracts and weaker Waha spread marketing gains could weigh on results.

Overall, Mackay increased his Q3 2026 estimates to reflect higher volumes, driven by rapid Permian egress pipeline commissioning, stronger throughput at Kings Landing I, and higher M2M commodity prices, offset by increased costs.

"As natural gas takeaway constraints ease across the Permian, investor focus centers on KNTK's YE26 volume trajectory and durability of its multi-year growth," said Mackay.

Mackay ranks No. 569 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 64% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.9%. See Kinetik's Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Moving to Crescent Energy (CRGY), an exploration and production company with activities focused in the Eagle Ford, Permian, and Uinta Basins. With a quarterly dividend of $0.12 per share (annualized dividend of $0.48 per share), CRGY's dividend yield stands at 3.56%. Earlier this month, Crescent announced an agreement to acquire Eagle Ford assets from Devon Energy (DVN).

Heading into Q3 earnings on Nov. 2, Evercore analyst Stephen Richardson reiterated a buy rating on Crescent stock and slightly lowered the price target to $17 from $18, citing dilution from new shares.

"CRGY's recent acquisition of DVN's Eagle Ford assets marks a strategic, growth-oriented move, potentially enhancing cash flow performance," said Richardson.

The 5-star analyst noted that the acquisition of the Eagle Ford assets at a net purchase price of $3.85 billion will add about 68 MBoe/d of net production and over 600 net Tier 1 locations. Richardson added that a solid track record of synergies from past deals in the Eagle Ford region increases confidence about Crescent achieving efficiencies with this deal.

Additionally, Richardson noted that the financial implications of the acquisition seem impressive, with a projected increase in cash flow per share of about 9% for 2027 to 2029.

The analyst believes that Crescent is not just expanding its business through acquisitions but positioning itself as a more durable company, regardless of market conditions.

Richardson ranks No. 478 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 13.2%. See Crescent Energy Statistics on TipRanks.

#2917. Social Security could pay full benefits until 2059 if taxable payroll kept pace with GDP: AARP report

Social Security's taxable payroll, which provides most of the revenue for the program that pays monthly benefits to millions of Americans, has fallen as a share of gross domestic product over the past four decades, according to a new report from the AARP Public Policy Institute.

Had payroll tax revenue kept up with GDP, the program could pay full benefits until 2059, according to the research — an additional 25 years beyond the latest projected depletion date of 2034 for the program's combined funds. The AARP Public Policy Institute provides policy research, analysis and development at AARP, a nonprofit, nonpartisan organization that represents Americans ages 50 and over.

"Social Security's finances have weakened not simply because America has grown older, but because the economy has changed, while the program's revenue system has not," Richard Johnson, vice president of financial security at the AARP Public Policy Institute, said during a Tuesday press briefing on the research.

"America's economy is larger and more productive than it was four decades ago, but Social Security is receiving support from a smaller share of that economy," Johnson said.

Social Security's trustees projected in June that the program's combined retirement and disability trust funds may be depleted in the third quarter of 2034. Unless Congress takes action, 83% of scheduled benefits will be payable at that time, according to the trustees' projections.

The trustees' report also included an earlier projected depletion date for Social Security's retirement trust fund — the fourth quarter of 2032, when 78% of benefits would be payable. However, the AARP said it based its analysis on the later 2034 date because Congress has shifted money between the funds in the past.

The program's current shortfall is worse than lawmakers had anticipated in 1983, when the last major Social Security reforms were enacted.

Rising income inequality means the payroll tax cap no longer covers 90% of earnings — a percentage lawmakers expected to remain constant when they made the 1983 reforms, according to research from the Roosevelt Institute, a New York-based liberal think tank. The share of earnings covered by Social Security payroll taxes fell to around 83% in 2000 and has mostly stabilized there, according to the institute.

AARP points to an aging population as another reason for Social Security's funding challenges. The worker-to-beneficiary ratio will fall from 3.4 to 2.4 between 2000 and 2030. Falling fertility rates are also shrinking the worker-to-beneficiary ratio.

Shifting income patterns have also meant that a smaller share of U.S. GDP is subject to the Social Security payroll tax. Taxable payroll has fallen from 41% of GDP in 1983 to 34% as of 2025, a level Social Security's trustees project will remain, according to the AARP.

AARP attributes the decline to a shift from wages toward business and investment income, which generally isn't subject to payroll taxes. Automation has reduced demand for workers, and pass-through businesses allow some owners to report compensation as profits rather than wages, according to the report.

Social Security would have collected $10.8 trillion in additional funding between 1984 and 2034 if taxable payroll had stayed at 41% of GDP, according to the AARP. That figure is in inflation-adjusted 2025 dollars.

In 2026, up to $184,500 in wages are subject to the Social Security payroll tax.

Payroll taxes accounted for 91% of Social Security's revenues in 2025, according to AARP's research, while 5% came from interest income and 4% was through federal income taxes paid by high-income beneficiaries on their benefits.

To increase Social Security's revenues, lawmakers may consider changes that make high-income taxpayers contribute more to the program while sparing low- to moderate-income taxpayers, according to the AARP report.

"The fix for Social Security isn't cutting earned benefits; it's rebuilding the revenue behind them," Johnson said.

Raising the payroll tax threshold — which is incrementally adjusted each year to keep up with average wages — would not affect most workers, since just 6% of individuals had earnings at or above the cap in 2024, according to the AARP.

In 2027, the Social Security taxable maximum is set to increase to $192,300.

Bills have been introduced in Congress to raise the cap to apply to higher thresholds, such as $250,000 or $400,000, or to eliminate it altogether. Getting rid of the cap would close 67% of the 75-year funding gap if workers do not receive a benefit increase in exchange for those contributions, according to AARP research based on the Social Security Administration's assessments.

Another option: Lawmakers may also shore up the program by taxing income not currently subject to Social Security payroll taxes, according to the AARP, following an increase in business activity through pass-through entities — such as sole proprietorships, partnerships, limited liability companies and S corporations.

Lawmakers could create a new tax on investment income to help fund Social Security's trust funds. A new 12.4% tax on investment income for active S corp. officers and limited partners would close about 25% of Social Security's 75-year financing gap, according to the AARP, which cited research from Social Security's actuaries.

One option not mentioned in the AARP research is raising the payroll tax rate, which is currently 6.2% for workers and employers, or 12.4% combined. The Social Security trustees estimated that an increase in the combined rate to 16.65% beginning in January 2026 would have made the combined program solvent for the 75-year period ending in 2100.

A national survey conducted in February found 62% of individuals would support gradually increasing the payroll tax from 6.2% to 6.5%, according to the University of Maryland School of Public Policy. That poll included 2,509 adults.

The 1983 Social Security reforms included changes to benefits, such as gradually raising the retirement age from 65 to 67, delaying the cost-of-living adjustment by six months and taxing Social Security benefits.

Research shows those changes have reduced lifetime retirement benefits by about 19% for people turning 65 in 2026 compared to what they would have received under the previous rules, Jenn Jones, vice president of financial security and livable communities at the AARP, said during the Tuesday press briefing.

While the AARP has not endorsed any specific benefit changes, Jones said the organization opposes cuts to monthly checks.

"Millions of Americans — we hear from them every day — cannot afford to lose a penny," Jones said.

The Federal Reserve Board on Thursday announced an enforcement action against American Express Company to address, among other things, the firm's failure to sufficiently detect and report certain suspicious activity related to money laundering.

The Board also identified significant deficiencies in how American Express Company's enterprise-wide anti-money laundering program was implemented, in particular at the firm's subsidiary national bank. The Office of the Comptroller of the Currency, the primary federal regulator of the firm's national bank, has issued a separate enforcement action against the bank.

The Board's action will help ensure that the firm operates in compliance with all U.S. laws and regulations.

#2914. Lazio, gol di testa e difesa solida: resta il nodo degli avvii di ripresa

Sei reti arrivano di testa, come per la Roma; tre dei nove gol subiti sono invece maturati subito dopo l’intervallo.

La Lazio ha segnato 25 gol e ne ha subiti 9 nelle prime 11 giornate. Secondo quanto riportato da Il Messaggero, La squadra di Gattuso ha raggiunto 29 punti, superando il precedente primato biancoceleste di 28 nel 2017/18. Al rendimento offensivo e alla tenuta difensiva si accompagna però un dato da correggere: tre delle reti incassate sono arrivate nei primi cinque minuti della ripresa.

CLICCA QUI E SEGUI LAZIOPRESS.IT ANCHE SU INSTAGRAM

I biancocelesti continuano a segnare nonostante le pressioni elevate e le assenze pesanti. Secondo Il Messaggero, per ritrovare una Lazio così incisiva nei primi 11 turni bisogna risalire al 1936/37, quando la squadra era guidata da Jozsef Viola.

Noslin e compagni trovano la rete attraverso azioni costruite, ripartenze e conclusioni dalla distanza. Sono arrivati anche sei gol di testa: tanti quanti quelli della Roma, un primato europeo secondo i dati riportati. Il totale delle reti è già a quota 25; la Lazio allenata da Sarri raggiunse quota 26 soltanto a inizio marzo.

Nei pareggi contro Milan e Napoli, i biancocelesti avrebbero potuto chiudere la partita già nel primo tempo. I dati sulle conclusioni descrivono però una squadra particolarmente efficace rispetto alle occasioni create: è decima in Serie A per tiri complessivi e nona per tiri nello specchio, mentre i 25 gol segnati sono otto in più dei 17 previsti dagli xG.

Pur senza un vero stoccatore, la Lazio beneficia degli inserimenti di Frattesi. Gattuso lo ha definito «uno dei migliori invasori d’Europa», sottolineando una qualità che contribuisce alla capacità realizzativa della squadra.

Un mese fa Gattuso ridimensionava il peso della gestione del pallone: «non mi interessa nulla, dobbiamo andare sempre in avanti». La crescita della sua Lazio passa, tuttavia, anche dal palleggio. A fine settembre la squadra era quindicesima nel campionato per possesso; dopo le percentuali registrate a ottobre è salita al sesto posto.

Quando affiora la stanchezza, i biancocelesti ricorrono alla manovra per mantenere il controllo del gioco. È successo anche dopo l’accelerazione del Napoli al rientro dall’intervallo: il tecnico ha riconosciuto la capacità dei suoi di «riequilibrare la partita» tenendo il pallone e allentando la pressione avversaria.

#2913. Federal Reserve Board releases results of the 2025 Survey of Consumer Finances, which provides the public and policymakers with detailed insights into the economic condition of American families

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 10:00 a.m. EDT Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Friday released the results of the 2025 Survey of Consumer Finances, which provides the public and policymakers with detailed insights into the economic condition of American families.

The data provide a representative picture of what Americans own, how and how much they borrow, and how they bank. The current version of the survey has been undertaken every three years since 1989 and provides the public with an extensive time series of thorough information on the economic conditions of American families. Alongside the survey data, the Board issued its summary report entitled Changes in U.S. Family Finances from 2022 to 2025 (PDF), which examines changes in income, net worth, assets, debt, and financial vulnerability.

Key findings from the 2025 survey include:

In addition to the summary report, an interactive chartbook displaying changes in these financial characteristics over time is available here. Additional data from the survey, including extensive data examining other economic conditions of American families, is made available to researchers and the public here.

The survey is conducted for the Board by NORC, a social science research organization at the University of Chicago. Participants in the study are chosen at random from 119 geographic areas, including metropolitan areas and rural counties across the United States, using scientific sampling procedures that aim to produce a sample representative of American families.

#2912. Jim Cramer says investors need to pay attention to CoreWeave. Here's why

CNBC's Jim Cramer said Wednesday that he's becoming less skeptical of CoreWeave after the neocloud's strong earnings and the CEO's "refreshing and reassuring" comments.

CoreWeave shares surged nearly 25% on Wednesday after the company reported better-than-expected quarterly revenue and raised its 2026 guidance. The results, which were out Tuesday evening, helped ease concerns about a slowdown in AI infrastructure spending. CoreWeave CEO Michael Intrator's subsequent interview with Cramer on CNBC just after Wall Street's opening bell offered further reasons for optimism.

One of the most encouraging takeaways from Intrator was disclosure that demand for CoreWeave's computing capacity is 10 times greater than what the company can supply. It was also discussed on the post-earnings conference call. That dynamic has allowed CoreWeave to consistently increase prices, supporting stronger gross margins even as it spends heavily to expand its infrastructure.

Intrator also addressed concerns about CoreWeave's substantial debt load, which has weighed heavily on its stock. Despite Wednesday's surge, shares are still down 15% year to date and down 55% from its 52-week high of $138 on May 6. The past month has been particularly tumultuous for the neoclouds, which also include Iren and Nebius. Iren reported strong earnings last week. Nebius reports its quarter Thursday morning.

Rather than relying solely on traditional bond markets, CoreWeave has increasingly turned to financing backed by its equipment and customer contracts. "When I pressed him, asking why he doesn't just go into the open market and buy these bonds that are trading so poorly, he admitted that he's been tempted to, but ultimately he still believes he can make an even better return building data centers," Cramer said. "Take that red flag off the table."

Another surprising revelation involved the value of CoreWeave's Nvidia chips. Intrator said some H100 chips purchased four years ago are now being contracted at premiums of 20% to 50% above its original cost, suggesting the equipment is retaining its economic value longer than many investors anticipated.

The Intrator interview also helped ease concerns about growing political opposition to data center construction. The CEO maintained that demand for computing power would continue to drive expansion, even if stricter regulations pushed some projects overseas.

For Cramer, the comments offered a welcome counterpoint to the recent pessimism surrounding AI infrastructure stocks. While he has grown more cautious about the sector amid political and financing risks, the "Mad Money" host maintained that abandoning it altogether could mean missing substantial upside. "We've taken some data center and related stocks off the table, unfortunately not Nvidia, [for the CNBC Investing Club] because our composure and our prudence dictates that to have too much data center is still too dicey. Still, we've left plenty of exposure to the group, which includes semis, software, networking, builders, turbines and cooling equipment, because we think the AI theme has much more room to run."

The Club does not own any neoclouds. But it does have diversified, albeit recently reduced, exposure to the AI boom via chip stocks and names that help run and power data centers.

#2908. The world's super-rich are piling into alternative investments. Here's why

The world's wealthiest investors are increasingly looking beyond stocks and bonds, pouring money into hedge funds, private equity and infrastructure assets, as market volatility and fears about stubborn inflation spur diversification.

From Asian billionaires to global family offices, alternative investments are becoming a bigger part of portfolios once dominated by traditional assets, said industry watchers

JPMorgan Asset Management's private wealth alternative assets in Asia-Pacific surged 40% over the past 12 months, according to Dan Watkins, the firm's regional chief executive officer, underscoring the growing appetite among high-net-worth and ultra-high-net-worth clients.

"So you really have this huge appetite for alternative exposure happening in that ultra-high-net-worth, high-net-worth category," Watkins said recently at a media summit in Singapore.

The shift comes as investors grapple with increasingly concentrated equity markets, geopolitical uncertainty and concerns that stocks and bonds may not provide the same protection against market downturns as they once did, experts said.

"We are seeing a sharp increase in planned asset allocation to alternatives among the family offices we speak to," said John Woods, chief investment officer for Asia at Lombard Odier.

Alternative investments now account for 60% of single-family office portfolios, surpassing traditional assets for the first time, according to BNY Wealth. Traditional assets accounted for 41% of allocations, the report found.

Wealthy investors are seeking diversification, income and access to investments outside listed markets, and concerns over elevated valuations, tight credit spreads and the growing correlation between stocks and bonds have accelerated the trend, Woods added.

Hedge funds are emerging as a favored option among wealthy investors seeking protection against market swings, particularly as traditional diversification strategies face growing challenges.

Olaolu Aganga, head of portfolio construction and analytics at Citi Wealth, said hedge funds have attracted the strongest recent interest among qualified clients, who are seeking returns less dependent on the direction of equity markets.

Private credit is another area drawing interest, offering investors contractual income streams, while some hedge fund strategies seek to profit from differences in performance between companies rather than relying on broad market rallies, said Woods.

Citi's 2026 survey of 351 family offices found that inflation was their biggest concern, followed by interest rates, financial system stability and market volatility.

Wealthy families are increasingly seeking investments that can generate income and help preserve purchasing power amid persistent inflation and interest-rate uncertainty, while diversifying portfolios against market volatility, according to Aganga.

The Citi survey, however, also showed public equities recorded the strongest net increase in allocations, suggesting wealthy investors are adding alternatives rather than abandoning traditional markets. "This is not a wholesale rotation away from public markets," Woods said.

Infrastructure has emerged as another major beneficiary of the shift, with the artificial intelligence boom creating enormous demand for data centers, electricity networks and digital connectivity.

Thomas Roland Guyot, head of private markets investment specialists for Southeast Asia at UBS Unified Global Alternatives, said investors are increasingly targeting assets that support AI, electrification and energy security.

Such investments potentially offer a combination of long-term growth and relatively predictable cash flows, particularly where revenues are supported by essential demand or long-term contracts.

Investor appetite is already showing up in fund flows. Net inflows into open-ended infrastructure funds rose 30% year on year in the 12 months through the first quarter of 2026, according to PitchBook data cited by UBS.

Private equity remains a cornerstone of wealthy investors' alternative portfolios, although they are becoming more selective. Family offices are increasingly favoring direct investments in companies, co-investments alongside private equity managers and purchases of existing fund stakes on the secondary market, rather than relying solely on traditional pooled funds, according to Citi Wealth's Aganga.

Such approaches can give investors greater control over their investments or opportunities to acquire assets at discounts, said Lombard Odier's Woods.

The broader shift is also being facilitated by the growth of evergreen and semi-liquid funds, which have lowered minimum investment thresholds and reduced some administrative hurdles associated with traditional private-market funds, Woods added.

Investing into alternative assets does not come without risks, even as mitigating those is driving flows.

Private investments can lock up capital for years, while valuations may be slow to reflect deteriorating market conditions. Even funds marketed as semi-liquid can restrict withdrawals when redemption requests surge.

"Improved access does not equal liquidity," JPMorgan's Watkins said.

Citi's Aganga also warned that performance can vary significantly between managers, while private assets may appear less volatile than they actually are because valuations are updated less frequently.

Investors must also guard against unintended concentration. A portfolio holding data centers through real estate, infrastructure and private equity funds may appear diversified while remaining heavily exposed to the same AI investment cycle, UBS' Guyot said.

For now, the flow of capital into alternative assets shows their increasing appeal among the rich, though not at the expense of traditional investment, with Woods pointing out that much of the new investment is being funded by fresh capital and excess cash rather than large-scale sales of stocks and bonds.

#2907. Meta head of infrastructure will leave and be succeeded by former AWS cloud exec

Meta's long-time infrastructure leader will leave the company in the new year, setting the stage for a recently hired former Amazon Web Services executive to oversee its massive data center technical operations.

Santosh Janardhan, Meta's head of infrastructure and co-head of engineering, revealed his upcoming departure on Wednesday in an internal memo obtained by CNBC. Janardhan, who has worked at Meta since 2009, said "it's time to pass the baton" to former AWS senior vice president David Brown, who joined Meta in July from AWS.

The executive move underscores Meta CEO Mark Zuckerberg's efforts to expand the company's data center operations under newer leaders at a time of increasing investor scrutiny on capital expenditure spending by the social media giant and its hyperscaler rivals.

Brown will now formally lead Meta's infrastructure efforts as part of the Meta Compute initiative, which is spearheaded by the company's president and vice chairman Dina Powell McCormick and technical leader Daniel Gross. Zuckerberg is relying on these new executives to continue developing cutting-edge data centers and related infrastructure needed to support its AI ambitions.

The company said during its recent third-quarter earnings report that it expects full-year capex to fall in the range of $135 billion to $145 billion. Meta also logged negative free cash flow of $2.12 billion, which S&P Global Ratings noted as part of its decision in late October to revise Meta's credit rating from AA- stable to AA- negative.

Janardhan said in the memo that he will help support Brown through the first quarter of 2027 as part of "the transition."

"As we make the next set of choices about our priorities, this is the right time for Dave to take the lead in shaping that work," Janardhan said in the memo. "Dave has spent his career building and operating dependable infrastructure for a wide range of demanding workloads. He has the instincts, depth and judgment this moment requires."

Bloomberg News first reported on the executive transition.
